**WORK AFTER AI · The annual assessment of how artificial intelligence is transforming work · 2026 edition**

# The Great Misconception About the AI Job Killer

<a id="WAAI-2026-C01"></a>
## Foreword

This report grew out of a contradiction. In the headlines, artificial intelligence is eating jobs. In the official statistics, you look for the job killer in vain. What you find there instead is something else, and it is the same in almost every data series: a technology that is being tried out widely and is only rarely really productive in use, an entry level that is changing without anyone being able to name the cause precisely, and an organization that does not yet know how it is supposed to work with a colleague who is not a person.

The title of this first edition is a claim, and the report is its test. It takes the job killer headline apart into three processes that have different causes and run at different speeds: the AI argument, the career gap and the long rebuild. It tests each of them against the best available evidence, against official statistics, against peer reviewed studies, against the vendors' own usage data and against documented cases from companies. Where the findings contradict each other, the text says so. Where a piece of evidence comes from someone with an interest of their own in the result, that is noted alongside it. Where a number could not be verified, it does not appear.

In 2002, the economist Carlota Perez described in "Technological Revolutions and Financial Capital" how every great technological revolution since the industrial revolution falls into two halves. In the first, the installation phase, financial capital drives the new technology forward with exuberance and bubbles; in the second, the deployment phase, the gains only arrive across the board once institutions, ways of working and skills have adapted to the technology.[1] Lay this pattern over the year 2026 and almost all of it is recognizable: the investment wave, the valuations, the loud announcements, and behind them an economy that has not yet taken the technology into its work at all. The job killer belongs to the first half of this story. The questions this report is about belong to the second. How quickly does an organization build the ability to work with the technology? And what happens to those that do not?

From now on, Work After AI will appear every year in the autumn, in the same structure, with the same core questions and with a prediction chapter that I grade publicly in the following year, my own predictions as well as those of others. The real value is not meant to lie in the single edition but in the series, which over the years makes visible what has actually changed and what was only announced. Part of that is disclosing my own interests, because I earn my money with enterprise software that uses artificial intelligence. The report is therefore explicitly not tied to any vendor, any model and any consulting firm, and it recommends nothing that could be bought. It orders the discussion. What consequences to draw from it is for each company to decide for itself, and that is exactly the point.

Gerhard Kürner, Wels, September 2026

---

<a id="WAAI-2026-C02"></a>
## The Eleven Key Findings of 2026

<a id="WAAI-2026-K01"></a>
**1. The job killer is a capital markets story.** Only about 5 percent of American companies that use artificial intelligence report any effect at all on their headcount, and the technology replaces software a good three times as often as it replaces people. The job cuts that are justified with it follow the logic of the stock market, not of the companies.[2]

<a id="WAAI-2026-K02"></a>
**2. Expectation outruns measurement.** In 2025, 32 percent of companies expected their workforce to shrink through AI in the following year, 14 percent actually experienced it a year later, and 39 percent nevertheless now expect it for next year. The same companies missed their prediction and then expect more of it.[3]

<a id="WAAI-2026-K03"></a>
**3. The career gap arises in hiring, not in layoffs.** Employment among 22 to 25 year olds in the most exposed occupations in the United States is 19 percent below the level of their peers in less exposed occupations, and the entire gap is explained by declining hiring. Whether the machine, interest rates or remote work is the cause is open; in Europe the drop is so far measurable only in Switzerland, which can mean protection or delay.[4] At the vendor Anthropic itself, the share of entry-level hires among new hires fell from 27 to 9 percent in 2026 while the workforce tripled: the quiet force becomes visible.[182] For Germany, the Institute for Employment Research tests this attribution for the first time against administrative data and does not find it confirmed: the relationship among 20 to 29 year olds is negative and growing, but it is not statistically established, while the business cycle acts more than twice as strongly.[195]

<a id="WAAI-2026-K04"></a>
**4. The entry level is shifting from execution to oversight.** When the machine takes over the routine work that used to be the learning task of the entry level, the entry level has to be rebuilt. Dual vocational training, which has never separated learning from working, is the underrated lever for this in the German speaking countries.[5]

<a id="WAAI-2026-K05"></a>
**5. Women carry the higher risk and have the lesser access.** In Germany, men use generative AI 20 percent more often than women in private life and 29 percent more often at work. Among 16 to 24 year olds, women are ahead. The gender gap arises in the workplace, and it closes where the employer pays for the training.[6]

<a id="WAAI-2026-K06"></a>
**6. What the individual gains does not arrive in the organization.** 80 percent of respondents report productivity gains of their own. 37 percent see any value at all in the operating result, but only about 6 percent a measurable contribution to operating profit of at least 5 percent. Of those 6 percent, just under three quarters rebuilt their workflows from the ground up. The long rebuild does not fail because of the machine, it fails because of the organization.[3]

<a id="WAAI-2026-K07"></a>
**7. In 2026 the human becomes the bottleneck.** Where agents run in production, they wait for the people who direct them. At the vendor Anthropic, by its own account, four of every five minutes of a session with an agent are spent waiting for a human input, and whoever wanted to read everything their own agents produce would need half a working week to do so. 69 percent of executives spend more time monitoring unchecked output, and in the only company measured over two years the load per reviewer has doubled. Organizations answer with tooling: agents review agents.[71][152][182]

<a id="WAAI-2026-K08"></a>
**8. Delegating is a leadership decision, and whoever stops checking loses the skill.** The willingness to hand work over to AI follows only to one third from the assessment of the technology. At the same time, a company loses capabilities it has long possessed as soon as the machine's output is adopted unchecked; in a field experiment, consultants working with AI outside the machine's performance frontier were wrong 19 percentage points more often than without it.[7][8][9]

<a id="WAAI-2026-K09"></a>
**9. The organization of the future orders its work by topic instead of by function, and in doing so it becomes smaller.** 43.5 percent of work related AI use concerns tasks that until now belonged to a different occupation; small teams take on adjacent specialist and process topics as well, and the specialist department becomes a review body. The HR function itself is caught in the pilot trap: 28 percent of its processes run productively with AI, its own expectation for the share of automatable tasks has fallen within a year from over 30 to 20 percent, and employees report 3.4 training days a year while the HR departments estimate 6.2.[10][11][12]

<a id="WAAI-2026-K10"></a>
**10. Marketing is the first function in which the rebuild becomes visible, from the inside as well as from the outside, and its teams keep growing despite AI.** In Germany, companies use artificial intelligence more often only in customer service than in marketing and communications: 54 percent of the users do so (Bitkom). Only 11 percent of organizations report having replaced positions with AI (Content Marketing Institute), and marketing teams grew by 2.5 percent over the past year (The CMO Survey, Duke University). From the inside, sales and management handle online marketing and brand management with AI themselves, while marketing takes on no adjacent task. From the outside, retail advertising overtakes television in 2026 (WARC), and Meta wants to leave creation, targeting and measurement entirely to its own system. 15.3 percent of marketing budgets are earmarked for AI, but only 30 percent of marketing chiefs consider their organization ready to use AI at scale (Gartner). What comes under pressure is the agencies' business model of selling working time, not marketing itself.[14][16][168][169][170]

<a id="WAAI-2026-K11"></a>
**11. In Europe, demographic pressure reverses the opening question, and the future of work is decided by the company's ability to work with the technology.** By 2040 Germany will lose around 13.3 million people in the labor force to retirement, that is 30 percent, and the younger cohorts do not make up for it. In the German speaking countries, artificial intelligence is the most likely instrument for holding on to prosperity, provided companies take it into their work before the experienced ones retire with their knowledge.[17]

---

<a id="WAAI-2026-C03"></a>
## What a trend is and what it is not, and what this report does not know

This report distinguishes three things that constantly run into one another in the discussion about artificial intelligence and work: what is measured, what is assessed and what nobody can know. Measured are the three forces around which the main part turns. The AI argument can be shown in layoff statistics, company surveys and capital market communication, the career gap in payroll and register data, the long rebuild in surveys, official statistics and by now in the usage data of the model vendors themselves. These three forces are trends in the strict sense, because they have a direction that appears in several independent sources and over more than one year, and because it can be checked in the coming year whether they hold. Assessed are the predictions and the outlooks at the end of the chapters; they state the author's view, are derived from the documented findings and are marked in the text as assessments. Nobody can know how fast the technology itself gets better. This report makes no statement about when a model reaches which capability. It cites the scenarios of others as benchmarks and grades them, but it does not place a scenario of its own beside them. Its central thesis does not depend on that abstention, because it holds that the rebuild of work is for now not failing because of the machine but because of the organization, and that applies to every speed of technical progress that is plausible in 2026.

What this report does not know belongs on this page as well. It does not know whether it means protection or delay that no effect has so far shown up at the entry level in Europe, because the spread of the technology here lags the American one by two to three years, and the measurements that could settle this question will not exist before 2027. It does not know whether the gap between pilot and production is a transitional phenomenon that closes as organizations learn, or whether, as the Notion survey suggests, it opens further with growing maturity. It does not know how the effect looks in firms with fewer than ten employees, because there is no reliable survey for that in Europe, and it knows next to nothing about wages, training and codetermination in small firms. It does not know what share of the American layoffs the technology actually accounts for, because the statistics count the reason the company states, not the one that applies. And it does not know whether the usage data of the model vendors, which it treats in several places as the sharpest measurement so far, will still count as such in five years, because none of these data series can be recalculated from outside. These gaps are not weaknesses of the report that could have been avoided, but the state of knowledge in September 2026, and they are the reason the report appears annually.

<a id="WAAI-2026-C04"></a>
## Method and disclosure

The report places other people's evidence in context and makes assessments of its own, and it keeps the two visibly apart. The evidence base of the founding edition consists of the sources listed in the sources section, among them official statistics from Germany, Austria, Switzerland, France, the United States and the European Union, peer reviewed studies, working papers from central banks and research institutes, the usage data of the three large model vendors, surveys by consulting firms and software vendors, and documented company cases. The same procedure applies to every source. It is read in the original, and every number used in the text is checked against the primary reference and, where a second independent source exists, cross checked against that one. Divergences between the two are stated in the text or in the sources section and are not smoothed over, and numbers that failed this check do not appear in the text, even when they are circulating in the press. For every source there is an internal dossier with its verification status, its limitations and a list of blocked values, and that dossier is the basis for future editions. Commercial senders and senders with an interest, that is consultancies that sell the rebuild they describe, vendors whose business depends on the spread of the technology, associations and companies speaking for themselves, are marked as such in every place; their numbers are carried as self reported statements, never as measurements. Job postings are never read as an employment effect, and an occupation's exposure to the technology is never equated with its replacement, because those two confusions are the most common errors in the public debate.

The report relies exclusively on sources that every reader can check for themselves. Up to version 1.1, assessments from the author's working conversations with owners and management were also included. They were dropped with version 1.2, because these conversations were neither conducted according to a sampling plan nor recorded and therefore cannot be verified from the outside. Since this version, the report makes assessments of its own in only two marked places, in the predictions and in the "Looking ahead" section at the end of the chapters, and both are derived from the documented findings. From the 2027 edition on, the DACH board pulse is added, a fixed catalog of core questions asked in the same form every year; the questionnaire, the number of participants and the field period are published with every edition. Added to it then is the report's lead metric, the productive rate, that is the share of companies in which at least one AI system handles a core task in regular operation, outside of pilot, test and one off initiative. The founding edition does without both, because a measurement that exists only once is not a time series.

Artificial intelligence worked on this report, and that is stated here. Research, source checking, drafts, editing and the management of the source inventory ran on the platform of 506.ai, in a working environment in which every check is documented; for the agentic checking of sources, that is the independent locating of the primary reference, the comparison of every number against a second source and the keeping of the blocklists, dedicated skills were developed that run this procedure in the same form for every source. The theses, the selection of sources, the predictions, every decision to include a finding and every sign off come from the author, and every text has been read back by him. The report does not carry this way of working as a footnote but as part of its subject, because it describes a division of labor between human and machine that it practices itself, with the same limits it describes in the chapters. The machine invented numbers several times where a figure contained none, it attributed sources wrongly and confused versions, and each of these errors was found only because a human compared the statement against the primary source.

The report is self funded, and its results are not for sale. It is paid for by no vendor, no consultancy, no association and no public body, it carries no advertising, and none of the organizations cited saw the text before publication. At the same time, the author discloses his interests. Gerhard Kürner is CEO of 506.ai, the European platform for service as a software and agentic engineering, and holds further roles in the field of enterprise software. None of these companies is a source, a piece of evidence or a case example in the report, the report is tied to no model and no vendor and recommends no product. The publisher is Kuerner Group GmbH, Perlenweg 20, 4600 Wels, Austria; the author is Gerhard Kürner. The present edition is registered under the DOI 10.5281/zenodo.22868155, the author under the ORCID 0009-0003-0611-0526. The report is cited as: Kürner, Gerhard (2026): Work After AI 2026. The Great Misconception About the AI Job Killer. Work After AI, 2026 edition, version 1.3. Wels: Kuerner Group GmbH. DOI 10.5281/zenodo.22868155. The report is published under the Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International license (CC BY 4.0), which permits redistribution, quotation and adaptation with attribution of the author, and every edition remains reachable at a permanent address. The present version carries the version number 1.3 and was published on November 6, 2026; the first publication, version 1.0, appeared on September 16, 2026, version 1.1 on September 18, 2026, and version 1.2 on September 24, 2026. The 2026 edition will be updated through the end of the year when new data sharpen the findings or errors need correcting. Every version carries its own version number with a date. The current version is at workafterai.org, and what has changed between versions is set out in the version history at the end of the report. From version 1.2 on, every version also appears as a machine edition, that is as a text edition with a fixed identifier for every chapter, key finding, prediction, external benchmark and source, as data files and as a skill package for assistants; the files sit next to the PDF under the work DOI and are described at workafterai.org.

The report works on three levels that move at different speeds. The edition is the annual level. It appears in the fall, and it is the only place where the previous year's predictions are graded and the measurement series are extended by one year. The version is the quarterly level. Within an edition, up to three further versions follow at intervals of roughly one quarter, each with a date, its own version number and its own permanent identifier (DOI), so that every citation refers to a fixed state. A version appends new sources and sharpens key findings; the predictions are neither changed nor withdrawn within an edition, because a prediction that is adjusted along the way can no longer be graded at the end of the year. A version is published only when new data change a finding or an error needs correcting; a version history without entries does not justify a version. The third level is the analyses on workafterai.org. They appear between versions whenever a development calls for it rather than on a calendar, and each analysis names the key finding it touches and says whether it confirms it, shifts it or leaves it open; it is incorporated with the next version. This rhythm follows the rhythm of the sources, because the official statistics and the large surveys the report relies on appear annually or quarterly, and a report cannot have new findings more often than its sources. For the founding edition 2026, published in September, an exception applies: version 1.3 is planned for early November after the fall studies, and the final version 1.4 for December 31, 2026.

Since version 1.3, this report uses a further, specially marked text form, the field report. It reproduces how named pioneers work with the technology, states their role, the date and their business interests, and does not count as evidence for a key finding. Such an observation only counts as a pattern once a second, independent voice or a measurement is added. Version 1.3 appears under the name Q4 Edition, the final version 1.4 on December 31, 2026 under the name Year-End Edition. From 2027, the versions within an edition carry the name of the quarter in which they appear, and the last version of a year is called the Year-End Edition.

The report develops further, and it states every change to its method. The structure around the three forces named above, the chapter on the organization of the future, the prediction chapter with its block of external benchmarks and the rules of source work stay the same from edition to edition, so that a longitudinal view emerges. From 2027 on, the board pulse, the productive rate, the impact matrix, which sorts the effect of the five changes to work, that is the AI argument, the career gap, the long rebuild, the new structure of work and demographic pressure, across 14 industries by time horizon, and the public grading of the previous year's predictions, our own as well as those of others, are added. What is an assessment in this edition is meant to be a measurement then, and where a definition changes, that is recorded in the method section of the edition concerned, so that the years can be compared.

<a id="WAAI-2026-C05"></a>
## Three forces in the labor market, one wrong headline, and the question that really counts

---

<a id="WAAI-2026-C05.1"></a>
### The headline that tries to explain too much at once

Hardly any story has taken hold as effortlessly over the past year as the one about artificial intelligence pushing people out of work. It has the advantage that it appears to confirm itself the moment you open the news. One large technology company announces thousands of job cuts on a single day, another eliminates entire departments, and in almost every one of these statements the word appears that promises to give the whole thing its meaning. The technology takes over, the human steps aside, and the layoff statistics deliver the evidence along with it.

The story is catchy, and in this form it is wrong. Not because artificial intelligence leaves no traces in the labor market, but because the headline fuses three completely different processes, which in truth rest on different causes and run on different time scales, into a single story. Anyone who takes it apart sees a picture that is less dramatic but far more consequential than the one in the headline. And they see where the question that actually matters lies, the one that is almost entirely lost in the excitement about the machine.

> "The headline about AI the job killer fuses three processes, which in truth rest on different causes and run on different time scales, into a single story."

**[Figure 1: Three processes with their own pace, packed into one headline.]**

<a id="WAAI-2026-C05.2"></a>
### The first force is the AI argument, a wave of layoffs that calls itself artificial intelligence and goes by another name

The most visible of the three forces is the wave of job cuts, and it is at the same time the one that has the least to do with the technology in whose name it happens. A survey by the consulting firm Gartner (gartner.com) among 350 large companies, all of which already work with artificial intelligence, did find that four out of five of these companies have cut staff. But it also showed that there is no connection between the depth of the cuts and the economic success of the investments. Those who cut the most did not end up better off, and in some cases worse. That is a remarkable finding, because it contradicts the internal logic of the headline. If the job cuts were really the result of a more productive machine, they would have to show up in the numbers, and that is exactly what they do not do. [18]

What shows up instead is an older and more sober pattern. A substantial part of what is presented today as technological modernization is the correction of overstaffing that built up in the years of cheap money. Investors from Silicon Valley, who are hardly suspected of underestimating the importance of the technology, say openly that nearly every large company is overstaffed, many by a quarter, some by half and more. [19] Artificial intelligence supplies the better argument for these cuts, because a reduction presented as modernization lands differently in the capital markets than a plain cost saving decision. This first force is therefore real in its consequences for the people affected, but it is wrongly named in its cause. This report therefore gives it the name that describes its mechanics, the AI argument.

<a id="WAAI-2026-C05.3"></a>
### The second force, the career gap, hits those starting out, and its cause is disputed in the research

The second force is subtler and more serious. It shows up not in the headlines about individual corporations, but in the data on young people entering working life. A widely noted study by the Stanford Digital Economy Lab showed, on the basis of actual payroll data, that employment among 22 to 25 year olds in those occupations that are particularly exposed to artificial intelligence has declined markedly in comparison, while older and more experienced employees in the same occupations remained stable or even gained. [4] Here, then, the technology appears to be doing exactly what the headline claims.

But at this point the matter becomes harder rather than simpler, because the research is anything but united about the cause. A study from the Federal Reserve by Jessica Liu and Douglas Webber covering more than a million companies finds no measurable effect,[20] and an analysis by the payments provider Ramp (ramp.com) together with the labor market data provider Revelio Labs (reveliolabs.com) does confirm the collapse at the entry level, but shows that it is precisely not driven by those firms that are actually adopting artificial intelligence.[21] A Stanford study of companies in 41 countries, by contrast, concludes that the entry point narrows precisely at the adopting companies, though through growth among the experienced and not through cuts among the young.[143] The collapse is therefore demonstrable, but attributing it to the technology is contested, and a substantial part of it is likely to go back to the sharpest turn in interest rates in four decades, which made every new hire at the entry level more expensive overnight. Anyone who wants to treat this second force honestly has to endure the dispute instead of resolving it in favor of the simple story. Because its finding appears in the same place regardless of how that dispute ends, namely at the beginning of the working life, this report carries it under the name of the career gap.

<a id="WAAI-2026-C05.4"></a>
### The third force, the long rebuild, is the actual effect of the technology, and it is slower than its reputation

The third force is the real change that artificial intelligence sets off inside companies, and it has the least in common with the pace of the headlines. It does not run through the spectacular replacement of entire workforces, but through the slow and laborious reworking of processes, through the question of which task a digital team member can take over and which it cannot, and through the far harder question of how an organization builds the necessary knowledge in the first place. This force is the most consequential of the three, but it is invisible, because it cannot be condensed into a press release. It happens in the engine rooms of companies, not on their front pages. This report calls it the long rebuild, because what decides success is not the new machine but the patient reorganization of work around the machine.

<a id="WAAI-2026-C05.5"></a>
### Why the three forces are constantly confused

The fusing of the three into a single story is not ill will but an understandable confusion, because all three are happening at the same time. The AI argument, the career gap and the long rebuild fall into the same window of time, and the human need to link simultaneous events causally does the rest. Artificial intelligence is at the center of attention anyway, and so it becomes the obvious explanation for everything that is moving in the labor market at once. The analytical work does not consist in denying one of these three forces, but in separating them cleanly from one another and showing the real mechanics of each.

This separation is the subject of the present study. It rests on the publicly available quantitative research, which it reports honestly in its contradictions instead of picking out the convenient findings. It places an emphasis on the European labor market, which is a blind spot in the largely American discussion, even though its structures differ considerably. And it uses only numbers that have been checked against their primary source and, where one exists, against a second independent source. Only with this separation and this checking does it become possible to see behind the headline.

<a id="WAAI-2026-C05.6"></a>
### What this is really about in the end

Once the three forces have been separated, the question that really counts shifts to an unexpected place. It is then no longer whether artificial intelligence destroys jobs, because that question, as will become clear, is posed wrongly. It is how fast an organization builds the knowledge that makes the technology productive in the first place, and whether, in the haste of cutting jobs, it is destroying precisely the capability that will later decide its success. This question stands at the end of the study, not at its beginning. The path there leads through the three forces, and it begins with the one that is the loudest and delivers the least of what its name promises.

---

<a id="WAAI-2026-C06"></a>
## The AI argument, the loudest of the three forces, says more about the capital markets than about the technology

The wave of job cuts is the most visible of the three phenomena, and it supplies the seemingly most crushing evidence for the thesis of the machine as job eater. When a corporation cuts thousands of jobs in a single day and speaks of artificial intelligence in the same breath, the connection seems obvious. On closer inspection, however, it dissolves, and a more sober explanation takes its place, one that has more to do with the peculiarities of the capital markets than with the capability of the technology.

The sharpest evidence for this comes from a study by the consulting firm Gartner from the spring of 2026, which covered 350 large companies each with more than a billion dollars in revenue, every one of which was already working with artificial intelligence. Four out of five of these companies had cut staff. The decisive finding, however, does not lie in that rate but in what shows up between the depth of the cuts and their economic success, namely nothing. There is no correlation between the extent of the cuts and the success of the AI investments, and those who cut the most were not better off in the end, in some cases worse. Helen Poitevin, who is responsible for this research at Gartner, sums it up in the sentence that many chief executives reach for layoffs in order to show quick AI wins, but that this attitude is misguided, because job cuts create budget room and not success. Part of the context is that Gartner has not made this survey available as a standalone publication; what is public is only the analyst's commentary and the figures quoted from it, while the methodology and the raw data are not disclosed, and the finding is therefore to be read as a consulting firm's survey whose results a reader cannot recalculate. [18]

That this pattern is now also spoken aloud where the labor market data originate is shown by a remark by Virginia Sondergeld, economist at the Hiring Lab of the job board Indeed (indeed.com), in Die Zeit in September 2026: Companies, she said, prefer to justify job cuts to investors and the public with artificial intelligence rather than with the weak economy. The sentence appeared in an article about the collapse of entry-level positions in Germany whose headline adopted exactly this justification, while the data beneath it date the beginning of the decline to the spring of 2022, half a year before ChatGPT. [141]

**[Figure 2: So far, companies are using AI to replace software, hardly any staff.]**

This finding contradicts the internal logic of the headline. If the job cuts really were the result of a more productive machine, they would have to show up in better numbers. That they do not points to a different mechanism, and this mechanism is neither new nor mysterious. A large part of what today goes under the name of technological modernization is the belated correction of overstaffing from the years of cheap money. Venture capitalists, whom nobody suspects of playing down the importance of the technology, name the true causes without varnish. Marc Andreessen, for example, considers practically every large company overstaffed, many by a quarter, most by half, some by three quarters, and traces this back to the zero interest rate period and the frantic build-up of workforces during the pandemic. Even Sam Altman, whose business rests on the spread of AI, concedes that there is a certain amount of cheating going on and that firms attribute layoffs to artificial intelligence that they would have carried out anyway, while deliberately holding back on the exact share. [19]

But why do so many companies choose this argument of all things when the real reason is a plain decision to save money? The answer lies in the reaction of the capital markets. Since the first broadly available language models appeared at the end of 2022, according to an analysis by J.P. Morgan (jpmorgan.com), around three quarters of the price gains in the broad American stock index have come from the field of artificial intelligence. [22] In such a climate, a cut presented as AI modernization lands with investors in an entirely different way than a sober reference to costs that are too high. At the same time, personnel costs are one of the largest freely controllable blocks of spending, so that reducing them cross-subsidizes the high investments in the technology. Job cuts finance AI investments without measurable success, and AI supplies the argument that justifies the job cuts.

> "Job cuts finance AI investments without measurable success, and AI supplies the argument that justifies the job cuts."

At this point caution is called for, because the trackers circulating online that record layoffs day by day are no use as evidence. They throw job cuts from the technology industry into one pot with those from the financial industry and from health care, which need not have anything to do with the technology, and in this way they arrive at totals that do not stand up to a clean recalculation. What carries weight is therefore not the statistics of the daily reports but the survey by the consulting firm Gartner among 350 large companies cited at the outset, which robustly shows that the depth of the cuts reveals nothing about the benefit of the technology. [18] The much cited example of a corporation that cut thousands of jobs despite brilliant quarterly figures also demonstrates the logic of the capital market rather than the effect of the technology, because a company that cuts from a position of strength rarely does so out of technological necessity.

How this mechanism can be observed in an individual case is shown with almost textbook clarity by the British neobank Starling Bank (starlingbank.com) in July 2026. The company announced the cutting of around 130 jobs, about 3 percent of its more than 4,000 employees, and publicly presented this as a simplification of the structure in the course of its AI build-out. The underlying business figures tell a different story. Revenue had fallen by 5.6 percent to 887 million pounds in the past financial year, pre-tax profit by around 3 percent to 217 million pounds, and Starling itself named as the main cause not its own technology but the lowered base rate of the Bank of England, which reduced interest income by 52.5 million pounds. The company's language, according to which structures were being simplified and duplication reduced, is the classic restructuring formula that existed long before generative AI, and nowhere does it say which of the eliminated activities are actually being replaced by an AI system. At the same time, Starling made clear that it would continue to hire AI and software developers, a pattern that describes a shift in the personnel mix rather than automation in the narrower sense. What is remarkable is that Starling can also point to a real, product-side AI commitment, from spending analysis to an agentic financial assistant, which is what makes the case so revealing, because what hides behind the argument here is not a company without AI substance but one with genuine AI substance, which additionally dresses a cost-cutting step forced by the interest rate environment in the story that sounds better on the stock market than the sober reference to falling interest income. [23] Three months later the same formula appeared an order of magnitude larger and with the roles reversed. According to a Financial Times report of October 7, 2026, HSBC (hsbc.com) plans to cut half of the management and specialist roles in its British wealth business and up to 70 percent of its financial advisers; the bank merely confirmed that it is consulting with staff. The business press headlined the report with the advance of artificial intelligence. The quarterly earnings release of October 27, 2026, by contrast, mentions the technology only among the risks and points to a cost-saving program through which the bank has actioned annualized savings of around 1.9 billion dollars since 2025; the number of full-time equivalent staff has fallen by 4,390 since the end of 2025.[200]

What can be inspected in miniature at a neobank with more than 4,000 employees was demonstrated in large format three days later by the largest software corporation in the world. Microsoft (microsoft.com) announced on July 6, 2026 the cutting of around 4,800 jobs, a little more than 2 percent of its roughly 220,000 employees worldwide, and the wire reports slotted the step straight into the AI layoff wave, in the German speaking countries even to the point of the formulation that the corporation was using productivity gains from artificial intelligence for the job cuts. The company itself said the opposite. Head of human resources Amy Coleman wrote explicitly in the memo to the workforce that the eliminated roles were not being replaced by artificial intelligence, though the technology was changing the way work gets done. The actual causes stood right next to it in the financial data, namely investments of 190 billion dollars in AI infrastructure in the current year alone, a share price that had lost around 30 percent and thus about 1.2 trillion dollars in market value within nine months, and the resulting pressure from the capital markets on operating costs. The cross-subsidy logic of this chapter can hardly be illustrated more clearly, because in the same week in which the layoffs were issued, Microsoft launched a unit funded with 2.5 billion dollars that sends 6,000 developers directly into its customers' companies to make AI systems productive there. What is being replaced, then, is not people by the machine, but classic sales and consulting roles by more technical roles at the same point of value creation, while the job cuts help pay for the data centers. [24]

The largest part of the cuts moreover falls on a business whose problems have nothing to do with the capability of the technology. The Xbox gaming division is losing sixteen hundred jobs in the first step and, by the end of the financial year, an expected 3,200, about a fifth of its global workforce, and the new division head Asha Sharma justified this in her memo with remarkable candor. The business was not healthy, it was working with margins three to ten times lower than those of comparable platform and publishing businesses, strategic bets such as the Game Pass subscription model had fallen short of expectations, and some work passed through up to 14 layers of management, which are now to be reduced to five at most. That is recognizably the vocabulary of a balance sheet repair and not that of automation. The only place where artificial intelligence actually appears in this justification is an indirect one, because the worst hardware crisis in the industry's history, which Sharma laments, is fed by chip prices that of all things the data center boom of the AI vendors is driving up. Where the technology acts on employment here, it acts through the capital and cost channel, not through the productivity of a digital colleague. That of all corporations the one that profits from the AI story like no other denies the replacement thesis internally and in writing, while the headlines ascribe it to that same corporation as a matter of course, makes the case perhaps the most revealing of the year so far. [24]

That this mechanic is not an Anglo-Saxon peculiarity had already been demonstrated a few days earlier by Europe's largest software corporation, in a third variant that manages entirely without layoffs. On the evening of July 1, 2026, the SAP executive board announced to the workforce in an internal email that new hiring would in future be concentrated exclusively on selected profiles, above all on central AI roles that were decisive for long term success, that internal travel without an AI connection would be paused and spending on suppliers cut, all to finance what was described as a significant AI push. Bloomberg had seen the memo, a spokesperson expressly confirmed the thrust when asked, and customer facing work and critical AI projects would accordingly remain fully funded. The language policy alone is revealing, because while the international press wrote of a hiring freeze, the company itself spoke merely of greater discipline in hiring, spending and travel, and the German business press emphasized that this was not a general freeze but strategic steering. It is the same gap in interpretation as at Microsoft, only with the roles reversed, because there the press sharpened the AI attribution against the company's denial, whereas here the company softens the description of a cost brake that is harder than it may care to present it. In Walldorf too the causes lay not in the engine room but on the capital market. The SAP share had lost around a third of its value since the beginning of the year, because investors fear that artificial intelligence could hollow out demand for the classic software business, and one day before the memo the corporation had lost the bidding contest for the industrial AI firm Cognite, which went to Schneider Electric for 3.1 billion dollars. Here nobody is laid off, and yet the technology acts on employment, namely through the diversion of personnel and non-personnel budgets into AI budgets under the pressure of a capital market that has already priced in the disruption of the corporation before it has taken place. [25]

The case gains its real depth only against the background of its prehistory. SAP (sap.com) had completed a restructuring at the beginning of 2025 that had grown from an initially announced 8,000 to a gross figure of around 10,000 positions and cost 3.2 billion euros, while the total workforce remained largely stable thanks to simultaneous new hiring, a rebuild, then, and not a reduction. Chief financial officer Dominik Asam cast this logic into a remarkable formula in the autumn of 2025, when he announced that the corporation would in future permanently cut 1 to 2 percent of its workforce per year and compared this to brushing one's teeth. Restructuring is thus no longer an event but a permanent condition, a quiet, continuous reduction below the headline threshold that appears in no layoff statistic as a wave and yet takes effect year after year. Chief executive Christian Klein said in the spring of 2026 that it would be an illusion to believe that artificial intelligence drives productivity and the workforce nevertheless ends up looking the same, that fewer of certain roles that exist today would be needed. That of all corporations the one that was celebrated in the summer of 2025, thanks to its AI bet, as temporarily the most valuable company in Europe is a year later saving on hiring, travel and suppliers in order to keep financing that same bet makes it the European key case of this chapter and at the same time points to a question that the second part of this report explores in more depth, namely out of what depth of capital European companies are actually running their AI race. [25]

The fourth case is supplied by the corporation that has advocated the replacement thesis most loudly, and it is the most revealing, because there the argument was not a pretext but the plan. According to a special report by the news agency Reuters from August 26, 2026, based on internal documents and conversations with more than 20 people involved, the leadership of Meta (meta.com) drew up in January 2026, under the name Project OT, the conversion into a company in which AI agents take over a large part of the daily work of thousands of employees and small cadres staffed with top talent supervise them. In the scenario planning, individual teams were shrunk by up to 60 percent, the cuts were to run in two waves in May and in November, and Meta has confirmed the plan, the two waves and the scenario to Reuters, but disputed the intention of cuts on this scale for the entire workforce. On the evening of May 19, a few hours before the first wave, Mark Zuckerberg called off the planning of the second. The first ran the next morning as scheduled and affected around 8,000 employees, that is 10 percent, and to the workforce Zuckerberg justified it not with the machine but with the capital expenditure that the corporation puts at 130 to 145 billion dollars for 2026. What led to the reversal Reuters could not establish; three circumstances that coincided are documented. The workforce openly pushed back, approval in the internal sentiment survey fell from 74 to 55 percent, and employees opposed the recording of keystrokes and mouse movements for training the company's own models with a petition. At the same time internal analyses that Reuters was able to see and that Meta has not commented on showed that the agent technology at the center of the plan did not deliver the hoped for gains: the number of changes to the internal platforms rose by 220 percent within a year, the number of changes that reached users as a new or improved feature by 36 percent, while serious technical and security incidents increased by 40 percent. On top of this came the investors' question of what the corporation had to show for its spending. At the beginning of July Zuckerberg conceded to the workforce that the technology had not developed as quickly as expected, and since then Meta has publicly advertised that it empowers people instead of automating work; in his essay from August he writes that companies will become smaller, that there will then simply be more companies with fewer people. [26] The case is so revealing for this chapter because it shows the mechanic from the other side. At Starling, Microsoft and SAP, replacement was a story about a decision to save money. At Meta it was meant seriously, and it failed because of the technology and because of the people it was supposed to replace. What remains is what remains in all four cases: the cuts that finance the investments, and a story that adapts to the situation.

That these four cases are not isolated outliers but an industry wide pattern is also evident in how the corporate leaders themselves speak in public. On July 6, 2026, the Wall Street Journal described a remarkable shift in mood among the leading figures of the American technology industry, away from the grim prediction of mass job losses through AI systems and toward a markedly more optimistic tone. OpenAI chief Sam Altman conceded at a conference in May 2026 that they had largely been right about the technological predictions but pretty wrong about their social and economic consequences. Anthropic chief Dario Amodei, who in May 2025 had still warned of the disappearance of half of all entry level jobs, reinterpreted the same question a year later as a pure management decision, that companies could do the same with fewer resources, which leads to layoffs, or do more with the same resources. With Amodei the distance this rhetoric has traveled can be measured especially precisely, because as recently as October 2024 he had, in his widely read essay Machines of Loving Grace, painted a country of geniuses in the data center whose blessings for health, prosperity and governance he described in glowing colors, while there he expressly left the question of work and meaning unresolved. In only a year and a half the path thus leads from the glowing picture of the future by way of the job loss warning to the soothing management formula, and this swing of the pendulum suggests that the public speech of corporate leaders about the employment consequences of their own technology follows less a stable body of data than the prevailing communicative weather. Amazon founder Jeff Bezos went further still in June 2026 at VivaTech in Paris and held out the prospect of a shortage of workers rather than mass unemployment for the future. A survey by EY-Parthenon among around twelve hundred chief executives in 21 countries underpins this shift with a figure: the share of those who expect substantial AI driven job cuts is said to have fallen from 46 percent in January 2025 to 20 percent in May 2026, though this specific figure could so far be verified only via secondary sources and not on EY's own primary page. [27]

At precisely this moment of public reassurance, the hard numbers show the opposite. According to the report by Challenger, Gray & Christmas (challengergray.com), in June 2026 artificial intelligence was for the fourth month in a row the most frequently cited reason for job cuts in the United States, with 14,029 AI attributed cuts in that month alone and 101,743 since the beginning of the year, equivalent to around 23 percent of all reported cuts. The technology sector recorded a total of 139,156 job cuts through June 2026, an increase of 83 percent over the same period in the previous year. While the executive boards express themselves more cautiously and more optimistically in public, at the same time companies are justifying more and more of their actual layoffs with artificial intelligence. What is said and what is reported thus diverge, and with that this chapter's thesis of the wrongly named force applies no longer only to individual companies but to the entire industry. [27]

How little the workforces themselves believe the replacement story that is told over their heads is shown by a survey in which, for once, the employees themselves have their say. The second annual sentiment survey by Lenny's Newsletter, one of the highest reach industry newsletters of the American tech scene, covered more than 5,000 working tech employees in the summer of 2026, predominantly experienced professionals from product management, and as a self selected reader survey it is a mood picture and not a measurement of the labor market, but revealing precisely as a mood picture. Four out of ten respondents are indeed worried about their job, yet the fear of losing one's own job to artificial intelligence ranks at 22 percent near the end of the list of worries, and the connection between the observation that the technology is already taking over parts of one's own work and the fear of layoffs is practically zero. What preoccupies employees instead is the spiral of expectations, that is the worry of having to deliver ever more for the same salary, of getting into a pace that cannot be sustained, and of watching the quality of one's own work decline. The replacement thesis that the corporations offer the capital markets in their communications and that the news agencies perpetuate in their reports on layoffs finds no counterpart precisely where it would have to show up as lived experience, namely in the inside view of those who actually work with the technology. Seen from the inside too, the machine therefore does not act as a job eater but as the metronome of an intensification whose beneficiaries sit elsewhere. [28]

> "The fear of losing one's own job to artificial intelligence stands at the end of employees' list of worries, and the connection between experienced AI takeover and the fear of layoffs is zero."

Outside the American technology industry the picture is less relaxed, and it shows that the misnamed force has consequences even where nobody is being laid off. In Oliver Wyman's survey of almost 12,900 working people in 15 countries, 68 percent would give up part of their salary to work for an employer that promises never to replace people with AI, with a median of 5 percent. Asked what should happen if the technology doubled their team's output, respondents put fewer staff in last place. Asked what will actually happen, they put it first. Expectations diverge along the hierarchy: in executive management, 31 percent expect higher pay or a bonus if AI raises their productivity and 7 percent a higher risk of being laid off, while among employees without managerial responsibility it is the reverse, at 9 and 24 percent. The most consequential figure is a third one: 62 percent of those who work with AI every day have at some point played down how good they are with it, to avoid extra work, scrutiny or the impression of being replaceable. These are self-reported answers from an online survey, and a stated willingness to pay is not yet a payment. But they describe a workforce that takes the AI argument of the layoff announcements seriously, regardless of whether it is true, and that therefore hides its skills from its own employer. Whoever justifies job cuts with the technology slows down the very transformation they claim to be carrying out. [190]

> "Whoever justifies job cuts with the technology slows down the very transformation they claim to be carrying out."

The share that the technology can have in these job cuts at all can by now also be bounded from the other side. The economists at Goldman Sachs have extrapolated the use cases currently in operation across the entire American economy and arrive at 2.5 percent of employees whose jobs would be at risk if these applications spread fully, which would lift the unemployment rate half a percentage point above its trend during the transition phase and, by the same calculation, would dissolve again within two years. For those affected that is no all clear, but it is an order of magnitude that bears no relation to the headlines. [29]

What the extrapolation estimates from above can, since the spring of 2026, be checked from below, against the official statistics. In its Business Trends and Outlook Survey of firms, the American Census Bureau ran a dedicated supplemental module on artificial intelligence with the full sample of 1.2 million establishments between mid November 2025 and early February 2026, recording separately where in the company the technology is used and what changes as a result. The result is sobering for the headline. Of the companies that use artificial intelligence at all, only about one in twenty reports any effect on the number of employees, and these 5 percent are split almost evenly between the two directions, 2.3 percent with an increase against 2.0 percent with a decrease. Where the structure of tasks changes at all, two thirds of companies exclusively expand the tasks of their employees, while pure replacement occurs at only 5 percent. Replacement meanwhile happens elsewhere, because 16 percent of users swap existing software and equipment for AI supported solutions. In this count the technology therefore replaces capital a good three times as often as labor, and that is the opposite of what the public excitement assumes. What appears in the news as an attack on the workforce is, in the books, first of all an attack on last year's software license. Two caveats belong here: these are self reports by the companies and not a measurement, and the statements about what employees do with the technology come from the company representative and not from the employees themselves. [2]

The Census Bureau's count, however, covers every company that uses the technology, including those where it has not yet changed anything. How the calculation turns out where the technology actually delivers is shown by a collection of cases that Erik Brynjolfsson's Stanford Digital Economy Lab presented in April 2026. In it, Elisa Pereira, Alvin Wang Graylin and Brynjolfsson examined only AI deployments that demonstrably run in operation, have been in use for at least three months and report a quantified benefit, 51 cases from 41 organizations in all, based on structured interviews with those responsible and on internal documents. In 45 percent of these cases the productivity gain led to fewer staff, in 19 percent a hire that would otherwise have been necessary did not take place, in a further 19 percent the workforce remained unchanged, and only in 17 percent did employees move into more demanding tasks. Where the gain actually materializes, it therefore costs jobs in the majority of cases, either existing ones or ones that are never created. Which way the decision goes, however, is determined according to the authors not by the technology but by strategy. Applications meant to bring in revenue tended to lead to redeployment or faster growth, applications for cost reduction tended to lead to direct cuts, and companies with cost-focused owners, such as private equity investors, or in a turnaround tended towards reduction, whereas growing companies tended towards acceleration. The authors themselves point out that the 45 percent might rather be a floor, because their cases come from an early phase of adoption. Two caveats belong here: the collection deliberately selects only successful cases and relies largely on self-reported information, and of the 41 organizations, 23 come from North America and only seven from Europe. Taken together with the Census Bureau's figures, the result is a picture that supports neither the headline nor the all-clear. The employment effect of the technology is small today because successful deployments are rare, and it grows with every successful deployment, in the direction set by strategy and owners. [187]

How little these measurements reach the public is shown by the annual survey that the polling institute Gallup has conducted together with Bentley University since 2023. In May 2026, 79 percent of adult Americans expected artificial intelligence to reduce the number of jobs in the United States over the coming ten years, six points more than a year earlier and the highest value since the series began. The share of those who ascribe more harm than benefit to the technology overall rose over the same period from 31 to 39 percent, and only 27 percent still trust companies to handle it responsibly. The mood tips most sharply of all among the youngest, whose entry level is the subject of the next chapter: among 18 to 29 year olds the expectation of shrinking employment jumped within one year from 62 to 75 percent, and the share that sees more harm than benefit from 36 to 47 percent, a larger jump than in any other age group. What is remarkable about this movement is its direction, because it contradicts the evidence of this chapter. While executive board rhetoric is softening, while employees in the technology industry barely fear replacement, and while both the extrapolation and the official self reports show the measurable effect to be small, the population is hardening around exactly the story this chapter describes as the AI argument. The job killer thesis no longer needs the layoff statistics, it has become a basic social assumption. An expectation shared this widely is itself an economic quantity, because it shapes career choices, education decisions and political moods long before the machine does what people credit it with. [30]

> "Among 18 to 29 year olds the expectation of shrinking employment jumped within one year from 62 to 75 percent."

That this expectation runs ahead of the measurement not only in the population but also inside the companies themselves can be read, since August 2026, off a single survey series. In the McKinsey stocktaking of summer 2025, 32 percent of respondents expected the number of employees at their company to fall in the coming year because of artificial intelligence. A year later, in the follow up edition, 14 percent report that this is what actually happened, by the authors' calculation less than half of what was expected, and two thirds state that little or nothing has changed about the size of their workforce. What is remarkable is what follows from this experience, namely nothing. For the year now ahead, 39 percent expect a decline, more than in that previous year's survey whose expectation has just proven too high. The obvious objection, that two different groups are simply answering here, the authors checked themselves and answered in a footnote. For the 552 respondents who took part in both surveys, the finding is the same as for the full sample. The same people therefore missed their prediction and then expect more of it.[3]

That this expectation behaves differently in small companies than in large ones is shown by the only series in the German-speaking region that has asked it in the same form for three years. For the SME labor market study of the insurer AXA (axa.ch), the research institute Sotomo (sotomo.ch) surveys Swiss companies with five to 250 employees every spring, most recently 336 firms in March 2026. Of the SMEs that say they use artificial intelligence, 12 percent expect to cut staff because of the technology in the coming two years, and this value stands unchanged at 12 percent in 2024, 2025 and 2026. Just under half expect time savings with no consequences for the number of employees. Only the other side has moved: the share that expects more work because of the technology without adding staff for it has risen from 17 to 22 percent, and the expectation of hiring additional people because of it has halved from 10 to 5 percent. Where there is no share price to move, the expectation of job cuts stays frozen for three years, and the new experience is called extra work. These are self reported answers from a survey that an insurer commissions for its customers, and firms with fewer than five employees, the majority in Switzerland, are missing from it.[135]

This first force is thus entirely real in its consequences for those affected, yet its cause is named wrongly. It is the correction of an overshoot and the cross subsidy of a wave of investment, not the work of a machine pushing people out of the office. Anyone who reads it as proof of the apocalypse confuses the justification with the substance.

This capital market logic has a flip side that is rarely stated openly and that comes, of all places, from the field of corporate valuation itself. The financial economist Aswath Damodaran, who has taught the valuation of young technology companies for decades and who faces the AI boom as a skeptic, laid out in the spring of 2026 that the enormous market sizes assumed on the stock market for artificial intelligence only add up if the technology replaces people and does not merely make their work easier. A tool that makes employees more productive opens up an incomparably smaller market than a technology that makes whole salaries redundant, and for that reason the market size held out in prospect is at its core nothing other than the wage bill of the work it promises to displace. That is a valuation assumption and not an empirical finding, but it explains why the job eater story is cultivated most stubbornly where the most capital hangs on it. Whoever promises the replacement of the human being sells the bigger story, and on the stock market the valuation follows the story. The job killer story is therefore not only the justification for a cost cut, it is at the same time the promise on which a considerable part of today's AI valuations rests, which explains both its tenacity and its vulnerability, because a promise that is not kept gets written off sooner or later. [31]

The same story that dresses up job cuts turns into its exact mirror image on the construction site of artificial intelligence. Where the corporations pass off their cuts as progress, the operators of data centers sell their facilities to regions as a job engine, and both promises hold up worse under examination than their noise suggests. A study by the economists Dany Bahar and Greg Wright, published in May 2026 through the Brookings Institution and deliberately written as an independent academic working paper against the studies financed by the industry itself, linked around 770 American data centers with the employment and wage data of the counties in which they stand and, using the synthetic control method, computed for each of the 93 counties that received their first large data center between 2008 and 2024 a twin without a data center, drawn from around 3,000 unaffected comparison counties. The finding is ambivalent. The facilities do create work, total private employment in an affected county rises by 4 to 5 percent over five to six years, construction gains eleven and the information industry 22 percent, and wages grow by 3 to 4 percent; for a typical county with around 98,000 employees that comes to between 2,000 and 4,000 additional jobs after six years. At the same time the same calculation shows how strongly the industry aligned numbers overstate the effect, because data center counties were already growing faster than the average before construction, so that a naive comparison of the kind some industry commissioned reports make overestimates the employment effect threefold. [32]

The real point for the discussion about public incentives lies in how unevenly the benefit is distributed. Only the large hyperscale facilities, and all the more so entire clusters of four or more data centers, set off the technology effect that is invoked; such a cluster lifts employment in the information industry by 23 percent, while a single facility or a pure colocation center that merely houses other companies' servers leaves barely measurable effects in the information industry. Yet that is precisely where most of the public money flows, because tax breaks and incentives amount to only around 2 percent of the construction investment in counties with hyperscale facilities, but to 62 percent in counties with colocation centers, so that the subsidy weighs heaviest exactly where it sets off the least work. Virginia's tax exemption for data centers alone cost the state more than 1.6 billion dollars in fiscal year 2025, according to its own financial report. For the European discussion, in which the same facilities are courted with the same arguments and the same location incentives, the finding is a warning, even though the numbers come from the United States and the paper is to be read as a working paper that has not yet been peer reviewed. The job engine error is the exact counterpart to the job killer error, and both live off the fact that nobody measures the promised effect against reality. [32]

The other side of the discussion deserves the same examination, and it comes out no gentler there. On August 18, 2026, the economists at Landesbank Hessen-Thüringen assembled the reasons why artificial intelligence is not to be expected to produce high unemployment for the economy as a whole, and named four mechanisms that are regularly missing from the doom scenario: productivity gains make human work more valuable instead of making it redundant, automation frees up capacity for other activities, innovations bring forth occupations that nobody could name before, and higher incomes generate demand that in turn creates employment. These mechanisms are well founded, the analysis expressly draws for them on a 2015 essay by David Autor, and the official American business survey above supports them involuntarily, because there capital is replaced a good three times as often as labor. Only the example with which analyses of this kind have been supporting their all clear for years has itself by now become a case that has to be checked against the time series. The example cited is American bank tellers, whose number had risen by 50,000 to around 550,000 between 1980 and 2010 despite the spread of ATMs. According to the occupational statistics of the American Bureau of Labor Statistics it stood at 347,400 in 2024, and the same agency expects a further decline of 13 percent by 2034. The mechanism remains correct, its piece of evidence does not. Anyone who supports the all clear with a snapshot whose time series has shown the opposite for a decade and a half makes the same mistake as the headline he wants to refute, namely confusing a story with a measurement. [33]

That this argument can be invoked not only for job cuts but also for one's own added workload has been demonstrated since August 2026 by a German agency. Andrea Nahles, chair of the executive board of the Federal Employment Agency, describes in an interview with Deutschlandfunk on August 15, 2026 an increase in appeal proceedings against job center decisions and traces it to the use of artificial intelligence by those affected. Where half a page used to be enough, ten pages are now common, which in her words creates an insane amount of extra work for the staff, especially since the longer letters sometimes contain hallucinations, that is, invented citations and court rulings. For a single federal state the development can be quantified, because the Regional Directorate North reports a total of 10,348 appeals for Schleswig-Holstein in the first half of 2026, which extrapolated is around a quarter more than in the previous year. How many of them actually came about with machine assistance the agency expressly cannot say, because that is recorded nowhere. [34]

It is exactly at this point that a look into the official statistics pays off, and it reorders the case. According to the agency's annual analysis from January 2026, 501,667 appeals were already filed nationwide in 2025, 78,310 more than the year before, and the appeal rate of the joint institutions rose from 1.8 to 2.1 percent across around 21.2 million decisions, with the agency naming as the most frequent points of dispute the calculation of housing costs, the withdrawal and reimbursement of benefits, and the crediting of income and assets. The increase had therefore begun long before anyone attributed it to the technology. The number with which the case is told publicly also measures something other than what it appears to measure. Of the 476,728 appeals settled in 2025, 147,213 led to a changed decision, from which the reporting makes the sentence that more than one in four appeals succeeds. But the job center had decided in legal error in only 42,303 cases, while almost half of the changes came simply from documents being submitted later in the proceedings or outstanding duties to cooperate being fulfilled. [35]

What remains of the case nevertheless carries weight, because it contradicts the common job killer story. If the agency's observation is correct, then in public administration the technology first appears not as work saved but as additional work, set off from the other side of the desk, and it hits precisely that sector which has the oldest workforce in the DACH region. Nahles' first idea, letting artificial intelligence talk to artificial intelligence, she rejects in the same interview as a permanent solution, and for good reason, because it describes a cycle in which both sides automate their effort while the procedure becomes neither faster nor more correct. [34]

That the Federal Employment Agency's case is not a German peculiarity is shown by the first international case collection on this phenomenon, published in October 2026 at the AIES conference of the professional associations AAAI and ACM. Chris Schmitz of the Hertie School, Lewis Hammond of the Cooperative AI Foundation and Alan Chan of the Centre for the Governance of AI searched public administration and the courts in twelve countries and, out of 2,288 candidates, included 84 cases from eleven countries in which a rise in submissions is documented and publicly attributed to artificial intelligence; in 69 percent of these cases, the affected authority itself makes that attribution. The authors call the phenomenon agentic flooding, but the name runs ahead of the finding, because they found no autonomous agents operating government portals on their own. In 87 percent of cases, a language model cheaply supplies legal-sounding text while people handle the rest of the procedure by hand, and in nine out of ten cases this makes submissions above all longer and more complex, in six out of ten also more numerous. Courts and legal services are affected most often. The German cases in the collection include the social courts. There, according to the Federal Social Court's annual press briefing of February 2026, a single statement of grounds ran to 4,500 pages in an extreme case, and in North Rhine-Westphalia urgent proceedings rose by more than 55 percent to 7,615 in 2025, although the president of the state social court attributes only part of this to briefs drafted by laypeople with machine help; at this point the collection attributes more to the technology than its own source supports. [184][185]

Austria and Switzerland are not among the countries studied, but the first cases are on record there too. On 1 September 2026, Austria's Federal Administrative Court imposed a fine of 200 euros for abuse of process on a complainant who, in a data protection case, had filed twelve statements within just over four months, in the court's assessment evidently produced entirely with language models, largely repetitive and without new facts. In June 2026, the Swiss Federal Supreme Court refused even to consider a recognisably machine-drafted appeal for lack of substance. The authors of the case collection nevertheless remain cautious, rule out causal and quantitative claims, consider the burden modest so far and see no serious risk to the operation of public administration. [184][186]

On this last point, this report disagrees with the authors, and the reason lies in an inequality of means that their own material shows but does not think through. On one side of the desk stands a tool that is freely available to every citizen, costs almost nothing and improves with every model generation. On the other side, every submission must be read and examined in full, whether it runs to half a page or to 4,500, and the law sets narrow limits on using the same technology in the decision. Only in a quarter of the collected cases has an authority introduced AI tools of its own at all, almost always for a single step. According to the Capgemini survey, only 3 percent of public sector organisations run generative AI at scale [172], and in the DACH region public administration loses staff to age-related departures first and most. When citizens write their submissions with the machine and officials read them by hand, the burden grows faster than an authority can fill positions. This report therefore expects that, without a redesign, public administration will grind to a halt in the coming years wherever it must accept freely worded submissions and examine each one individually, first in objections against official decisions and urgent proceedings before the social courts, then in complaints, statements in participation procedures and freedom of information requests. At the social courts of North Rhine-Westphalia a case already takes 15.6 months on average, and in urgent proceedings, which mostly concern basic income support or unemployment benefit and thus rent and subsistence, a decision that arrives only after months is no decision at all. [184][185]

> "Without a redesign, public administration grinds to a halt wherever citizens write with the machine and officials read by hand."

What authorities do about it was also recorded by the authors, and it confirms the concern more than it dispels it. In just over half of the cases, authorities responded, mostly with small steps, and the authors expect that under time pressure they will reach for the measure that works immediately, that is, fees, in-person appointments, blocks and penalties; the Austrian fine for abuse of process is an early example. A technology that eases access to justice is thus answered with barriers that narrow it again, and first for those who cannot afford a lawyer, precisely in social court proceedings, where according to the state social court of North Rhine-Westphalia lawyers are already in short supply. The way out the authors recommend is a redesign of procedures with structured submissions and digital identity, and it is the only one that relieves both sides of the desk at once. It does, however, take years and demands exactly the organisational work that appears throughout this report as the truly scarce resource. [184][185][186]

<a id="WAAI-2026-C06.1"></a>
### Looking ahead

If the mechanics of this chapter are right, then the AI argument has an expiration date. Capital markets reward a story only for as long as it cannot be checked, and exactly this checkability grows with every quarter in which the missing link between cuts and success established by Gartner holds. [18] It is foreseeable that investors will begin to ask the question this study asks, namely which concrete task an AI system has actually taken over when job cuts are justified with the technology, and companies that have no answer to that will then pay more for this argument than it earns them today.

The real opportunity lies on the other side of the same calculation. The billions that job cuts free up today do in fact exist, and the companies that direct them not into the story but into the patient rebuild of their processes are buying exactly that capability which becomes visible in the further course of this report as the truly scarce resource. In a few years today's balance sheets will show who understood the wave of layoffs as a message to the stock market and who understood it as an investment in a new organization.

---

<a id="WAAI-2026-C07"></a>
## The career gap is measurable, but its cause is not so easy to pin down
