The second force is quieter than the first and considerably more serious, because it does not hit the experienced, who are reorganizing themselves, but the young, who have not even started. It shows up in the harder entry into working life, and unlike the wave of layoffs it cannot be dismissed as a mere justification. Here there is a real and measurable effect. Only its cause is anything but settled, and it is precisely this lack of clarity that is analytically the most interesting part of the whole discussion.

Before the individual studies get their say, it is worth looking at the measure they all work with, because in the public discussion it is misunderstood almost without exception. When the talk is of occupations strongly exposed to artificial intelligence, this exposure captures how many of an occupation's typical tasks overlap with what the technology can do today, and precisely not how likely it is that these activities will be replaced. The OECD sharpened this distinction once more in its most recent paper on skills in the age of AI, and in doing so drew a picture that looks contradictory at first sight. The most exposed of all are the highly qualified white collar occupations, the IT specialists, the business professionals, the managers and the engineers, that is, the activities that work most with language, analysis and knowledge. Yet the same occupations carry the lowest risk of actually being automated, because they rest on non-routine judgment, on social coordination and on experience that has so far escaped the machine, while conversely the highest automation risk lies with the low and medium skilled activities with routine processes, which sit far down the exposure ranking. Exposure therefore describes where the technology can change and complement work, and not where it displaces people, and anyone who equates the two is already committing that confusion of observation and cause which this chapter is about. An honest reading includes the fact that the underlying assessment of automation risk cannot yet capture the full effect of the widely available generative tools, because its survey period predates them, so that the line between mere exposure and real replaceability remains in motion. [36]
Since August 27, 2026 there has been official evidence for this. For the first time, the Bureau of Labor Statistics has attached its own exposure ranking to its employment projection for the years 2025 to 2035, and has furnished it with an unusually strict caveat: the tier is not an employment prediction, not a wage prediction and not an estimate of whether people will be replaced, and it does not even distinguish between automation and complementation; the ranking measures no employment effect, it merely supplies additional information for choosing an occupation. How seriously this caveat is meant can be read off the projection itself. Nursing sits in the second highest exposure tier and gains around 194,700 positions by 2035, software development sits in the highest tier and adds a good 10 percent, while in that same highest tier general office clerks lose 156,200 positions. Whoever ranks occupations by their exposure therefore gets a different order than whoever ranks them by expected demand. [37]

What exposure does not capture either is how well employees would cope with losing their job if that actually happened. Sam Manning and Tomás Aguirre of the Centre for the Governance of AI, a nonprofit research institute, have measured this second quantity in a volume that the National Bureau of Economic Research published in September 2026 under the title The Economics of Transformative AI. The editors Ajay Agrawal, Erik Brynjolfsson and Anton Korinek have gathered 16 papers in it that ask what an artificial intelligence matching human thinking in nearly all domains would mean for markets, companies and work. For American occupations, Manning and Aguirre combine four characteristics into a measure of adaptive capacity: the savings employees can draw on at short notice, the transferability of their skills to other occupations, the density of the labor market where they live, and their age. The result does not fit the widespread notion that the technology hits the weakest first. Exposure and adaptive capacity rise together, and of the employees in the most exposed quarter of occupations, around seven in ten work in occupations in the upper half of adaptive capacity, above all in managerial, professional and technical occupations. The group to which both apply at once, high exposure and low adaptive capacity, is considerably smaller and clearly defined. It comprises 6.1 million employees, 4.2 percent of the workforce covered, and works in secretarial, clerical, reception and assistant roles, that is, in the part of office work for which the Bureau of Labor Statistics expects job losses among general office clerks through 2035. The authors themselves state that neither their measure nor exposure predicts who will lose their job; the study measures no effect of the technology but sets the preconditions for a change of occupation against exposure. Whether this group turns out similarly narrow in the German-speaking countries cannot be read off the American data, because employment protection and unemployment insurance cushion a job loss differently there, as the Europe part of this report shows. That these are the same secretarial, reception and clerical occupations in which, according to the International Labour Organization, women are overrepresented is taken up in the following chapter. [191]

The widely noted starting point is a study by the Stanford Digital Economy Lab that rests on actual payroll data of American employees and that the authors have updated twice since; the authoritative version is the one of August 12, 2026, with data through June 2026. It finds that employment of 22 to 25 year olds in those occupations that are particularly exposed to artificial intelligence lies 19 percent below the level it would have reached had it followed the growth of their less exposed peers. In absolute numbers, employment in this age group fell by around 11 percent in the two most exposed fifths between the end of 2022 and June 2026, while it rose by around 10 percent in the three least exposed fifths. Older and more experienced employees show no comparable shortfall in the same occupations; the 35 to 49 year olds in the two top exposure fifths even gained around 10 percent. At first sight the technology here seems to be doing exactly what it is said to do, namely closing off the entry level and rewarding experience. What matters is that this is a relative effect between more and less exposed occupations and not a measured loss of jobs in absolute numbers. [4]

The real gain of the new version, however, lies not in the number but in the question of how this gap comes about. A shrinking headcount can have two causes, fewer new hires or more separations, and the two mean something entirely different. The authors separate this cleanly for the first time and find that separation rates among young employees in the exposed occupations fell at least as much as in the weakly exposed ones, that is, in exactly the opposite direction to what displacement would mean. The entire gap arises on the hiring side. The finding is therefore not the picture of a machine pushing people out of their jobs, but of an entry that is narrowing, and this distinction says more than any percentage figure. At the same time, the authors set out the weaknesses of their own measurement more openly than in any earlier version. The gap shrinks from 18 to 9 percentage points as soon as the education level of the occupations is netted out, and they themselves leave open whether education is a competing explanation here or precisely the channel through which the technology works. And it turns out considerably smaller in the official American household survey than in the payroll data, at minus 0.022 against minus 0.132 for the same window, while both sources almost agree in the information industry, financial services and business services, at minus 0.213 against minus 0.231. The direction of the finding is thus supported more broadly than ever before, but by the authors' own judgment its magnitude is tied to their data source and cannot be scaled up to the economy as a whole. [4]

Since the summer of 2026, however, this finding can no longer be dismissed as a snapshot. In June 2026 the same lab, together with the research unit of the payroll provider ADP (adp.com), launched a monthly updated dashboard that carries the measurement forward as a running series, and the first research note on it shows a gap that keeps widening instead of closing. In the underlying sample of 25,000 firms covering more than 700 occupations, employment of 22 to 25 year olds in the most exposed occupations has been shrinking by 3.8 percent per year since the launch of ChatGPT, while the least exposed occupations in the same age group are growing by 2 percent per year, and according to Erik Brynjolfsson, the lab's director, the decline has been deepening by around half a percentage point per month since the first publication. Muted signs of the same pattern now reach into the age group up to 34, which in the original study had still been unremarkable. The finding itself thus stands more stable and more current than ever before, and its interpretation remains untouched by that, because the updated measurement too reports a relative gap between occupational groups and no quantified job loss. [38]

**[Figure 3: The gap at the entry level is widening in the exposed occupations.]**

Exactly at this point the scientific controversy begins, and it is manned with weighty arguments on both sides. A study from the Federal Reserve by Jessica Liu and Douglas Webber ("AI Adoption and Firms' Job-Posting Behavior", March 2026), which evaluates more than a million companies, yields no indication that the adoption of artificial intelligence depresses employment, and with estimates so tightly bounded that even a small effect would have been noticed; what is measured there is job posting behavior and not wages themselves. [20] The study by Anders Humlum and Emilie Vestergaard ("Still Waters, Rapid Currents", 2026), which rests on Danish register data covering 25,000 employees from 7,000 workplaces, likewise finds no effect on income or working hours; it does confirm the collapse at the entry level, but shows that it does not originate with those firms that actually adopt the technology. [39] The collapse is therefore demonstrable, yet attributing it to artificial intelligence stands on shaky ground.

The broadest answer so far to precisely this question of attribution came in September 2026 from the Stanford Digital Economy Lab. Bharat Chandar, co-author of the Stanford series on entry-level employment, and Bouke Klein Teeselink of King's College London compare not occupations by their exposure but companies by whether they actually adopt generative AI, and they do so across 41 countries at once, drawing on 1.25 billion job postings and 154 million employment records from public professional profiles. A company counts as an adopter once it has posted, after the launch of ChatGPT, at least one job that requires working with generative AI; what is then measured is not the posting but the workforce of these companies' foreign subsidiaries, compared with similar subsidiaries of non-adopting companies in the same country and sector. The result contradicts the Danish finding on one point and confirms this report on another. By March 2026, the entry-level share of the workforce at adopting subsidiaries had fallen by 1.9 percentage points, in 23 of the 31 countries for which a separate estimate can be computed, from the United States through Spain and Poland to Brazil and Saudi Arabia. The shift, however, comes not from cuts but from growth. The number of experienced employees rises by 6.7 percent, the number of entrants is 2.5 percent lower, a value statistically indistinguishable from zero, and total employment at adopting subsidiaries grows by 3.3 percent; in none of the countries does it measurably fall. Adopting the technology thus changes whom a company employs, and hardly how many. Within occupations, demand among the experienced shifts toward the most exposed tasks and among entrants away from them; the share of computer and mathematical occupations grows most clearly, and within them the entry-level share falls most steeply. Among entrants the technology saves labor, among the experienced it creates additional labor, as the authors sum it up, and they state explicitly that growth of adopting companies relative to the rest does not prove growth in total employment, because adopters may also be taking market share from the others. For the German-speaking region the study contributes little, since for Germany, Austria and Switzerland the country estimates remain without a secure finding, and the professional profiles cover only around a quarter of official employment in Germany and Austria. The data source is moreover the same commercial one on which the Ramp analysis rests, which sees the collapse precisely not at the adopting firms; it covers mainly foreign subsidiaries of multinational groups and overrepresents managers and professionals. As an answer to the attribution question the finding is nonetheless the strongest so far: where companies demonstrably adopt the technology, the entry point narrows, yet no work disappears in the process, it moves one level up. [143]

For the United States alone, two economists at Harvard University arrive at a sharper picture with a related approach. Seyed Mahdi Hosseini Maasoum and Guy Lichtinger track workforces by seniority level in résumé data on around 65 million employees at more than 280,000 American companies and count a company as an adopter as soon as it advertises a position meant to introduce generative AI in-house; this applies to just under 4 percent of companies, which together account for around 16 percent of employment. In these companies, employment of entry-level workers falls from the first quarter of 2023 relative to the other firms, by around 9 percent after six quarters, while no such break appears among experienced staff. The decline is concentrated in occupations whose tasks the technology is most likely to take over, and it arises mainly because fewer newcomers are hired, not because more of them leave. Consistent with this, adopting companies list tasks that the technology can take over particularly easily less often in their postings for entry-level positions; statistically, however, this partial finding is only weakly supported. The paper is not peer reviewed, and the authors speak of an association, not a cause: adopters are considerably larger and differently composed than the other firms. The authors test monetary tightening, the post-pandemic hiring boom in the technology sector, remote work and a lower supply of newcomers; in their words, these tests support their interpretation, but they cannot rule out all other explanations. Together with the Stanford study across 41 countries, the Danish finding is thus faced with a second measurement that locates the slump precisely in the adopting companies.[164]

A special place in this dispute belongs to a research note that Anthropic (anthropic.com) itself put out in March 2026, written by the economists Maxim Massenkoff and Peter McCrory. It develops a new measure that interlocks the theoretical feasibility of an occupational task with the actually observed occupational use of its own models, weighted by degree of automation, and then tests the employment consequences with the official American household survey, that is, with a third data source alongside the payroll records of the Stanford series and the job postings of the study by the Federal Reserve. The result turns out remarkably sober. Since the end of 2022 there is no systematic rise in unemployment in the most exposed occupations, no matter where the threshold for exposure is drawn and in the unemployment insurance data as well, and the unemployment rate of the youngest stays just as flat. Only on the hiring side does the familiar pattern repeat itself, because the rate at which 22 to 25 year olds take up a new position in a strongly exposed occupation lies 14 percent below the 2022 level after the launch of ChatGPT, by the authors' own judgment just barely statistically significant, while among older workers no such decline can be observed. The direction of the Stanford finding is thus replicated once more and in a further data source, but distinctly more weakly, and the weight of this source lies precisely in its origin. A vendor whose valuation hangs on the story of its technology's economic force would have every reason to report dramatic employment effects, and instead publishes a null finding on unemployment together with a finding on the entry level that is secured by the thinnest of margins. As a self-measurement on its own platform, the note nevertheless remains driven by interest and cannot be recomputed by outsiders, and the authors themselves record that the young who are not hired may also stay in existing positions, take other paths or return to university. [40] Exactly this weakness is offset by a finding that comes from a quite different corner. Sarah Eckhardt and Nathan Goldschlag, working for the Economic Innovation Group, divided American workers into five equally sized groups by their degree of exposure and tracked the development of their unemployment rate between 2022 and the beginning of 2025. In the fifth of the most exposed employees the rate rose by 0.30 percentage points, in the fifth of the least exposed, by contrast, by 0.94. Anyone looking for the traces of a machine pushing people out of employment finds them weakest of all precisely where they ought to be clearest. This evaluation too comes not from a peer reviewed journal but from a Washington think tank, yet it has no vendor behind it and it measures unemployment where the Anthropic note remains dependent on its own platform. [41]

An even more recent study shifts this dispute onto a third level of observation and thereby makes it clearer rather than settling it. The research team of the financial services provider Ramp linked the actually observed spending behavior on artificial intelligence for a good 21,000 American firms with the employee data of a labor market data vendor and found that after adoption the companies expanded their headcount by a good 10 percent over the following two years, but that this increase was borne almost entirely by the most intensive adopters, while the hesitant adopters showed no appreciable change. What is remarkable is that the entry level of all things grew fastest, because at the most heavily investing companies the number of entry level employees rose by 12 percent over two years. At first sight this finding cuts across the Stanford result, according to which the entry level is closing off for the young, and yet the two do not contradict each other once one recognizes that they ask different questions. Stanford examines occupation and age and answers in which activities access is narrowing for the young, while Ramp examines the firm and the intensity of its adoption and answers which companies grow. A company can hire more people in total because it scales with the technology and becomes more productive, and in the same move devalue the entry level within the particularly exposed occupations. The Stanford researchers even record this separation themselves, in that they recompute their collapse once more without the information sector as a check and find it almost unchanged, so that it does not hang on the technology occupations, while the gains at Ramp conversely cluster precisely in the information sector and at the most investment intensive firms. This finding too calls for the customary caution, because the source is a commercial vendor with its own interest in rising AI spending, because it measures a correlation and not a causal build up of jobs, and because its adopters are from the outset the larger and faster growing companies. What it contributes to the discussion is therefore not an all clear, but the insight that the second force is not to be read as an either or, but as a coexistence of two axes on which the young can lose in relative terms while the adopting firms grow in absolute terms. [21]

What this coexistence looks like inside a single company was shown in October 2026 by, of all companies, the vendor whose research note this chapter already cites. For the Anthropic Institute, Nathan Wilmers, Maxim Massenkoff and Peter McCrory evaluated their own company's personnel, usage and output data and interviewed 17 employees. The quiet force becomes visible in 2026: at the vendor itself, the share of entry-level hires among new hires falls from 27 to 9 percent. Their share of the workforce in technical departments declined from 21 to 14 percent since early 2025, postings for entry-level positions have fallen particularly sharply since December 2025, and all of this in a company that tripled in size over the same year and reports no job cuts. By the authors' account, the discussion about taking on fewer entry-level people began in January 2026 in the teams that work most intensively with the company's own technology, because writing code and finding bugs could increasingly be handed to the machine; one manager says the performance jump of a new model changed overnight what they were looking for in candidates. A third of managers in machine learning and a fifth in software engineering report hiring less for that reason. At the same time, what hiring managers record in their interview scorecards is shifting. Judgment and autonomy gain, domain depth loses, and one manager explains this by saying that he expects the models to remain uneven in their capabilities and therefore wants people who can be deployed across domains. The career gap thus arises here exactly as this chapter describes it, through hires that do not take place, and most strongly in those activities in which the machine takes over the most demanding tasks. Two caveats belong here. A vendor is reporting on itself, with figures nobody outside can verify, and about a company that deploys its technology earlier and more completely than any other; whether it is ahead of the rest or remains a special case cannot be decided in 2026. And the finding runs against Ramp's payroll data, according to which the firms investing most heavily employ more entry-level staff; the most intensive adopter of all shows the opposite. The authors themselves note that none of the measures with which their company protects its employees touches the decline among entry-level hires.[182]

> "The quiet force becomes visible in 2026: at the vendor itself, the share of entry-level hires among new hires falls from 27 to 9 percent."

A third decomposition of the same finding was put forward in August 2026 by a quantitative macro fund, and it starts neither from the occupation nor from the firm but from the individual work task. Benjamin Verschuere and Angus Cameron of Liminal Capital (liminal-capital.com) had language models sort all 17,536 task descriptions of the American occupational database O*NET into three classes, work that the machine completes on its own from start to finish, work for which a human has to be present or has to sign off, and physical work that it does not touch, and then mapped the result back onto occupations through the official employment and wage statistics. On that basis, 32 percent of American employment falls into the first class, 15 percent into the second and 53 percent into the third, and since 2021 work in the first class has grown 3.3 percentage points a year more slowly than the untouched work, while work in the second class has grown faster by the same margin. The figure that matters for this chapter stands at the beginning of the working life: the number of 22 to 25 year olds taking up a job in the most replaceable occupations is roughly a third below its 2021 level, by the authors' calculation around 227,000 entry level positions a year, without layoffs in the same occupations having increased. The New York Times put this finding on the front page of its business section on September 16, 2026, under the headline "The Quiet Way A.I. Is Hitting the Work Force". The value of this work, however, lies not in the headcount, which merely confirms the familiar pattern once more, but in the fact that it sets replaceable and complementable shares against each other within the same occupation instead of booking an occupation as exposed as a whole. That is exactly why the averages of the large exposure indices show so little, because in them two opposing movements cancel each other out that in reality hit different people. An honest reading includes the fact that the calculation here comes not from a research institute but from a fund that lives on recognizing such turning points early, that the work is not peer reviewed and that the classification of tasks comes from the very technology whose effect is to be measured; the authors themselves describe the results of their model as a scenario and not as a forecast. [136]

That the collapse is being attributed to the technology with growing matter of factness despite this uncertain body of evidence is demonstrated by the Corporate Recruiters Survey 2026, a worldwide survey of hiring managers that the Graduate Management Admission Council (GMAC), the association of leading business schools that administers the GMAT admission test, published in early summer 2026. More than 600 hiring managers from 39 countries were surveyed, a good half of whom recruit for companies on the global Fortune 500 lists, and around a third of them stated that they are replacing entry level positions with artificial intelligence, led by the technology industry, where 40 percent made this statement, followed by manufacturing. At first sight the cause finally seems to be named here, because the employers themselves cite the machine as the reason, yet precisely this form of evidence calls for the greatest restraint. It is a self-report about what hiring managers believe they are doing, and not a measurement of what the payrolls show, and it comes moreover from an association that has its own interest in the value of a business education. The figure therefore supports the direction of the Stanford finding, but it is at the same time the purest example of that confusion of observation and cause which this chapter is about, because a real hiring freeze at the entry level is furnished with the justification that promises the most attention in the current climate. [42]

An obvious and uncomfortable alternative explanation is the sharpest turn in interest rates in four decades, which made every new hire at the entry level more expensive overnight. It explains why the entry level in particular suffers, without any machine having to be involved, because entry level positions are the first that a company stops filling when money becomes expensive. There is a precedent that demonstrates this pattern strikingly. In the phase after the bursting of the dot-com bubble, a widespread story with no empirical basis kept young people from studying computer science, until the market recovered a few years later and demand returned. The pattern is repeating itself with astonishing precision. A steep rate increase is followed by a hiring freeze at the entry level, a convenient technological scapegoat supplies the explanation, and as soon as rates fall, demand returns.

Since January 2026 this interest rate explanation has also been available in worked out form, and its origin makes it doubly remarkable, because it comes from Zanna Iscenko and Fabien Curto Millet, two economists from the staff of the chief economist of Google (google.com), published through the Washington think tank Economic Innovation Group. Their evaluation of more than 238 million American online job postings shows that postings in the most exposed occupations already reached their peak in the spring of 2022 and fell steeply thereafter, more than half a year before ChatGPT appeared and in exactly the month in which the Federal Reserve began its sharpest series of rate increases in four decades. The employment decline from the end of 2022 would accordingly be only the delayed echo of a hiring freeze that came before it, because normal turnover is no longer offset by new hires. To this is added a mechanical argument that bears directly on the construction of the Stanford measurement. An age group as narrow as that of the 22 to 25 year olds is, unlike the older cohorts, not refilled from below by cohorts moving up, but lives almost entirely on new hires, and in a worked example by the authors a single year of hiring freeze without a single layoff is enough to shrink it by a quarter, while all other age groups remain unchanged. Finally, the exposed occupations cluster in the most interest rate sensitive industries of all, because around 38 percent of the employees in the top exposure fifth work in the information industry, in finance and in business services, against less than 2 percent in the bottom fifth. That of all people the chief economist of a technology group is the one presenting the reassuring reading has to be stated openly and counsels caution, but it does not devalue the data, and so the interest rate thesis has since stood in the room no longer as a suspicion, but as a worked out counter thesis. [43]

The Stanford researchers replied to this in February 2026 with an analysis of their own, and their answer shows how much this dispute sharpens the discussion instead of paralyzing it. AI exposed occupations, runs their first objection, are on average precisely not the most interest rate sensitive ones, the two measures are even negatively related, and the shortfall of the exposed occupations shows up regardless of how interest rate sensitive an occupation is. With the second objection the researchers turn the timing argument against the interest rate thesis, because in the calculation with the most comprehensive controls, which fully nets out firm specific shocks, the collapse among the youngest only becomes statistically detectable in 2024, that is, exactly in the period in which companies actually built the technology into their processes, and thus too late for the turn in interest rates and too early for an all clear. This second objection, however, is to be read with a caveat, because that very control calculation is one the authors report in their version of August 2026 only as consistent in direction, with a precision that fluctuates across the subsamples. At the same time the authors concede that artificial intelligence is unlikely to be the sole cause of the development. The dispute is therefore not decided, but it has become more precise, and precisely this sharpening is the gain, because the question is no longer whether the career gap exists, which the other side does not dispute either, but how much of it belongs to the machine and how much to expensive money. [4]

The interest rate explanation is joined by a second movement that narrows the entry level independently of any technology, and which is by now visible in European figures as well. McKinsey's HR Monitor, for which 1,303 HR professionals and 5,501 employees in ten countries were surveyed in January 2026, measures a clear decline in mobility. Voluntary turnover stands at 4 percent worldwide and 3.7 percent in Europe, a decline of two percentage points on the previous year, and only 17 percent of the employees surveyed intend to change employer within six months, after 23 percent a year earlier. At the same time pay moves from fifth to first place among the reasons for staying and is named by 52 percent, while 57 percent have had no change in pay within twelve months. This describes a labor market in which employees stay out of economic caution and not out of satisfaction. For the career gap it is the second half of the mechanism that Iscenko and Curto Millet describe for the Economic Innovation Group, because an entry-level position as a rule does not arise because a company grows but because someone moves on, and when those who move on stay put, access to the profession closes without any machine being involved. The survey comes from a consulting firm and rests on self-reports from a panel, and its year-on-year comparisons can only be read as a direction because the set of countries changed, which suffices here because the direction is what matters.[149][43]

Since late spring 2026 a second alternative explanation has stood alongside the turn in interest rates, and it is the analytically sharpest challenge that has so far arisen to the AI attribution, because it does not merely voice the suspicion but tests it. A working paper from the London School of Economics with the telling title The Broken Ladder, written by Peter John Lambert and Yannick Schindler, starts from a simple observation: the occupations most exposed to generative AI are for long stretches the same ones that can most easily be shifted to remote work, so that any study that looks only at AI exposure involuntarily measures the second great post-pandemic shock as well. On the basis of around 240 million new hires and a good 400 million job postings from the United States, Great Britain, Canada and Australia for the years 2017 to 2025, the authors first show the familiar finding, because considered on its own a markedly raised AI exposure goes together, up to 2025, with a junior share of new hires that is around five percentage points lower. Only the same holds for remote work exposure, and as soon as both quantities are estimated jointly, the remote work effect persists, while the AI coefficient shrinks sharply and is often no longer statistically distinguishable from zero. That in these data the collapse at the entry level already sets in during 2022, and thus before the mass diffusion of the generative tools, fits this picture. The authors do not claim that the technology plays no role, but they do claim that a considerable part of what the discussion attributes to the machine is in truth a consequence of distributed work, which has made the training of beginners expensive, and this paper too, as a working paper that has not yet been peer reviewed, is to be read with the appropriate caution. [44]

Almost at the same time, a post on the research platform of the Federal Reserve Bank of New York, written by Natalia Emanuel, Emma Harrington and Amanda Pallais, supported the same reading from the unemployment side. The unemployment rate of young American college graduates rose by a fifth between the years before and after the pandemic, while among experienced graduates it even declined slightly, and this widened age gap sits entirely in those occupations that can be done well remotely, while in in-person occupations it disappeared again long ago after a brief spike in 2020. By the authors' back-of-the-envelope calculation, the shift to working from home thus explains 64 percent of the entire increase, and they describe the mechanics behind it using the internal data of a Fortune 500 corporation: those who sit next to their colleagues receive measurably more feedback and mentoring, the loss hits the youngest hardest, and the same firm permanently filled positions in distributed teams with experienced people, even after the offices had long been open again. Here too caution belongs to the picture, because this is a blog post with a calculation expressly labeled as an estimate and with mechanics from a single software firm, and the authors themselves note that the technology may have a stronger effect in the future. For the question of this chapter the point is considerable nonetheless. The dispute over the cause of the collapse at the entry level thus has three candidates, the machine, expensive money and distance, which do not exclude one another, and the most honest reading is that the most popular of the three explanations is currently the one with the weakest empirical support. Yet the explanations converge in their end point in a troubling way, because whether the entry level loses its learning function to the machine, which takes over the routine preparatory work, or to the distance, which cuts off mentoring, it thins out in either case, and the question of where the experienced people of the day after tomorrow are supposed to come from arises regardless of which of the three candidates ultimately proves to be the main cause. [45]

> "The unemployment rate of young college graduates rose by a fifth between the years before and after the pandemic, among the experienced it fell, and this age gap is found entirely in those occupations that can be done well remotely."

Since the spring of 2026 there has for the first time been a study on this dispute that tests both candidates against each other instead of dealing with them one after the other, and it comes from Europe. Magnus Lodefalk, Lydia Löthman, Michael Koch and Erik Engberg of the AI-Econ Lab at Örebro University use a coincidence of the Swedish interest rate cycle as a measuring instrument. The Riksbank began its rate increases in April 2022, seven months before ChatGPT appeared, and this gap makes it possible to separate the two explanations in time, which is not possible in the American data because the two ran almost simultaneously. The result is split in two. In the 4.6 million job postings of the public employment service the decline clearly goes back to the turn in interest rates, the effect of the ChatGPT launch is statistically indistinguishable from zero, and it clusters of all places in those occupations that precisely cannot be moved to working from home. For employment itself a different picture emerges. On the basis of the complete population register, that is not a sample but all employment relationships with monthly employer reporting, an age gap opens up within the same employer. In the most exposed occupations, employment of 22 to 25 year olds in the first half of 2025 is 5.5 percent below that in less exposed occupations, for 26 to 30 year olds it is just under 5 percent, and for those over 50 there is an increase of a good one percent. Among young women the decline is about twice as large as among young men, and a third of this gap disappears once their occupational distribution is statistically adjusted to that of the men, while the rest sits within the same occupations. This is the first evidence outside the United States, and on register data rather than on surveys or postings, for what this chapter claims: overall demand is not collapsing, but who gets hired is shifting. The authors themselves urge caution. The paper has not yet been peer reviewed, the 5.5 percent is the end point of a curve that diverges over time and not the average effect, which is considerably smaller, and from Finland there is a null finding using the same research design. That is exactly where things stand: the effect is there, it is now documented in Europe as well, and it is smaller and slower than the headlines present it. [46]

What the official statistics of the United States have to say on this was worked out by the central bank itself in August 2026, and its finding is broken down more sharply by age and education than any series of job postings. Ingrid Chen, Marianna Kudlyak and Riva Mikhlin of the Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco (frbsf.org) evaluated, in Economic Letter 2026-20 of August 3, 2026, under the title Job-Finding Anomalies of the Current Expansion, the monthly transitions into employment from the Current Population Survey between January 2023 and January 2026. The share of unemployed people who find a job within a month fell in those three years from 30 to 25 percent, the corresponding share of those outside the labor force from 4.9 to 4.2 percent. What is revealing is less the decline itself than its distribution, and it differs between the two groups. Outside the labor force, where students and career entrants stand, 16 to 24 year olds lost 20 percent of their prospect of finding a job and 25 to 34 year olds 16 percent, while the value for 35 to 64 year olds barely moved; those hit there are above all the young and the less educated, people with a college degree not at all. Among the unemployed, by contrast, the slump sits with the 35 to 54 year olds and with college graduates, whose value fell by 30 percent, more than in any other education group. The three central bank economists note that this pattern does not fit a mature stage of an expansion and may point to structural forces rather than a cyclical downturn. For the young they read it as a normalization after the exceptionally strong demand of the post-pandemic years; for the well educated unemployed they name downsizing in the technology sector, managerial restructuring, government contractor reductions and, as one possibility among several, the early effects of displacement by artificial intelligence in professional occupations, without being able to separate the shares. For this chapter the result cuts both ways. The narrowed access to a career is in the official statistics and among exactly those cohorts this chapter is about, yet the central bank looks for its cause not in the machine but in the cooling of an overheated market. Where it names the technology as a suspect, it is not the entrants who are affected but the experienced with a degree. [142]

How the technology affects the entry level can by now be measured. The earlier value of an entry level hire lay in being the cheap labor for the routine preparatory work, for the research, the first draft, the simple code. It is precisely this preparatory work that the digital team member now takes over. That this channel is actually at work has been shown by the Stanford researchers, who used Anthropic's usage index to break usage per occupation down into an automation share and an augmentation share, and only the first is connected with the employment trends. The larger the share of tasks that are handed over entirely to the machine, the weaker employment of the youngest in an occupation develops, while the share of genuine collaboration between humans and the AI colleague shows no discernible connection. [38] What retains its value is the judgment that only experience confers. In this way the technology upgrades experience and devalues the entry level without displacing the experienced.

> "The technology upgrades experience and devalues the entry level without displacing the experienced."

A recent Austrian study backs this pattern with a concrete number. An analysis of around 25,000 open positions published in June 2026 counts, among the more than 1,000 postings classified as data or AI positions, only about 6 percent that are aimed at people at the entry level, while almost 80 percent address experienced profiles and the ratio of senior to junior is more than six to one. This number is valuable as evidence, but it demands the same care that this study applies everywhere. It comes from a commercial source, it measures job postings and not actual hiring, and it captures a single reference date, so that it can show that the entry level share is low today, but not that it has fallen. When the accompanying coverage nonetheless attributes this low share to artificial intelligence, it commits exactly that confusion of observation and cause which this study criticizes, and thereby unintentionally becomes an example of the described pattern itself. [47] How far this protection of the experienced extends remains open, however, and a counterexample comes from finance. At HSBC, according to the Financial Times report of October 2026, up to 70 percent of British financial advisers are to go, that is, precisely experienced staff in direct client contact whose judgment ought to be protected by this logic. So far the bank has only confirmed that it is consulting with staff. Whether the machine takes over the advice there or a cost-saving program cuts personal advice back to clients with complex needs cannot yet be said; for this report the case is therefore a caveat and not a counterproof.[200]

A German finding close to official statistics at the same time sets a limit to the story of an entry level that is uniformly without prospects, and it also demonstrates how much a single aggregate number conceals. According to the analysis by Timon Hellwagner and Enzo Weber at the Institute for Employment Research, based on the microcensus and the statistics of the Federal Employment Agency, the labor force participation of 20 to 24 year olds in Germany stood at 76.8 percent in 2025 and thus at its highest level in decades, an increase of 7.1 points since 2015, while 25 to 64 year olds gained only 3.1 points over the same period. Anyone who reads this as an all clear for the entry level, however, is measuring the wrong quantity, and that can be shown from the same source. For one thing, according to an earlier analysis by the same institute, the increase between 2015 and 2023 comes predominantly from students, whose participation rate rose by 19.3 points to 56 percent, while among non-students it rose by only 1.6 points to 85.9 percent. To a considerable extent what is measured here is therefore the side job alongside studying and not entry into a profession. For another, the 2026 analysis shows where this labor force participation sits. Young people are overrepresented by 4.4 percentage points in food and hospitality occupations and by 3.4 percentage points in retail occupations, while they are underrepresented by 3.0 percentage points in business related service occupations and by 3.8 percentage points in company management and organization, that is precisely in those office occupations over which the dispute about AI exposure is conducted. Both caveats belong with it: the authors themselves reduce the lead in hospitality to 2.7 percentage points once part-time students are taken out, and the fact that 20 year olds rarely sit in company management is not an effect of the technology but a matter of course in any career. What remains nonetheless fits exactly into the picture that the Swedish register data draw. Labor force participation as a whole is high and continues to rise, and yet within the exposed occupations who gets hired is shifting. The one does not refute the other, they are two different measurements, and only the second answers the question of this chapter. [48]

A third German measurement demonstrates that the entry level is also narrowing where the technology is certainly not the cause. Andrea Nahles, chair of the executive board of the Federal Employment Agency, reported in mid August 2026 on 433,000 apprenticeship positions registered for the current year, 7 percent fewer than in the previous year, of which 162,000 were still unfilled at that point, and on the fact that only 55 percent of firms train apprentices at all any more. As the reason she names not artificial intelligence but companies' uncertainty about order books and economic prospects. For the question of this chapter that counts twice. In the German speaking countries the entry level runs to a considerable extent through the dual apprenticeship system and not through the academic office job on which the American discussion hangs, and it is precisely this path that is thinning out for cyclical reasons. Added to this is what, by the same agency's figures, hangs on it, because anyone without a completed vocational qualification carries an unemployment risk of 21 percent over their working life, compared with 3 percent for those with a qualification. Anyone who tells the career gap solely as an effect of the machine overlooks the fact that the most reliable protection against its consequences is already narrowing for other reasons. [5]

Since October 2026 there has for the first time been a measurement for Germany that tests the attribution to the technology itself instead of circumventing it. Melanie Arntz, Michele Dinelli and Oliver Schlenker at the Institute for Employment Research of the Federal Employment Agency have analyzed the monthly administrative data on an average of around 22.3 million full-time employees subject to social insurance contributions between January 2021 and December 2025 and set three measures against one another: exposure to language models, an occupation's suitability for remote work, and an occupation's vulnerability to the business cycle.[195] Unadjusted, precisely the divergence that the American series describe shows up, but only among the young: full-time employment of 20 to 29 year olds in highly exposed occupations fell by 3.8 percent between 2022 and 2025 and in less exposed occupations by 2.6 percent, a gap of 1.2 percentage points, while the same gap among 30 to 39 year olds comes to almost 6 and among 40 to 64 year olds to around 5 percentage points in favor of the more exposed occupations. What matters is what happens as soon as the three measures are estimated jointly. The relationship between exposure and employment remains negative among the young and grows stronger over the years, yet the authors state that it is "weak and not statistically significant"; the confidence intervals included zero in all three years. Vulnerability to the business cycle, by contrast, acts negatively throughout, it intensifies over the years, and it acts most strongly on the young: in occupations in economically weakening sectors, their employment fell by 4.2 percent between 2022 and 2025, by the paper's account more than twice as strongly as the AI relationship. A statistically robust AI relationship is found in only two subgroups, in manufacturing and in occupations not dominated by academic qualifications, and precisely not where the public discussion suspects it, namely in the academic office job. Two limits belong to this finding. The exposure measure captures how much language model work an occupation contains, not whether people in the firm actually work with these models; and the analysis covers only full-time positions subject to social insurance contributions, so it expressly excludes apprentices, which means it does not see the path into working life that is the more common one in the German-speaking countries.

How sharply entry via vocational training differs from entry via the academic office job can, since September 2026, be read off a time series for Germany for the first time. The Hiring Lab, the research arm of the job board Indeed, has sorted the job postings on its own platform since 2019 by whether their title signals an entry-level position, an internship or an apprenticeship, and the three series have diverged since 2022. Postings for entry-level positions peaked in July 2022 at up to 75 percent above the 2019 average, have since fallen by more than two thirds and in July 2026 stood 46 percent below the 2019 level, while the market as a whole, after a decline of 38 percent, was still 8 percent above 2019. In 26 of 28 occupational groups the supply of entry-level positions fell, in 23 of them more steeply than the overall market, by 75 percent in marketing, 70 percent in software development and 77 percent in IT applications; it grew only in nursing, medicine and pharmacy and in gastronomy and hospitality, that is, in occupations with staff shortages that do not require a university degree. Postings for vocational training including dual study programs, by contrast, stood on average 50 percent above the 2019 level in the season from August 2025 to July 2026, despite slight declines since 2023, and in 20 of 23 occupational groups the supply of apprenticeships developed better than entry-level positions; in software development, 70 percent fewer entry-level positions stood against 27 percent more apprenticeships for IT specialists. Two caveats belong here, and the author of the analysis, Virginia Sondergeld, draws them herself. Apprenticeship occupations lie to a greater extent in fields the technology barely reaches, since only in 15 percent of apprenticeship postings can more than 60 percent of the required skills be taken over or supported by generative AI according to the company's own measure, against 42 percent of entry-level postings, and for companies in uncertain times it can simply be the cheaper option to train young staff themselves rather than hire university graduates. What the analysis says about causes fits the picture of the three candidates: The gap between entry-level positions in heavily and in lightly exposed occupational groups opened in the spring of 2022, half a year before ChatGPT, and the Hiring Lab states that an AI effect cannot so far be demonstrated in the data. What is measured are postings on a single platform, filtered by words in the job title, not hires and not employment. [140]

How these data land in public view became apparent even before the analysis was published. Die Zeit ran them on September 18, 2026 under a headline about where entry-level jobs are disappearing because of AI, and in the text let Melanie Arntz, Vice Director of the Institute for Employment Research (IAB), explain the opposite: In an economic crisis it is usual for entry-level positions to disappear, because companies are reluctant to lay people off, rely instead on age-related departures and hire fewer new staff, which means less effort and keeps knowledge inside the company. That is, in one sentence, the mechanism this chapter has measured in American and Swedish data: The gap arises on the hiring side and not through separations, and it hits the youngest because their age group consists almost entirely of new hires. That this restraint keeps knowledge inside the company is at the same time the reason why the experienced retain their value, and it leads to the question this chapter asks at the end, namely where the experienced of the day after tomorrow are supposed to come from. [141]

What these German measurements show for positions and apprenticeships has had a counterpart in wages since August 2026. In its June 2026 business survey, the Munich based ifo Institute asked more than 3,000 German companies that already use artificial intelligence how the technology will affect their employees' wages over the coming five years, and the answers diverge markedly by professional experience and qualification. For workers with less than five years of professional experience about every second company expects falling wages, for the more experienced around four in ten. The most revealing number in the survey is the one that contradicts the common expectation. For entrants with a university degree the share, at 50.8 percent, is even slightly above the 48.3 percent for entrants without a degree, and the prospect of a rising wage arises only with professional experience and then above all for the graduates, for whom 26 percent of companies consider a positive wage effect possible, compared with 16.3 percent for graduates at the start of their careers and 5.9 percent for entrants without a degree. The formal qualification therefore does not protect the entry level, it pays off only together with experience. This is exactly the shift that this chapter has already measured in postings, in stocks and in hiring, here for the first time from German firms and not in positions but in wages. Honesty requires noting that expectations were surveyed and not wages actually paid, that only companies already using the technology answered, and that the institute publishes neither the wording of the question nor the answer categories, which leaves open whether the respondents were thinking of wages actually falling or of slower growth. [49]

Since the spring of 2026 the Austrian snapshot from the job postings can be checked against a second, independently collected measurement that counts not postings but the stock. A research team at the Vienna University of Economics and Business around Christian Schumacher and Can Tihanyi, together with Andreas Schumacher, the chief innovation officer of the plant engineering firm VTU, measured the Austrian AI workforce from the standardized occupational profiles of Revelio Labs, that is essentially from the data of professional networks such as LinkedIn and XING, which cover a good fifth of official employment: 2.86 million position records from eight years.[50] By this account Austria has around 4,600 specialists visible in the panel whose occupation consists at its core of developing artificial intelligence, operating it or embedding it into business processes, and of them only 6 percent are at the entry level, compared with 23 percent in the rest of the employment in the same panel. The stock thus confirms the postings, and it says more than they do, because it shows how this workforce came about: not through the entry level but through lateral moves by experienced specialists from adjacent technical and analytical occupations. There is a second finding on top of that which sharpens the career gap. The few who do enter are the most likely to leave again, a good fifth of the entrants continue their career abroad, at the top of the hierarchy it is just under one in ten. The authors themselves read the 6 percent in two ways, as a characteristic of an experience intensive activity and as a warning that a workforce without new talent ages. For this chapter the point lies elsewhere: of all places, it is in the occupations that the technology itself brings forth that the entry level is narrowest. The report has not been peer reviewed and rests on a panel of professional network profiles, its absolute numbers are therefore panel sizes and not official stocks; the pattern, however, matches what postings, American data and Swedish data show alike.

An unusual piece of evidence for the same pattern comes from a June 2026 study by Harvard Business School and INSEAD that does not examine existing workforces but newly founded firms themselves. Hyunjin Kim and Rembrand Koning linked the employee data of around 2,200 American start-ups from the cohorts of the accelerator Y Combinator (ycombinator.com), as well as of a broader sample of more than 40,000 venture financed firms, with a database of employee profiles and found that AI native companies are around 25 percent smaller than comparable non-AI firms of the same founding cohort and the same industry and, on average, organized half a hierarchy level flatter. At the firm level the share of entrants at the AI native companies is around 4 to 6 percentage points lower and the share of the experienced correspondingly higher, a pattern that corresponds exactly to the shift described earlier within the same occupations, observed here, however, not at existing firms that are rebuilding themselves, but at firms that are designed from their founding onward with less entry level staff. The authors themselves expressly urge caution about translating their firm level finding directly into an economy wide employment finding, because artificial intelligence at the same time makes far more company formations possible than before, the number of first time AI financings in the broader sample was around eight times as high in 2024 as in 2020, so that a firm that is smaller on average does not necessarily mean less total employment in the segment. For the second force under discussion here it nonetheless remains notable that the devaluation of the entry level shows up at two independent levels of data, once in the wage data of existing occupations, once in the organizational structure of newly founded firms. [51]

> "AI native companies are around 25 percent smaller and organized more flatly than comparable firms of the same founding cohort and the same industry."

That this pattern does not remain confined to existing occupations and newly founded firms is suggested by an observation from the summer of 2026 that comes from practice and can count only as an observation from practice. The American content marketing specialist Kaleigh Moore counted around 50 advertised full-time positions for visibility in AI search systems within two weeks, a job profile that did not exist 18 months ago and that is only now looking for a name under abbreviations such as AEO or GEO, at companies from Stripe (stripe.com) through Pfizer (pfizer.com) to eBay (ebay.com) and across the industries. What is revealing about this collection is less the number, which comes from the private count of a commercially interested observer and measures no market size, than the level at which the positions are located. The advertised positions are almost without exception roles for directors, vice presidents and senior managers with their own budget and their own reporting lines and with salary ranges that reach more than $200,000 at the top, while entry level and junior positions are practically absent from the collection. The first marketing job profile that the AI wave itself has brought forth is therefore being staffed from the top down, and where new work arises the entry level is apparently not created in the first place. Job postings prove no employment effect, but they do prove organizational decisions, and this decision turns out here the same way as at the AI native new firms: the new field begins with experience. [52]

> "The first marketing job profile that the AI wave has brought forth is staffed from the top down, entry level positions are not even provided for."

How far this pattern has by now reached established market observation is shown by a prediction from the market research firm Gartner dated July 2026, which is doubly revealing for the question of this chapter because it unites both sides of the calculation in itself. On the one hand Gartner expects that in 2029, 60 percent of organizations will move their software development across the board to considerably smaller teams, compared with 15 percent in 2026, to units of today typically four to five and in future in part only two to three people, in which the classic role boundaries blur and every member, alongside their own specialty, also carries the oversight of AI agents. On the other hand Gartner precisely does not connect this expectation with shrinking workforces, because the responsible analyst Aliyah Camacho expects the technology to make demand for software developers rise overall and not fall, because the growing need for software will exceed the efficiency gains, and she insists that the small teams are not a cost cutting tactic but a reordering that aligns itself with the respective strengths of human and machine. The real point for this chapter lies in the warning that Gartner attaches to the same release. Those who rely on the technology in order to cut junior positions, it says, impede the transfer of knowledge, thin out their own supply of new talent and in the end force themselves to recruit in the most expensive and most contested segment of the labor market, and by 2028, according to the dated prediction, organizations that proceed this way will have hollowed out their own base of new talent. As the estimate of a commercial market research firm that does not disclose its method, the number itself is to be treated with the caution it calls for, but its direction is notable, because the one speaking here is not an education provider with its own interest in new talent, but the market researcher who is itself declaring the smaller teams the norm for companies, and in the same breath it tells them that the entry level must not be what gets cut to pay for it. [53]

> "Those who rely on the technology in order to cut junior positions end up having to recruit in the most expensive and most contested segment of the labor market."

Alongside these measurements stand statements and analyses by companies that have a stake of their own in the answer, and they explicitly do not belong in the same class as the measurements. Read as voices, they nevertheless show how those involved think about entry-level work themselves. Mark Russinovich, chief technology officer of the Azure cloud division, and Scott Hanselman, who is responsible for the developer community at Microsoft, describe in April 2026 in the journal of the Association for Computing Machinery how coding assistants accelerate experienced developers and slow down newcomers. They themselves reduce the resulting incentive to the formula "hire seniors, automate juniors", and they explicitly advise their colleagues against it: newcomers should continue to be hired, even at the price of temporarily lower capacity.[156] The AI Jobs Barometer of the consultancy PwC counts in American job postings for the most exposed occupations that entry-level positions requiring at least ten skills that in 2019 were demanded exclusively of experienced staff grew by 35 percent between 2019 and 2025, while the other entry-level positions in these occupations shrank by 10 percent.[157] Peter Brown, who leads workforce advisory at PwC and co-authored the joint report on entry-level work with the World Economic Forum, notes himself when asked that the Barometer shows what employers are asking for, and not necessarily what happens once someone is hired. The report itself draws on PwC's annual survey of chief executives: according to it, expectations of AI-related structural change at the entry level are almost twice as high as at mid and senior levels, as growth just as much as decline in employment.[158] According to its chief administration officer, the chipmaker AMD is hiring more interns than ever and pairing them with experienced engineers, because newcomers are often already highly proficient with the tools.[159] An analysis of usage data from ChatGPT Enterprise shows that entry-level employees send more messages than the average user in the same company, whereas managers and executives send fewer; the figure measures frequency, not the value of use, all five authors are financially tied to OpenAI, and the paper is not peer reviewed.[160] The counter-model to retreating from entry-level hiring is represented by two service firms. According to its chief financial officer, the IT group Cognizant took on just under 20,000 university graduates in 2025 and plans between 24,000 and 25,000 for 2026,[161] and the audit firm EY is replacing its previous internship in the United States with a paid learning phase of eight to twelve months, which, however, only starts in January 2028.[162] All of these statements come from companies that use them to advertise, advise or sell. They do not prove a trend, but they show that companies see the same question the measurements raise, and that they answer it differently.

How the expected reduction in employment is supposed to come about at all is answered by a survey that is methodologically in an entirely different league from the self-reports of HR managers. For the working paper "Firm Data on AI" (Yotzov, Barrero, Bloom, Bunn, Davis and others, National Bureau of Economic Research, 2026), four central banks and one Australian university surveyed just under 6,000 executives in the United States, the United Kingdom, Germany, and Australia between November 2025 and January 2026 with identically worded questions, whereby the participants were not paid, their identity was verified, and the panels have been validated against the official aggregate figures over ten years. For the past three years, more than nine out of ten of these companies report no effect of artificial intelligence on their employment whatsoever. For the coming three years they expect an average decline of 0.7 percent, and to a subsample of British firms the researchers put the decisive follow-up question, namely where this reduction is supposed to come from. Around two thirds of it is accounted for not by separations from existing employees, but by fewer new people being hired. That is the mechanism of this chapter, confirmed from the management side and from a source with no vendor interest. The labor market this expectation describes loses no jobs, it stops advertising new ones, and anyone who already holds a job notices nothing of it. Honesty requires noting that these are expectations of executives about their own company and not a measurement, and that the average figure only comes about because the researchers assign numerical values to five answer categories. [54]

The same survey put identical questions to around 3,000 American employees, and the distance between the two answers is perhaps the most revealing number in the entire paper. The employees expect artificial intelligence to raise employment at their own company by half a percent over the coming three years. Their superiors expect a decline for the same company, in the United States of 1.2 percent. On productivity the relationship reverses, because there the employees are the more cautious side at 0.9 percent and management the confident one at 2.3 percent. Anyone looking for the job killer will therefore find it neither in the payrolls nor in the minds of the workforce, but in the expectation of the executive floor, and so far that expectation has no measured counterpart. The same holds on the other side: the confidence of the employees also rests on nothing but an assumption. Two groups in the same company are in all likelihood wrong in opposite directions, and the public debate is only the loud version of this misunderstanding. [54]

> "Anyone looking for the job killer will find it neither in the payrolls nor in the workforce, but in the expectation of management, and so far that expectation has no measured result."

What the payroll data, the firm data, and the analyst warnings measure from the outside, those affected confirm not only in their expectations but also in the advice they pass on to those coming up behind them, and in a form that gives pause. The same sentiment survey by Lenny's Newsletter that supplied the inside view of employees in the chapter on the first force asked its more than 5,000 working respondents for the first time in 2026 whether they would recommend a career in their own profession to someone starting out. More than half would actively advise against it, and the gradient follows exactly the work experience whose beginning this chapter is about. The more senior someone already is, the more likely they still consider this career path worthwhile, founders advise almost neutrally, experienced professionals in the middle of their career advise against it most clearly, and even a third of those who are optimistic about their own career would no longer recommend their own path to anyone. The authors sum up this stance in the formula that the water is fine but you should not come in: the established feel secure themselves because the technology amplifies their experience, yet they have lost confidence that the path by which they came up is still open to those coming after them. It is notable that in its recommendations to executives the survey issues the same warning as Gartner, namely not to let the entry level dry out, because the entry level work that the machine does today was the teaching material on which the experienced of tomorrow learn. These figures too call for the usual qualification, because they come from a self-selected readership of an American industry newsletter, shaped by product managers and predominantly experienced, and they measure attitudes, not actual new hires. Yet that is precisely where their own weight lies, because here it is not those affected complaining about rejection they have suffered, but the beneficiaries of the shift themselves testifying that the entry level they no longer need has broken away for the others. [28]

The devaluation of the entry level has a consequence that reaches far beyond the current debate and is almost always overlooked in the excitement about the headline. If the level thins out on which people used to learn the craft, the question arises where the experienced professionals of the day after tomorrow are supposed to come from. The upgrading of the experienced by the technology lasts only as long as enough experienced people come up behind them, and an economy that neglects the training of its next generation is using up a finite resource. The second force is therefore real, but contested in its cause, and the honest position does not consist in resolving the dispute in favor of the simple story, but in enduring it and at the same time showing where the actual danger lies. That this question does not begin at the labor market is shown by the study of 26,811 Chinese schoolchildren evaluated in the chapter on the third force, in which ability was already declining at school while the work handed in kept looking better.[55]

How far the tasks on which newcomers learn have already passed to the machine is shown by Oliver Wyman's survey of August 2026 from the perspective of those who lead the next generation. 74 percent of office employees from middle management upwards say that at their workplace AI does at least some of the tasks on which newcomers used to learn, and of these only 27 percent say their employer has fully created new ways for newcomers to acquire the same skills. At the same time, office employees without managerial responsibility who use the technology frequently spend, by their own estimate, almost a third of their working week steering AI tools and checking their output, which is a form of management long before they have staff of their own. On the side of the boards an intention is added: 43 percent of the chief executives whom Oliver Wyman surveyed together with the New York Stock Exchange at the start of the year, more than half of them from North America, plan to cut entry-level positions over the next two years. These are perceptions, estimates and intentions and not a measurement of employment. The first two figures show from inside the companies the shift from execution to supervision that this report lists as its fourth key finding, and the third shows that many boards at the same time regard the entry level as the place to save, which Gartner explicitly warns against. [190]

Whether the technology really spares older workers can, since summer 2026, also be examined at the other end of the career. Geoffrey Sanzenbacher of the Center for Retirement Research at Boston College used the panel data of the American household survey Current Population Survey to track how many employees aged 55 and over are no longer working a year later, taking into account how strongly their occupation is exposed to artificial intelligence. Older workers are just as exposed to the technology as workers in the middle of their careers. Over the whole observation period, with first observations from 2014 to April 2025, the more exposed half of older workers leaves employment less often than the less exposed half, 11.7 versus 14.1 percent. The author attributes this advantage to the fact that exposed occupations require more education, are less physically demanding and pay better. Before the launch of ChatGPT, it persisted even with the same characteristics. Since then, in the author's assessment, it has shrunk considerably, and a significant share of the increase is due to transitions into unemployment; for exits from the labor force and for retirement, no measurable increase is visible. The finding rests on a single study, a short report by the research center, and the author himself names biases in both directions: if cuts to government research funding under the Trump administration hit exposed occupations particularly hard, that could overstate the effect, while the growth of AI-related occupations could understate it. For this chapter, this means: older workers in exposed occupations still leave employment less often than the others, but their advantage has narrowed since ChatGPT, and the additional exits show up in unemployment, not measurably in retirement.[163]

<a id="WAAI-2026-C07.1"></a>
### Looking ahead

The career gap will not be closed by one of the three candidate causes winning the scientific dispute, because the entry level thins out in each of the three scenarios. It will be closed by companies deliberately rebuilding the entry level, and the outlines of this rebuild are already recognizable. If the machine takes over the routine groundwork that used to be the teaching material of beginners, then the learning task of the person starting out shifts from execution to oversight, from the first draft to the examining judgment on the machine's draft, and an entry level understood this way can build experience faster than the old one, not more slowly. The German speaking countries in particular have, in their dual vocational training, a long grown institution in which this principle of guided learning on the real case has been practiced for generations and which can be carried over to working with digital team members, while other labor markets first have to invent something comparable.

> "When the machine takes over the routine work that used to be the learning task of the entry level, the entry level shifts from execution to oversight, and experience can be built faster that way than on the old path."

That this rebuild does not remain a mere wish is shown by the first companies already trying it out. The consulting firm BCG (bcg.com), in whose survey of 70 executives more than half name the slower development of junior staff as a risk, reports on the oil company Shell, which has reversed the learning paths of its entry level hires. There the younger ones must first outline a problem themselves, test their assumptions, and present a first analysis of their own before they bring in artificial intelligence at all, which then refines their work instead of taking it off their hands. The idea hits the core of the career gap, because judgment matures only on the work itself, and anyone who spares beginners the laborious groundwork entirely takes from them the practice out of which experience grows in the first place. As the report of a consultancy with its own interest in such rebuilds, the example is to be read with caution, but it shows vividly what a rebuilt entry level can look like. [56]

At the same time it is foreseeable that the companies cutting their entry level positions today will, in a few years, have to buy in on the most expensive and most contested segment of the market what they failed to train in junior staff. The first companies to have rebuilt the entry level will then have their lead exactly where the gap is opening up today.

---

<a id="WAAI-2026-C08"></a>
## The second axis of the career gap runs along gender, in risk as much as in access

The debate about the career gap is conducted almost exclusively as a question of age, and the previous chapter showed why: that is where the measurements are, and that is where the dispute is. The second axis runs along gender, and it deserves a chapter of its own, because it shows up in two different places that are regularly conflated in the public debate. The first place is risk, that is, the question of whether women more often work in occupations whose tasks the technology can take over. The second is access, that is, the question of whether women use the technology where they work as much as men do. There are now solid answers to both questions, and they come out differently.
