For the German-speaking region this counts twice, because the German labor market, as the German Institute for Economic Research notes, is strongly segregated by gender, and because the occupations in which demographic departures create the greatest additional demand are predominantly women's occupations.

On the risk side the international evidence is clear in its direction and divided on its magnitude. A study by the International Labour Organization published in March 2026, based on harmonized microdata from 84 countries, finds that female-dominated occupations are exposed to generative AI almost twice as often as male-dominated ones, at 29 versus 16 percent, and that the gap is larger still in the highest exposure category, at 16 versus 3 percent, although here too the distinction between exposure and actual displacement described at the outset applies. The cause lies in occupational choice itself and not in the technology, because women are overrepresented in secretarial, reception and clerical occupations with routine, easily codified tasks, while men work more in construction and manufacturing, where activities are harder to automate. [57] A methodologically independent study by the International Monetary Fund from early 2024, which uses a broader exposure measure not tailored to generative tools alone, confirms the basic direction, namely that in most of the countries examined women work more often in highly exposed occupations than men, but it adds a nuance. Because this exposure among women is split roughly in half between occupations in which the technology tends to complement and those in which it tends to replace, women therefore carry not only a greater risk but at the same time face a greater opportunity, provided that access to the appropriate tools is open to them. [57] It is precisely this access that has been the sticking point so far, because a study by the OECD from late 2024 does find a broadly similar unadjusted exposure rate between the genders, but documents a gap of 20 percentage points within the same occupations in the actual use of ChatGPT, at the expense of women. [57]

With the American payroll data on which the Stanford measurement of the career gap rests, this finding can for the first time be tested not only against exposure but against actual employment. Brynjolfsson, Chandar and Chen note in the August 2026 version that women at every age work in more heavily exposed occupations than men, in their own sample as well as in the official population survey. Of women aged 22 to 25, according to the official survey, 37.8 percent work in the two most heavily exposed fifths of occupations, compared with 31.4 percent of men the same age, and the gap persists across all age groups. The employment declines among the youngest are correspondingly steeper for young women than for young men, which the authors explain by the fact that young women are more concentrated in the shrinking, highly exposed occupations. The occupations themselves also differ by gender, because in the most heavily exposed fifth men are concentrated in technical activities such as software development, and in the least exposed fifth women are concentrated in cleaning and health occupations. The authors themselves urge caution, because their sample overstates the exposed occupations, more so for women than for men, and they explicitly describe the gender question as a topic that will have to be watched further in the coming years.[4]

For Germany a dedicated study of the risk has been available since April 2026, and it corrects the obvious expectation. Virginia Sondergeld of the Hiring Lab of the job board Indeed, at the same time a guest researcher at the German Institute for Economic Research (diw.de), has, together with Katharina Wrohlich and Julia Redelings of DIW, linked her own company's transformation index with the gender shares from the microcensus. That the first author works for the data provider needs to be disclosed, but it changes nothing about the link to the official data. The index estimates, for each occupational group, what share of the skills demanded in job ads can be changed wholly or in part by generative AI. There is no linear relationship between the share of women and transformation potential, but an inverted U. At both ends of the distribution, in childcare with more than 90 percent women as in construction with more than 95 percent men, hardly any core skill can be changed by the technology; in childcare fewer than one percent of skills can be changed completely and two thirds only minimally. The occupations with the highest potential, by contrast, software development at just under 84 percent, accounting at a good 75 and marketing at 73 percent, are predominantly mixed. Women work somewhat more often in occupations with higher potential, but the difference lies in the middle of the distribution: in occupations where 60 to 70 percent of skills can be changed, 23 percent of women work against 12 percent of men, while at the ends both genders are equally represented, at the lower end with 20 to 30 percent changeable skills at around 13 percent each, at the upper end with 70 to 80 percent at around 7 percent each. The institute concludes from this that the transformation potential concerns men as much as women, and that the need for further training exists for all. For this report the flip side of the finding is the more revealing one: the female-dominated care and support occupations, in which the demographics chapter locates the greatest additional demand, are the least reachable for the technology, and so precisely where the staffing gap is largest, the relief that the rest of the economy is hoping for fails to arrive.[58]

For the German-speaking region, the finding on access has been available since December 2025 for the first time as an official measurement, and it shows where the gap arises. Eurostat's statistics on ICT use in households, which asked about generative AI for the first time in 2025, find for Germany that 35 percent of men and 30 percent of women between 16 and 74 used generative AI in the three months before the survey. In private life, men use the tools 20 percent more often than women, 30 against 25 percent. At work the figure is 29 percent, 18 against 14 percent. The gap is therefore larger on the job than at home. Among 16 to 24 year olds it reverses: 57 percent of young women had used generative AI, 49 percent of young men. The gender gap, then, is not brought along from school and circles of friends, it arises in the workplace. Austria shows the same pattern with the same signs, a lead for men of 15 percent in private use and 30 percent at work, and a lead for women of 14 percent among the young. [6] PISA 2025 supports this finding a few years earlier, because among fifteen-year-olds in both countries it is the boys who more often do not use AI for school at all, 11 against 7 percent of girls in Germany and 13 against 10 percent in Austria.[177]

**[Figure 4: The gender gap arises at work, not at school.]**

What lies behind this is shown by a special analysis by the Institute for Employment Research together with the Initiative D21. It rests on a survey from July 2024 and therefore no longer reflects the current size of the gap, but it does reflect its conditions. Where the employer funds the training, the gap among intensive users shrinks to a single percentage point. Where the knowledge travels informally through technology-minded circles of friends and professional communities, by contrast, use rises only among men and the gap widens. [6] The gender axis of the career gap is therefore less a question of inclination than one of organization, and it is decided by the very factor that the chapter on the long rebuild identifies as the strongest of the entire rollout, paid and explicitly permitted training.

> "The gender axis of the career gap is less a question of inclination than one of organization, and it is decided by paid training."

The international sources contradict one another on the question of how unequally unadjusted exposure is actually distributed, and the German study shows that in this country the axis runs through the middle of the occupational distribution and not along its edges. But they agree precisely on the point that matters in this chapter. Whether through occupational choice or through access to the tools, the burdens and the opportunities of the technology are not distributed at random but follow existing patterns in the division of labor, and anyone who looks only at age sees only one half of the career gap.

<a id="WAAI-2026-C08.1"></a>
### Looking ahead

The gender axis will not be decided in the coming years by the technology, but in the same place as the age axis, namely by how companies organize training. According to the IAB analysis of 2024, the German usage gap shrinks to a single percentage point where the employer pays for further training, and it grows where the knowledge travels informally through technology-minded circles of friends. That is an unusually clear instruction for action on a question that is otherwise readily treated as a matter of culture, and it matches what the chapter on the long rebuild identifies as the single strongest factor of the entire rollout. Two longitudinal questions are thus set for the follow-up editions of this report. The first is whether the American observation that employment of young women in exposed occupations declines more steeply than that of young men repeats itself in the German-speaking region once the delay has been made up, or whether dismissal protection dampens this axis as well. The second is whether the gender gap at work of 29 percent that Eurostat reports for Germany in 2025 becomes measurably smaller in the annual figure for 2026, and whether it shrinks first in those companies that pay for the training. The additional demographic demand in the care and support occupations, where the technology can bring hardly any relief, remains the quantity that puts all the others in perspective. It does not shrink because office work becomes more productive, and it helps decide how many women will be able to move at all, in the coming years, into those occupations where the technology would offer them the greater opportunity.

---

<a id="WAAI-2026-C09"></a>
## The long rebuild is the most consequential of the three forces, and for now it is failing not because of the machine but because of the organization

Now that the loud first force has turned out to be a capital market phenomenon and the second has proven to be a measurable but contested collapse at the entry level, what remains is the third, the one that has the least in common with the pace of the headlines and is therefore the easiest to overlook. It is the real change that artificial intelligence sets off inside companies, and it does not run through the spectacular replacement of entire workforces but through the laborious reworking of processes, through the question of which task a digital team member takes over reliably and which it does not, and through the even harder question of how an organization builds the knowledge that makes this new division of labor possible in the first place. This force is the most consequential of the three, but it is the slowest, and its slowness is not an omission, it is its actual nature.

Just how large the distance between the promise of the technology and its actual effect inside the company turns out to be shows most clearly in the gap between pilot and production. A widely noted study by the NANDA initiative at MIT from July 2025, which carries the fitting title of the great AI divide, found, on the basis of interviews with senior managers, a survey of executives and an analysis of more than 300 publicly documented AI projects, that 95 percent of the organizations examined got no measurable success out of their initiatives and that only about 5 percent of the pilots already integrated had a noticeable effect on revenue.[59] The figure is contested in the sharpness of its claim, and it deserves the same caution as any catchy ratio, but its direction is confirmed by independent sources. McKinsey's large stocktaking from November 2025, which surveyed just under 2,000 specialists and executives, comes to the result that only around a third of companies have begun to take artificial intelligence beyond the experiment and into broad use, while two thirds still have that step ahead of them, and that only around 6 percent of respondents attribute to the technology both at least 5 percent of the operating result and a noticeable contribution to value, while not even four in ten see any effect on the result at all.[60] Two methodologically quite different approaches thus arrive at the same picture, namely that the technology is widely tested but has so far hardly become truly productive anywhere.

Nine months later the continuation of the same survey series is available, and its finding is more revealing for this chapter than any single record number. For the edition of August 25, 2026, a good seventeen hundred participants from 97 countries answered between May 4 and June 8, and adoption has clearly increased this year. 44 percent report that their company is putting the technology into broad use across the entire enterprise, after 38 percent in the previous year, and 56 percent instead of 51 percent use it in three or more functions. The share that attributes any effect at all on the operating result to it still stands at 37 percent and is thus practically unchanged, and the group with at least 5 percent of the result and at the same time a noticeable contribution to value remains at around 6 percent. A year of broader adoption has therefore not narrowed the distance between pilot and production. Its sharpest form appears in the same survey in the difference between the individual person and their company. 80 percent of respondents report that the technology has improved their own productivity, 50 percent that it helps them make better decisions, yet only 37 percent find any of that again in their company's result. What the individual gains does not arrive in the organization. Where it does arrive, one difference stands out that fits everything this chapter has shown so far. Among the 6 percent with a measurable contribution to operating profit, just under three quarters say they rebuilt their workflows from the ground up because of the technology, in the previous year it was 55 percent, among all other respondents it is 25 percent.[3]

> "Among the 6 percent with a measurable contribution to operating profit, just under three quarters rebuilt their workflows from the ground up because of the technology."

What sets the small group with a measurable contribution to operating profit apart from all the others is examined in the same survey across eleven operational practices, and the ranking of the differences is more revealing than any single one of them. The two groups are furthest apart on the rebuilding of workflows, closely followed by the ambition to reshape the whole company instead of only cutting costs, and by the question of whether top management visibly takes responsibility for its initiatives itself, which twice as many of the successful companies report. They are closest together in precisely the place where one would most expect the difference, namely on the question of whether the company has set up a dedicated role with the authority to speed up adoption and clear obstacles out of the way. Such roles exist about equally often in both groups. Granting authority is therefore not enough. The goal setting also turns out differently than expected: the successful companies do not pursue the efficiency goal more often than the others but somewhat less often, 78 versus 82 percent. The difference is that they additionally aim at growth and innovation, 74 versus 47 and 65 versus 49 percent.[3]

> "The successful companies do not pursue the efficiency goal more often than the others, the difference is that they additionally aim at growth and innovation."

**[Figure 5: What the individual gains barely arrives in the organization.]**

BCG paints a friendlier picture of the same situation in the update of its annual survey published at the end of September 2026. According to the consultancy, almost half of all companies now draw benefit from the technology, and the firm sees this as refuting the assumption that it does not pay off inside companies. Measured against its own survey from the previous year, however, the progress turns out more modest. The top group of the most advanced companies grew from 5 to 7.5 percent, the group of companies currently bringing their use into broad operation from 35 to 41 percent, together from 40 to 48.5 percent. The comparison with last year's 5 percent, which the press release suggests and many reports have adopted, sets last year's top group against two groups from this year. More remarkable than the increase is the agreement at the top, because at 7.5 percent the most successful group at BCG lies in the same order of magnitude as the roughly 6 percent with a measurable contribution to operating profit at McKinsey. In addition, benefit at BCG does not mean a measured effect on results but a score above the midpoint of a maturity scale built from respondents' self-assessment across 41 capabilities, and more than half of the companies surveyed report annual revenue above 5 billion dollars. The survey of 1,330 executives from more than 60 countries therefore describes large corporations and their judgment of themselves, and it comes from a consultancy that sells exactly the transformation programs it recommends. For that reason it does not change the finding of this chapter, because adoption keeps growing while the effect reaches only a few.[176]

What the few companies with measurable success had to overcome along the way is described by the Stanford Digital Economy Lab's collection of cases, which was already drawn on in the chapter on the AI argument for the question of staffing. Asked what was hardest to solve, those responsible for the 51 successful deployments most often named change within the company and acceptance among users, which accounted for a third of the answers, followed by the quality and structure of data at 17 percent and the redesign of processes at 10 percent, while the technical hurdles, from systems integration through legal and internal requirements to the choice of vendor, came to 23 percent together. Of the successful projects, 61 percent had at least one failed predecessor, and according to the authors these failures followed a common pattern: the teams had treated artificial intelligence as a technology project and not as an undertaking that changes processes and collaboration. Because the collection selects only successful cases, it says nothing about how frequent success is, and precisely for that reason it does not contradict the findings of MIT and McKinsey but complements them. It describes the profile of those few companies in which the technology shows up in the results, and in this profile the hardest work was not done on the machine. [187]

What the individual actually gains can by now be quantified more precisely than through self-reports, because for the effect on the single task there are two randomized experiments that count as a reference in the academic literature. Shakked Noy and Whitney Zhang of the Massachusetts Institute of Technology had a total of 453 college-educated professionals work on job-related writing tasks of 20 to 30 minutes in 2023, half of them with access to ChatGPT. The time spent fell by 40 percent, the quality as rated by blinded assessors rose by 18 percent, and inequality among the participants decreased, because those who had been weaker before gained the most. The peer-reviewed version appeared in Science.[61] In the same year, Fabrizio Dell'Acqua and Karim Lakhani of Harvard Business School, Ethan Mollick of the Wharton School and their coauthors randomly divided 758 consultants at Boston Consulting Group (bcg.com) into groups with and without access to GPT-4; that was around 7 percent of the consultants without management responsibility at that company. Within the performance frontier of the machine, the consultants with AI completed 12.2 percent more tasks, were 25.1 percent faster and, according to the peer-reviewed version published in Organization Science in 2026, delivered quality that was about a third better; three of the authors worked at the company under study, which the study itself discloses.[9] That is the best available evidence for the gain of the individual, and it shows a time saving of a quarter to two fifths on bounded single tasks of under half an hour, measured with the models of 2023. That is far from a doubling of a person's effectiveness and further still from a doubling of a company's performance. Anyone reading about the 80 percent of McKinsey respondents who report productivity gains of their own has in these two experiments the yardstick for what such a gain means for the individual: a noticeable saving on a partial task that arrives in the operating result only once the organization turns the time gained into other work.

Who actually steers this conversion in day-to-day operations is something the management consultancy Oliver Wyman (oliverwyman.com) asked employees themselves in August 2026. In its survey of almost 12,900 working people in 15 countries, including Germany, France, Italy, Spain and the United Kingdom, 54 percent of workplace AI users say that each person decides for themselves what happens to the time gained, or that nobody decides explicitly. 31 percent name their manager or team, and only 8 percent report a uniform rule set by their company. The costs of the technology are thus managed centrally, as the consultancy itself notes, while its gains are distributed person by person or not at all. This fits the survey that Oliver Wyman conducted at the start of the year together with the New York Stock Exchange among 415 chief executives, more than half of them from North America. Only 50 of them, roughly one in eight, report a cost reduction or revenue increase of more than 10 percent from artificial intelligence, and among these 52 percent put the redesign of roles and processes among their three most important priorities, compared with 33 percent among the rest. Both surveys rest on self-reported information, the consultancy itself points out that its group comparisons do not establish a causal relationship, and with its AI unit it sells precisely the redesign work that its figures suggest. Even so, these figures make the mechanism behind the gap described in this chapter visible: the gain stays with the individual because nobody in the company has charge of it. [190]

> "The gain stays with the individual because nobody in the company has charge of it."

Since the spring of 2026 the distance between pilot and production can moreover, for the first time, not only be asked about but measured on the actual usage data itself. The same Anthropic research note that documents the absent unemployment effect in the chapter on the career gap examines two things for every occupational task, namely whether it could in theory be taken over by language models and whether it is in fact already being taken over in professional use of the vendor's own platform. The distance between the two is enormous. In the most exposed occupational category, the computer and mathematical occupations, 94 percent of tasks would be feasible in theory, 33 percent are actually taken over, and across total employment no use of their activities can be measured at all for three in ten workers. It is also revealing that only this usage measure is related to the independent employment projections of the American labor statisticians, while purely theoretical feasibility on its own shows no relationship whatsoever. It is therefore not what the technology could do that changes work, but that part of it which organizations actually put to use. This confirms the double finding of MIT and McKinsey from an entirely different direction, from usage data instead of surveys, although this measurement too is to be read as the self-observation of a single vendor whose customer base is not a random sample of the economy.[40]

The same vendor supplies a second measurement from usage data with its ongoing Economic Index, which captures the same gap from another side. Since the beginning of 2026 the index has systematically compared how people use the same model in chat and how organizations use it through the programming interface, and in doing so it runs into two separate worlds. Companies use the model three quarters of the time for pure automation, they hand it the task without follow-up questions, and on average the process is over after 5 minutes, while the people in dialogue iterate, adjust and learn. The price of this automation is measurable, because in automated use by companies the estimated success rate is 49 percent, in human dialogue 67, so the task succeeds less often precisely where no one clarifies and corrects any more. If Anthropic weights its own productivity prediction honestly with these success rates, the contribution to American productivity growth calculated from it is halved, from 1.8 to around one percentage point per year. This measurement too is the self-observation of a vendor with all the reservations of the previous paragraph, but on the basis of the usage data it shows what the surveys of MIT and McKinsey report from inside companies. The gap between pilot and production does not arise because the machine can do too little, but because the organization automates away the part of the work on which its success depends, namely the clarifying, adjusting and learning involved in dealing with it.[62]

Since July 23, 2026 this diagnosis no longer rests on the usage data of a single vendor alone. With the AI & Economy ATLAS, Google and Google DeepMind have for the first time measured the use of their own services, almost 15 million anonymized interactions across the Gemini app, the AI mode of search and the programming interface from two weeks of April 2026, accompanied externally by economists such as David Autor and Diane Coyle. The result repeats the pattern with different numbers: Gemini use is observable in 68 percent of all occupations, which together account for a good 88 percent of American employment, yet at the median it touches only 21 percent of the tasks of an occupation, and in only 3 percent of occupations does it reach more than three quarters of the tasks. The direction of use matches as well, because in demanding knowledge work fewer than one in ten conversations aims at handing a task over to the machine completely, the bulk is preparatory work, revision, idea generation and looking things up. The technology is therefore widely tested but used shallowly, and it predominantly accompanies the work instead of replacing it: what the usage data from Claude showed, the data from Gemini shows just the same.[63]

With OpenAI (openai.com), at the end of July 2026 the third large model vendor also presented an ongoing measurement of the use of its own services, and it adds to the picture the question of whose work people are actually handing to the machine. For the first edition of the series Work at the Frontier, OpenAI mapped more than 800,000 work-related messages from American ChatGPT users to the activity catalogs of the official occupational database O*NET. A good 60 percent fall to general work such as emails and summaries, yet of the requests that can be assigned to a discipline, 43.5 percent concern tasks that traditionally belong to another occupation: whoever works in customer service, in design or in human resources predominantly pulls in work from outside their own field on the specialist requests, runs the numbers on financial data, fixes software problems or drafts advertising material. The pattern is most pronounced in the smallest units, because among typical users the out-of-field share in workspaces with two to five licenses is around an eighth higher than in those with more than 100; there, the person who reaches for the machine is the one who has no specialist beside them to delegate to. OpenAI itself draws a remarkably cautious conclusion from this: the technology changes not only how work is done but who does it, while the specialists remain indispensable for judgment and review. This measurement too is the self-observation of a vendor at the level of individual messages, without information on the quality and use of the results and, unlike at Anthropic and Google, without a disclosed analysis period. But it adds the missing dimension to the series of usage data: while use per occupation remains shallow, the tasks are already migrating across the organization. Occupational boundaries are shifting faster than the work is passing to the machine, and this shift too is not an achievement of the technology alone, but a question of how an organization arranges review, responsibility and collaboration around the new possibilities.[10]

In September 2026 the same vendor presented a second edition of this series, and it does not measure the same thing again but something new, namely whether the work from outside one's own field sticks.[196] For more than 1.5 million work-related messages from American accounts in the window from April to July 2026 and for a panel of around 6,200 continuously observed workers, OpenAI counts how often a task from another occupation does not appear for the first time but has already been handed to the machine by the same person before. This share of recurring out-of-field work in the use that can be assigned to a discipline rises within four months from 13.1 to 25.9 percent, and whoever has handed over such a task once returns to it in the following month in 23.6 percent of cases, compared with 8.4 percent in a comparable group without this history. The most persistent are not the technical tasks but those at the customer interface: customer communication recurs in 54 percent of cases, advertising copy in 44, marketing material in 37, legal research by contrast in only 10 percent, against a mean of 18.5 percent. For the question of this chapter, what counts is less the level than the direction. What people hand to the machine does not cross the occupational boundary in a one-off attempt but settles in as a habit, and it does so first where marketing, sales and customer service interlock. Two qualifications belong to this, and OpenAI names the first itself: this is not a representative sample of the American workforce but observed use in a selected group. The second lies in the denominator, because measured against all observed activity, that is, including general work, the same development reads 7.0 to 12.1 percent, and the vendor itself reports this second calculation in its appendix.

The same gap between technical feasibility and actual use can also be documented independently of the vendors themselves, and officially so for Germany. The substitution potential of the Institute for Employment Research has measured at the task level since 2013 what share of the core activities of an occupation could already be performed technically by computers or computer-controlled machines, explicitly as a potential and not as a replacement that has taken place. In 2022, 38 percent of employees subject to social security contributions worked in an occupation with a high substitution potential, compared with 34 percent in 2019, yet how far this potential is actually used remains another question, precisely that distinction on which the Anthropic figures and the gap between pilot and production in this chapter also rest. Just how movable this yardstick is becomes visible in a case from the measurement itself. The training occupation of tax clerk was considered 100 percent automatable by the institute from 2016 onward, because for each of its typical activities a technology could be found that could have taken it over completely. With the new training regulation that came into force in 2023, the same value fell to 50 percent, because the occupation had been described anew, with more support, advice and strategic tasks and less pure bookkeeping. The technology had not fallen behind, the occupation had shifted, and the measurement followed. Occupations rarely disappear entirely, they are rewritten, and whoever reads the automatability of an occupation as a number is always also measuring how old its description is.[64]

What matters is why that is so, because at this point the diagnosis shifts away from the machine. The MIT study explicitly does not attribute the failure to the quality of the models but to a learning gap of the organizations, that is, to the difficulty of fitting the technology cleanly into existing processes and of building the knowledge required for that inside the company.[59] That is the real bottleneck of the third force. The models are ready faster than the organizations that are supposed to absorb them, and the bottleneck lies not in the data center but in the ability of a company to learn how a capable tool becomes a productive digital colleague.

> "The bottleneck lies not in the data center but in the ability of a company to learn how a capable tool becomes a productive digital colleague."

For the distinction between a technology that supports existing work and a technology that organizes work from the ground up, a study by Hyunjin Kim and Rembrand Koning on American AI start-ups supplies a sharper pair of terms, even though it starts from quite different firms than the established corporations of this chapter. It separates a process channel, in which AI tools such as a language model or a programming assistant support the work of existing employees, from a product channel, in which AI capabilities are built directly into the product that is sold, so that the knowledge work migrates into the product itself instead of into an internal team. In their sample, only the product channel predicts the size and the depth of the hierarchy of a firm, while the mere use of AI tools inside the company, widespread as it is, significantly influences neither the one nor the other.[51] Applied to the third force discussed here, this means that the real starting point does not lie in equipping employees with one more tool, but in rebuilding processes and products from the ground up around the capabilities of the technology, exactly that act of learning at which the MIT study sees the organizations failing so far.[59]

That this finding is not an American peculiarity but describes the German economy across its full breadth is shown by a study by IW Consult commissioned by eco, the association of the internet industry, from July 2026, for which 500 companies weighted by size were surveyed. It determines that around 40 percent of the German economy now use artificial intelligence, an increase of 118 percent since 2024 that reaches down into small businesses with fewer than 50 employees, and it captures the German approach in the fitting formula of refinement instead of reinvention. Instead of putting billions into developing their own foundation models, companies take globally available models and adapt them to their specialized processes with their own industry data, that is, exactly that work of adaptation which keeps the knowledge inside the company instead of giving it away to an outside product. More revealing than the boom figure itself, however, is how narrow the top of this movement remains, because only about one company in five already uses the technology actively for innovation, and among the users 46 percent reach exclusively for finished standard solutions, while merely 10 percent really develop the models further in a targeted way and another 44 percent combine both routes. The breadth of use and the depth of its productive anchoring therefore diverge clearly in the domestic figures as well, which confirms the gap between pilot and production once more, this time in the statistics of the German economy itself. That the survey was commissioned by an industry association which explicitly derives from it the call for less regulation belongs to the assessment, but it changes little about the basic direction, because it is consistent with the observations of MIT, of McKinsey and of the Bundesbank.[65]

How independent this organizational decision is of the mere assessment of the technology is shown by a more recent study by Innessa Colaiacovo and Rembrand Koning, the same coauthor as in the study just cited, among just under 200 founders of North American technology start-ups, one of the groups most familiar with artificial intelligence of all, since nine in ten use it personally. By means of a randomized experiment the authors exposed the founders to optimistic or pessimistic information about the capability of the technology in customer service and showed that while the assessment of the technology can be shifted measurably, the willingness to actually hand it work is explained by that, even afterward, only to a good third. Founders who formally integrate artificial intelligence into their workflows differ from those who merely leave it to their staff in passing hardly at all in their assessment of the technology itself, but clearly in their actual delegation behavior and in the staffing effect they attribute to the technology: without it, the one group estimates it would need 91 percent more staff, the other 24 percent. Even in this particularly technology-minded sample, just under a third of the founders moreover state that they have realized no staff savings at all, which confirms the gap between pilot and production from the side of the users themselves and at the same time sharpens it, because by this account the bottleneck lies neither in access to the technology nor in belief in it, but in the independent, laborious decision to actually redivide the work, as the authors themselves sum it up when they write that the effect of artificial intelligence on work and organizations depends not only on what the technology can do, but also on what executives are prepared to do with it.[7]

That this diagnosis is no peculiarity of management consulting but reaches all the way into the central banks is shown by the keynote address "Already Here, Not Yet Everywhere," which German Bundesbank President Joachim Nagel delivered on April 21, 2026, at the International Economic Symposium in Rome, and which captures the whole matter with a phrase from the writer William Gibson, according to which the future is already here, just very unevenly distributed. Applied to artificial intelligence, this means that its capabilities have long been visible in many applications while its macroeconomic effect still barely appears in the aggregate statistics, and for this delay Nagel reaches for the same historical parallel that goes to the core of the third force. When electricity spread through the economies, the productivity gains at first remained small, because the plants did install the electric motors but did not reorganize their production right away, and it was precisely this reorganization, not the availability of the machine, that decided success in the end.[66] The most famous example of this reorganization is Henry Ford's assembly line of 1913. The Highland Park plant was designed from the ground up around electric power available everywhere, because only that made it possible to arrange the machines in the order of the work steps and to drive the moving belt itself, whereas a classic steam factory had to group its machines around the central transmission shaft and its belts. Economic historians such as Warren Devine and David Nye record that mass production of the Model T would not have been practicable without electricity.[67] Volume production of the automobile was therefore not a consequence of the new machine alone, but of the newly ordered work around it. It is the same learning gap that MIT observes at the project level, now seen from the altitude of monetary policy.[59]

> "It is not the availability of AI that decides success, but the reorganization of work around it."

For the German-speaking countries the same Nagel speech supplies the concrete confirmation, because it draws on the Bundesbank's own company survey. By that account most German firms do expect productivity gains from generative artificial intelligence, yet the intensity of actual use continues to rise only at a moderate pace, which suggests that many companies are still experimenting rather than moving the technology into day-to-day operations. The accompanying data in the Bundesbank monthly report of March 2026 put figures to this picture, because while the share of German firms that use generative AI tools is high by European comparison, the share of working time in which they are really put to use grows only slowly from year to year. The gap between pilot and production thus no longer appears only in American and global surveys, but in the figures of the German economy itself, and the third force loses the appearance of a distant phenomenon that concerns only the front pages of Silicon Valley.[66]

> "While the share of German firms that use generative AI tools is high by European comparison, the share of working time in which they are really put to use grows only slowly from year to year."

Just how concretely this gap shows up in a single, particularly regulated industry is documented by a study from Wavestone (wavestone.com) and the Institute of Information Systems at the University of St. Gallen, which between October 2025 and May 2026 surveyed a total of 30 business and IT leaders from banks, insurers and technology vendors in the German-speaking countries. The finding is the sharpest in this chapter so far, and it even has a face. The most advanced interviewee heads the central AI lab of a large insurer, already runs 75 productive use cases there in classic machine learning and generative AI, among them an application that searches some half a million court rulings, and has still not brought a single agentic use case into production. He himself rates his institution at only a three on a maturity scale of ten, and in that shows the distance between an institution that operates AI and one that lets itself be changed by it. The study sums up the result itself in one sentence that confirms this report's central thesis from an entirely different industry: it is not the capability of the models but the maturity of the operating model that decides how far a company can actually scale agentic AI. Regulation sharpens this further, as the AI lead of a German bank discloses: "We process personal and highly sensitive data here. Technically that is not the problem, but the regulatory effort, the AI Act, the GDPR, DORA, those are the biggest constraints." As an organizational answer, a central-decentral model is winning out at most of the institutions surveyed, a lean central AI unit for governance and shared capabilities, coupled with business-specific use cases in the individual divisions. Part of the context is that Wavestone, as a consulting firm, takes its share of exactly the transformation it examines here, while the academic involvement of the University of St. Gallen secures the study's independence from individual vendors.[68]

> "The insurer runs 75 productive use cases, among them an application that searches half a million court rulings, and still has not a single agentic use case in production."

At a level that is otherwise missing from the consultancies' surveys, the public sector, the Capgemini Research Institute (capgemini.com) supplies the only figure so far for the same gap. For its report "Data and AI in Government 2026", the institute surveyed 600 executives of public sector organizations in 16 countries between March and April 2026, more than half of them in Europe. Only 28 percent of them report having moved artificial intelligence from pilot to full-scale production. Independently of that, 70 percent say that operating, monitoring and refining the systems has required more effort than expected. Classical machine learning has come furthest, deployed across most or all functions by 21 percent; generative AI is deployed that widely by 3 percent, and agentic systems are at the very beginning. For context, these are self-assessments by executives of their own organizations, "successfully scaled" is not an externally verified measure, and the publisher sells precisely the services the report identifies as the bottleneck. As a third, independently collected finding pointing in the same direction alongside the Bundesbank and the St. Gallen study, the figure still earns its place, because it shows that the gap between trial and operation does not depend on the industry but on the organization, and public administration is no exception.[172]

The same gap between trial and operation shows up across the breadth of small and medium sized enterprises, for which there are only a few measurement series in the German-speaking region. Since 2024 the Swiss SME labor market study by AXA and Sotomo has asked not only whether companies with five to 250 employees use artificial intelligence, but how. In March 2026, 35 percent integrated the technology deliberately into their processes, 13 percent in all areas of the company, 39 percent were trying it out in individual projects or on the employees' own initiative, and 26 percent did not use it. Trial and use together jumped from 55 percent in 2024 to 71 percent in 2025 and stand at 74 in 2026; the jump is over, the plateau is reached. More revealing is the series of fields of use. In five of eight fields the 2026 value lies below that of 2025: translations fall from 52 to 47 percent, correspondence from 47 to 42, personalized advertising from 24 to 16, while the optimization of work steps holds at 35 percent and data analysis keeps growing from 32 to 34 percent. The text tasks with which the first use began reached their peak in 2025; what remains are the applications in the workflows. The study itself compares only 2024 with 2026 and calls that stagnation, and so the peak of 2025 disappears from its own presentation. The size axis runs continuously all the way down to the small firm: 23 percent of companies with five to nine employees see a need for action on their own business model, 38 percent of medium sized ones and 42 percent of firms with 50 or more employees, and 33 percent of the small ones rate the technology as an opportunity against 49 percent of the medium sized ones. Behind each size class stand only around 100 respondents, and micro firms with fewer than five employees are missing entirely.[135]

> "With personal data the problem is not the technology but the regulatory effort from the AI Act, the GDPR and DORA, says the AI lead of a German bank."

That this gap can be closed, and what it actually costs an organization to close it, has been documented since July 2026 by, of all things, a government agency. The US State Department, with its Generative AI Playbook, has laid open the full path of its internal assistant StateChat, from the first data strategy to operation with more than 62,000 users at 98 percent of its diplomatic posts worldwide, out of a workforce of some 80,000 people. What is remarkable about this document is less the success than the accounting of the effort behind it. From the build-out of its own analytics center in 2019 through the data strategy of 2021 to the preparation of agentic systems in the spring of 2026, seven years passed, the security clearance for sensitive data alone cost another year of procedural work, and the department describes precisely that not as a brake but as a precondition of the benefit, because without access to the real working data the tool would have remained worthless, a remarkable counterposition to the complaint about regulatory effort that the previous paragraph quotes from a German bank. The playbook devotes only a fraction of its pages to the technology; the rest is given over to adoption in everyday work, 94 open office hours with some 17,000 participants, more than 430 training sessions with more than 48,000 people trained on the train-the-trainer principle, and, as the single most effective measure, explicit permission from above, because the most common inhibition among staff was uncertainty about whether they were allowed to use the tool for their work at all. After the leadership of one regional bureau personally invited their people, the number of weekly active users there rose by 367 percent.[69]

The successes the department measures after a year of operation are modest, and credible for exactly that reason. Nine out of ten regular users report time savings, on average 1.6 hours per week and task, most often when drafting emails and memos, and among those who gain no time the internal survey names as one main reason that checking the machine's results takes longer than doing the work themselves would have taken, that is, the supervisory work this chapter will come back to, documented here for the first time from inside an organization with 80,000 employees. All of this is to be read as the self-presentation of an agency that wants to write itself to the front of the American administration, based on a voluntary survey without independent productivity measurement, and what becomes of the freed-up hours structurally is left open by the document. But as a publicly viewable record of what the long rebuild really costs a single organization, it is so far without counterpart, and its finding agrees with every measurement in this chapter, because at no point was the bottleneck the model, but the organization that first had to be built around the model.[69]

A global survey by the software vendor Notion (notion.com), which in the spring of 2026 covered more than 6,000 decision makers and users in 16 markets, confirms the same diagnosis once more, this time not on the profit and loss statement but on the maturity of the organizations. 88 percent of organizations still use the technology where it serves as an individual tool for drafts, summaries and first ideas, and only 12 percent have already built it into recurring workflows or let it run autonomously. More telling than this distribution is what changes as maturity grows. The share of decision makers who concede that their organization is investing in the technology faster than its own workforce can keep up with it grows with increasing maturity from just under half to more than two thirds. The learning gap therefore does not close the further an organization advances; it opens wider, and thus it is confirmed from a third, independent side that the real bottleneck does not lie in the availability of the technology but in the pace at which an organization builds the knowledge needed to use it. Part of the context is that Notion itself sells AI-supported work software and has its own interest in presenting deeper system integration as the decisive leap in maturity, but the direction of the finding agrees independently with the MIT and the McKinsey figure as well as with the Bundesbank observation.[70]

> "The decision makers confirm the gap not on the profit and loss statement, but in the maturity of their own organization."

> "88 percent of organizations use the technology only as a tool for drafts, summaries and first ideas, only 12 percent have built it into recurring workflows."

**[Figure 6: The more mature the organization, the more often learning lags behind.]**

The learning gap that Notion describes on the side of decision makers has a second half, and it lies with the employees themselves. Since August 2026 there has been a measurement that asks not about intentions but about actual participation. Annemarie Künn-Nelen, Raymond Montizaan and Anna-Lena Wittich of the Research Centre for Education and the Labour Market at Maastricht University linked 4,173 observations of 3,166 Dutch workers from 2017 and 2020 to the OECD's occupational automation index and related both to whether someone had taken part in training in the preceding two years. What the literature has long described as the training gap is confirmed here with full force: those who work in a more automatable occupation take part 17 percentage points less often than the rest, 41 against 63 percent, and this gap holds up in every control calculation. More revealing is what stands beside it. Only 31 percent of respondents consider their own work technologically changeable at all, while 51 percent work in occupations above the median, and the two measures are negatively related. 39 percent work in a more automatable occupation and deny the change for themselves; only 12 percent see both at once.[150]

It is precisely this self-assessment that decides how large the gap turns out to be. Among the more automatable occupations, 38 percent of those who see no technological change in their own work take part in training, against 51 percent of those who do. Among the less automatable occupations the same assessment goes along with hardly any difference, 61 against 65 percent. The gap does not close, then, but it shrinks markedly where the workforce and the occupational statistics see the same thing. Where this association appears is shown by splitting by funding and initiative: for self-funded training, which makes up only a small share, it cannot be detected, but it is clear for training funded and initiated by the employer. The training gap can therefore no longer be attributed to companies alone, without the responsibility thereby passing to the employees, because it is largest where the two sides do not see the same situation. This is to be read with limitations that do not weaken the finding but mark out its scope: the analysis describes associations and not causes, it rests on Dutch data from 2017 and 2020 that measure automation in the older sense, and more automatable here means above the median of the occupational distribution and not at risk, because not a single occupation in this sample reaches the threshold at which the OECD itself speaks of high risk. The authors expressly refuse to call the employees' assessment a misperception, because it remains open whether the occupational statistics know a job better than the person who does it. That very restraint is the reason the finding stands here: not as evidence that employees are wrong, but that training takes place where two assessments meet.[150]

How the learning gap is by now arriving in the executive suites themselves is shown by the third annual AI at Work survey from the employment services provider G-P (globalization-partners.com), for which the field institute Wakefield Research (wakefieldresearch.com) surveyed a total of 2,850 executives from vice president level upward in six markets in March 2026, among them Germany. Without exception, all respondents now use the technology, yet 73 percent concede that the success of their AI spending has fallen short of expectations, the share of those who describe their own approach as an offensive innovation strategy fell within one year from 60 to 42 percent, and just under 70 percent announce that they will cut AI budgets should the company targets for 2026 be missed, in Germany at 58 percent the most reserved figure, but still a majority. What is new about this year's edition is therefore less the diagnosis, which agrees with all the findings gathered so far, than the deadline it sets for it, because the patience that the long rebuild by its nature demands is being limited, where the budgets are approved, to a single year, and with that the slowest of the three forces comes under a beat that contradicts its nature. That these are self-reports from a vendor survey belongs to the context, because G-P, as a provider that formally employs staff on behalf of other companies, sells cross-border employment together with its own AI agent and advertises both in the same report, yet precisely because this business lives on belief in the technology, the documented disillusionment weighs more heavily rather than less. For the question of this chapter that means the gap between pilot and production has arrived in the profit and loss statement and is no longer read there as a maturing phenomenon that is given time, but as a bill that has to be settled within a single fiscal year.[71]

> "Just under 70 percent of executives announce that they will cut AI budgets should the company targets for 2026 be missed."

> "The gap between pilot and production has arrived in the profit and loss statement, and there it is no longer read as a maturing phase, but as a bill that has to be settled within a single fiscal year."

How this impatience squares with the actual movement of companies is shown by the most recent large-scale measurement to date, the second edition of the quarterly Global AI Pulse from the consulting firm KPMG (kpmg.com), which between the end of April and the end of May 2026 surveyed a total of 2,145 executives in 20 countries. The share of companies that anchor the technology broadly in their own operations has nearly doubled within a single quarter, from 13 to 22 percent, the largest movement the survey has measured anywhere on its maturity curve so far, and at the same time the share of those that can show an established, demonstrable success fell slightly from 8 to 7 percent. The gap between pilot and production therefore widens at exactly the moment when three quarters of respondents are convinced that the technology already delivers measurable value to their company, and 79 percent state that they would hold on to that priority even in a recession. The companies' answer to this tension is not retreat but management, because the planned budgets stay stable while almost half of respondents have already re-timed the rollout of their AI agents as soon as the costs began to exceed the expected benefit. More telling than the gap itself is what distinguishes the companies with demonstrated success from the rest, because it is not the level of spending but the order behind it. Where responsibility for the results of the technology is clearly named, an established success is reported more than three times as often, 14 versus 4 percent, and where an organization has full visibility of its AI running costs, five times as often, 15 versus 3 percent, while three quarters do say their CEO drives the topic as a strategic priority, but only a quarter actually locate ultimate responsibility at the top. That these figures come from a consulting firm that earns its money closing exactly this gap belongs to the context, but the finding adds something to this chapter's diagnosis that the earlier snapshots could not provide, namely the proof over time that the gap is not a question of the technology or of the money, but of the organization, which has yet to move responsibility and cost visibility into its structures.[72]

> "The gap between pilot and production widens at exactly the moment when three quarters of respondents are convinced that the technology already delivers measurable value."

What this order looks like in operation was publicly demonstrated in the summer of 2026 by a corporation that three months earlier was still the counterexample. The ride-hailing company Uber (uber.com) had used up its entire AI budget for 2026 after just four months, and President and Chief Operating Officer Andrew Macdonald conceded in May that the connection between this spending and the features actually shipped could not be established so far.[73] In the quarterly report of August 5, 2026, Chief Financial Officer Balaji Krishnamurthy then reported that the cost per token had fallen over the past months while usage had continued to increase, so that total spending on artificial intelligence remained largely stable.[74] Chief Technology Officer Praveen Neppalli Naga disclosed on the same day how that had been achieved, and none of it concerns the machine: a higher hit rate in the cache for recurring requests, changed defaults for model choice and context length, the routing of simpler tasks to cheaper and open models and, most tellingly, a display that shows every developer their own AI costs per hour in real time. Efficiency, as he explained it, had been treated as an engineering task and not as a budget question.[75] What tipped here within a single year at the same company was therefore not the technology and not the size of the budget, but cost visibility, and at this point the digital workforce visibly gets, for the first time, what every human one has long had, an hourly rate.

What this case does not deliver belongs to the context, and it is exactly the quantity Macdonald was concerned with in May. What fell was the price per unit, and it fell in the market over the same period as well, because the vendors cut their list prices and open models moved up, so that from the outside it cannot be separated which part of the decline goes back to the company's own engineering work and which to the market; the company names no amounts. What is answered is the question of the costs, not the question of the success. It is telling which metric the chief financial officer cites for the benefit, namely a doubling of code output per developer, precisely the quantity this report rejects in the chapter on the technological substructure as evidence of output and not of value.[76] Amazon shows how hard it is to let go of such quantities. The role guideline the company published for its software engineers on October 27, 2026 warns against turning token consumption, tool usage or code changes into targets because that drives up cost and complexity, yet considers these metrics closely linked to outcomes for now and allows them to be used with care in performance reviews.[198] Of the three tasks that belong to the cost accounting of a digital workforce, measuring the costs, attributing the benefit and proving the contribution to operating profit, the first is thus solved and the other two are open. That is no small step, because without the first the other two cannot be had, but it is not the proof of benefit it is currently taken for.

How far the distance between technical maturity and operational effect reaches can be tested most precisely where the replacement was announced earliest and loudest. In 2016 Geoffrey Hinton recommended stopping the training of radiologists, because within five years it would be obvious that machine learning reads images better than humans do. Ten years later the official French health statistics keep the books on what came of it. The number of radiologists and nuclear medicine physicians in France rose between 2014 and 2024 from 9,100 to 10,200, an increase of 13 percent, that of radiology technologists from 29,000 to 32,000, and for their recruitment the same statistics explicitly record shortages for 2024. The number of outpatient imaging centers grew over the same period from 860 to 1,070, spending on their services rose in 2024 by 7 percent to 5.5 billion euros, carried by volume growth of 8 percent with falling prices. As the cause of this demand the agency names not the technology but the aging of the population, and artificial intelligence appears in the same account exclusively as an expectation for the coming years, as one of those medical fields in which it could unfold and bring productivity gains. In the discipline, then, whose data suit the machine best, the replacement has not occurred a decade after it was announced, and the official statistics still carry it in the conditional. [77]

Where the technology actually arrives in operations, it so far relieves rather than replaces. A before-and-after study by the Charité in Berlin in German long-term care shows that an AI voice assistant cuts documentation time per early shift by 15 minutes, or some 28 percent. The technology therefore takes hold exactly where the burden is greatest, without making a single care worker redundant; the study, however, has no control group, and its lead author works for the maker of the product under study.[78] At the same time, use is distributed highly unevenly. In Anthropic's usage data, some one third of workers account for no measurable use at all,[40] and even where Google's analysis of Gemini usage observes the technology reaching into the workshop, for instance with mechanics who interpret test results and upload images of faults, it captures only a narrow slice of the occupation.[63] The third force therefore works narrowly so far, more augmenting than replacing and very unevenly, and that is almost the opposite of the across-the-board displacement the headline claims.

<a id="WAAI-2026-C09.1"></a>
### Relief on the individual task has a flip side in the working day, and there the workforces pay for their organizations' learning gap

How fragmented the working day has become was measured by Microsoft in June 2025 on the basis of the anonymized usage data of its office software, which gave the finding the telling name of the infinite workday. By that account an employee is interrupted during core working hours on average every 2 minutes by a meeting, an email or a chat message, for the most heavily burdened fifth of users this adds up to some 275 interruptions a day, meetings after 8 p.m. increased by 16 percent within one year, and in the accompanying survey of 31,000 knowledge workers in 31 countries almost half of the employees and more than half of the managers describe their own work as chaotic and fragmented, while a third say the pace of work of the last five years is simply no longer manageable. With its AI assistants Microsoft sells at the same time the remedy meant to lead out of this situation, so the figures are to be read as the diagnosis of a vendor with its own interest, yet the most remarkable sentence of the report turns against its own sales logic, because it warns that without a newly ordered rhythm of work, artificial intelligence threatens merely to accelerate a broken system.[79]

> "During core working hours an employee is interrupted on average every 2 minutes, and without a newly ordered working day artificial intelligence only speeds up this broken system."

Why this intensification appears in no productivity statistic is explained by a laboratory experiment that the computer scientist Gloria Mark, the leading researcher on the interruption of knowledge work, published together with colleagues from Humboldt University as early as 2008. Test subjects who were interrupted constantly did not complete their tasks more slowly or worse, but faster, because people compensate for expected disturbances with a higher working pace, and they paid for it with significantly more stress, frustration, time pressure and effort, measurable after just 20 minutes of interrupted work. In business terms the calculation therefore appears to work out, while its costs migrate entirely into the people, and that is exactly why the intensification stays invisible until it becomes visible as exhaustion.[79]

> "The intensification stays invisible until it becomes visible as exhaustion."

The long rebuild adds a new layer to this old mechanism, because the digital team member does not only take work off people's hands, it also demands supervision. Anyone who directs several AI agents in parallel, checks their interim results, corrects their misfires and coordinates their handovers is no longer working at their own rhythm but at the rhythm of the machines, and Gartner describes precisely this role in its picture of small teams, where every team member carries, alongside their own specialty, the supervision of AI agents.[53]

> "The digital team member does not only take work off people's hands, it also demands supervision."

How widely this supervisory work has already arrived is shown by the G-P survey already cited on the budget caveat, in which 69 percent of executives state that they now spend more time monitoring unchecked AI work and reworking it like editors, which is why the report simply declares 2026 the year of the human editor.[71] Orchestration thus becomes the new form of multitasking, and it meets workforces that are in part already working at their limit. The sentiment reading from the survey of tech employees cited in the chapter on the first force fits in here, because the share of those at least moderately burned out jumped within a single year from 44.7 to 55.7 percent, and what the respondents fear is not replacement but the pace, the worry of landing in a rhythm that cannot be sustained, while every gain in productivity immediately becomes the new benchmark of expectation.[28] For the diagnosis of this chapter that means that the learning gap is not only a gap in success, but a gap in strain. An organization that invests faster than it learns does not pass the difference on to the machine but to its people, and whether the time freed up is translated into relief or into intensified expectation is the same leadership decision that this chapter already knows from the delegation question.

> "Orchestration becomes the new form of multitasking, and it meets workforces that are in part already working at their limit."

What this supervision costs can by now be stated in minutes. In Oliver Wyman's survey of August 2026, workplace AI users estimate that of every hour they work with the help of the technology, they spend 22 minutes on average checking, correcting or redoing its output. These are the respondents' own estimates, and the consultancy explicitly leaves open how much of this would have arisen without the machine as well. All the same, the checking is not thorough. Only 17 percent of respondents always verify important information from AI against another source, and 42 percent report that wrong information caused a real problem in the past year, minor problems included. The human at the checkpoint is thus expensive and patchy at the same time, and Oliver Wyman concludes that oversight of systems acting in large numbers can no longer rest on a person in front of every single decision. [190]

How this supervision is distributed once the machine writes a large share of the code shows up first in software development, and looking there pays off even for companies that write no software themselves. Google states that 75 percent of new code in the group is now machine-generated and approved by developers, and Anthropic puts the share in its own case at more than 80 percent; both sell the tools, and both figures are self-reported and counted in different ways.[151] Researchers at Stanford University and Carnegie Mellon University studied a company that had committed itself to doubling the number of merged changes per developer. Across 802 developers and 196,212 change requests between January 2024 and April 2026, the load per reviewer there roughly doubled and machine review overtook human review, while the rates of accepted and reverted changes held steady.[152] In a self-selected survey of 415 software practitioners by researchers at Oregon State University and two other universities, 84.3 percent of heavy users report that their review time has not become shorter.[153] Largely the same group from Stanford and Carnegie Mellon finds across 441 open-source projects that 73.8 percent of the configuration files in which teams give their coding agents rules are created once and never changed again; supervision of the agents thus often exists only on paper.[154] In these cases the bottleneck is no longer writing but reviewing, and the company studied resolves it by increasingly leaving review to the machine as well. What this could mean for the people who are supposed to review in future is suggested, of all things, by an experiment from Anthropic's Fellows Program. In the preregistered experiment with 52 developers learning a programming library unknown to them, the group with an AI assistant scored, according to the authors, 17 percent worse on the subsequent test without help, without having been significantly faster. In an analysis that was not preregistered, the gap was largest in debugging, and the authors see this as a sign that precisely the skill needed to review machine-written code could suffer.[155] None of these studies is peer reviewed, the company figures are self-reports, and apart from the experiment the studies rest on a single case, on a survey or on observation. They nevertheless suggest that this field shows earlier than others where work is shifting, and this is why this report treats software development as a leading indicator for the other functions.

What such a solution looks like in a large operation was described by Meta itself in June 2026, in a paper by thirty-one of the company's employees. By their account, the number of lines of code per change submitted by humans, by Meta's own measure, rose by 106 percent within a year and the number of changes per developer and month by 51 percent; Meta attributes more than four fifths of the growth in changes to AI agents, and the share of changes reviewed within a day declined. The answer goes by the name RADAR, a multi-stage review process made up of a risk model, a review by a language model and automated tests, which by the time of publication had reviewed more than 535,000 changes and merged more than 331,000 of them into the code without human review. What is instructive here is less the technology than the dial. How much goes through without humans is set by each organizational unit in its own risk rule, for changes by humans the safest five percent, for proven automated sources the safest fifty, and when Meta raised the threshold for the automated sources from the safest 25 to the safest 50 percent, the share of approved changes rose to 60 percent. That the changes selected this way are reverted less often and cause fewer incidents than the rest is evidence not of the quality of the machine but of the quality of the preselection, and the authors concede as much themselves. They also name the price that this report follows in the chapter on the career gap: review by colleagues used to be the place where knowledge was passed on within the company, and the more of it the machine takes over, the less of that remains.[179]

Alongside the gap in strain a second one opens, and it hits the capabilities of the workforce directly. So far this chapter has described the learning gap as the failure to build new capabilities in time. Now the reverse case is added, that a company gradually loses capabilities it has long possessed. The consulting firm BCG calls this distributed skill loss and means by it not that a single person is slipping, but that an entire workforce loses its judgment at the same time, as soon as everyone works with the same tools and on the same data basis. What is revealing is which capabilities the executives surveyed see as most at risk, because they are not the routine steps that are easy to hand over anyway, but the most demanding ones, the sense of which problem is to be solved in the first place, and the judgment of how to decide, that is, precisely those capabilities that a machine imitates effortlessly and truly replaces only with difficulty. The path there is inconspicuous and works for exactly that reason, because since the digital team member delivers usable results quickly enough, one gets used to accepting them as good enough and skips the step that used to be the actual work, the examination of the reasoning behind them. Anyone who stops questioning unlearns it, the way a muscle wastes away when it is no longer needed.[56]

> "Now the reverse case is added, that a company gradually loses capabilities it has long possessed."

> "Anyone who stops questioning unlearns it, like a muscle that is no longer needed."

This finding has to be read with care, because it rests on the assessment of 70 executives and comes from a company that makes its consulting business out of this very problem, and the research on so-called cognitive debt, on thinking weakened by outsourcing, is also still in its early stages and is contested.[56] Since the beginning of the year, however, the first larger experimental measurement of it has been available. Two behavioral researchers at the Wharton School, Steven Shaw and Gideon Nave, had 1,372 test subjects solve reasoning tasks in three experiments and left it to them whether to consult an AI, nearly 10,000 decisions in total. More than every second task was delegated to the machine, and the direction of the effect depended entirely on whether the machine was right. With correct AI answers the hit rate rose by 25 percentage points, with wrong ones it fell by 15 points below the level of those who worked without AI at all. The most troubling part was the self-confidence, because it grew in both cases, with correct as well as with wrong answers from the machine. The researchers call this reflex cognitive surrender, the adoption of a machine judgment without one's own examination, and they observe it the more often the greater the trust in the technology is, while pronounced analytical thinking protects against it.[8] The BCG finding itself nevertheless carries little weight as a measurement, and all the more as the self-observation of an occupational group,[56] because the slackening judgment does not stay with the individual. Where everyone works with the same systems, the results converge, similar answers, similar phrasing, similar conclusions, and the more similar they become, the less reason remains to ask a colleague's opinion at all, while the pace that demands everything at once takes the last of the time away from discussion. At the same time responsibility shifts, because if a result comes from artificial intelligence, it becomes easier not to regard it as one's own any more, and even experienced people replace their judgment with that of the machine out of habit and under time pressure, without anyone noticing the slippage in them. For this chapter it follows that the capability of a company is nothing fixed that one has once and then keeps, but something that persists only in use, and whether the technology sharpens it or hollows it out does not depend on the technology itself but on the same leadership that already decides about delegation and intensification.

> "The most troubling part was that the self-confidence grew in both cases, with correct as well as with wrong AI answers. Cognitive surrender is what the researchers call the adoption of an AI judgment without one's own examination."

That this capitulation happens not only in the laboratory but in everyday working life was shown by the field experiment at the Boston Consulting Group that this chapter cited at the outset for the gain inside the performance frontier, because the same experiment contained a second task deliberately placed outside that frontier. The consultants were to combine a data analysis with qualitative evidence from interviews, and the machine answered plausibly but wrongly. The group with AI access delivered a correct solution 19 percentage points less often than the group without. The same machine that had made the consultants a quarter faster on the first task made them worse than without the tool on the second, and from the outside there was no telling on which side of the frontier the two tasks lay.
