The authors speak of a jagged performance frontier of the technology.[9] For the long rebuild this is the experimental evidence for the skipped step of examination. The loss does not arise because the machine is unusable, but because the person stops checking as soon as it has been of use to them once. An organization that books the gain inside the frontier buys the loss beyond the frontier along with it, as long as it does not teach its people where the frontier runs, and it shifts with every new model.

That this mechanism concerns not only the individual decision but the building of capability itself was shown by a preregistered field experiment in the year before, and its design brings the decisive question to the point. Hamsa Bastani, Osbert Bastani and their team at the University of Pennsylvania had nearly 1,000 students in grades nine through eleven at a large high school in Turkey practice mathematics in four sessions. Every session followed the same sequence, a review by the teacher, a practice phase and a concluding test without any aids, and the only thing varied was the practice phase. One group received a tool that behaved like an ordinary chatbot, a second the same model with an instruction written by teachers that withheld solutions and gave graduated hints instead, a third worked with the usual materials. In the practice both groups with machine assistance were far ahead, the first by 48 and the second by 127 percent. In the test without aids the first group fell 17 percent below the control group, while the second was statistically indistinguishable from it. The same model thus led to opposite results depending on how it was used, and the conversation transcripts show why, because those who could get the solution asked for it and copied it down without making an attempt of their own. For a company the second finding may weigh more heavily than the first, because neither of the two groups recognized its own situation. The one that performed worse did not consider itself worse, and the other, which did not perform better at all, considered itself distinctly better.[80]

What laboratory and self-report can only suggest was tested for the first time in June 2026 by a measurement of ordinary use over years, and it comes not from paid work but from school. David Strömberg of the University of Stockholm together with Victor Lei and Yanhui Wu of the University of Hong Kong followed 26,811 Chinese students in grades seven through twelve over 30 months and made use of the fact that generative artificial intelligence arrived at the participating schools at staggered times, which makes it possible to compare groups with and without access. Homework performance rose by 18 percent, while the time spent on it fell by 30 percent. The results of the monthly tests without aids fell by 20 percent within half a year, and in the large entrance examinations on which the further educational path depends in China, the losses came to 18 and 24 percent, with the full effect only showing after about two years. What is decisive for this chapter, however, is not the size of the loss but its distribution, because it sits almost entirely with that group of around 80 percent of users whose strikingly short working time combined with high homework marks points to an outsourcing of the work rather than to working with the tool. Those who kept up their working time and used the machine alongside it lost almost nothing. It is not the tool that produces the loss, but the giving up of the path to the result.[55]

Transferring this to the company is interpretation and not a measured result, because what was surveyed was a school system in a single Chinese county, and the work is available as a discussion paper and has not been peer reviewed. The mechanism, however, is the same one that this chapter describes for the workforce, and the school supplies what the company has so far lacked, namely a test without aids. A company measures its AI success by what in this study is the homework mark, that is, by the finished result and the time needed for it, and both quantities pointed upward here over months while the underlying capability was already giving way. Anyone who takes this delay seriously reads their own adoption figures differently, because a company does not get its examination day monthly, but only when a case arises for which the tool has no template.[55]

How far this pattern reaches beyond a single Chinese county can be read since September 2026 from the largest school assessment in the world, even though it cannot establish a cause. In the PISA 2025 survey, in which more than 760,000 fifteen-year-olds in 91 countries and economies took part, the OECD asked for the first time how often young people use AI chatbots for school, separately for four purposes. On average across OECD countries, the highest science scores are reached by those who never have texts summarized or drafted for them, and after accounting for socio-economic background they are 29 to 32 points ahead of those who do so daily. Learning with the machine shows a different picture, because those who use it for this once or twice a week perform on par with non-users, while rare and daily users fall behind. Within the German-speaking region, Germany shows the clearest gap. Students there who regularly have texts summarized or homework drafted score 22 to 23 points below their peers, before and after accounting for background, while in Austria the gap is at most 12 points and in Switzerland up to 18. Because use and performance were measured in the same survey at the same time, it is impossible to separate whether the machine weakens ability or whether weaker students reach for it more often. An independent analysis of the same data by Ignacio Lepe of Yale University and Dante Contreras of the Universidad de Chile narrows the question, however. Holding total use fixed, drafting and summarizing go with lower scores and research and help with learning with higher ones, and this also holds when students are compared with schoolmates at the same school. If only weaker students reached for the machine more often, all four purposes would have to carry the same sign. The worldwide survey therefore cannot prove a cause, but it shows at scale the same pattern as the field experiments in Turkey and China: what matters is not how often the machine is used, but whether it takes over the work or supports the person doing it.[177][178]

The BCG finding and the Wharton School experiment describe what happens to human judgment after the machine has answered. How much is already decided beforehand, namely with the question itself, has been measured cleanly for the first time by a not yet peer reviewed paper by Taha Choukhmane, Tim de Silva, Weidong Lin and Matthew Akuzawa of the Massachusetts Institute of Technology and Stanford University, albeit in an entirely different field, namely private financial advice. A thousand representatively selected Americans wrote their own questions there to an AI adviser, and the researchers calculated the advice given through an entire working life. The machine's advice was by and large good, it would have brought most respondents closer to what the textbooks recommend. But it turned out differently depending on the person asking, and in order to clarify why, the researchers randomly inserted into those questions that do not state the gender of their own accord the sentence that one is a man or a woman. This made it possible to separate what goes back to the question from what goes back to the model: two thirds of the difference in the recommended equity share arose from people asking differently, one third from the machine answering the same question differently.[81]

The finding comes from the financial planning of private households and not from paid work, and transferring it to the company is therefore interpretation and not a measured result. It is nevertheless close at hand, because what the authors record at the end of their paper is the diagnosis of this chapter in different language: the demand side will limit the quality of what people receive even as the models get better. A company that gives everyone the same license and then looks at very different results does not have a model problem, it is watching its own learning gap at work.

The official business survey by the Census Bureau, which in the chapter on the AI argument makes the employment effect come out so small, also measures the depth of the anchoring, and it thereby quantifies the distance between pilot and production more precisely than was previously possible. 57 percent of the companies that use artificial intelligence do so in at most three of their business functions, 65 percent limit their employees' use to at most three types of task, and a typology of users shows that with 37 percent the largest group consists of minimalists, while the technology is used comprehensively across all functions in a full 4 percent of cases, that is, in barely four out of a hundred companies. Even more revealing than this narrowness is how unevenly timed the adoption is. In 36 percent of the companies in which employees work with artificial intelligence there is no formal adoption at the company level at all, so the use has grown from below and past the leadership. Conversely, 19 percent of companies with a formal adoption show no discernible use in their employees' tasks, there the decision has been taken and has got stuck in the implementation. Seeing both at once is the real gain of this survey, because on that basis the spread within a company is not a single uniform process, but consists of two movements independent of one another that can fail to meet. [82]

The findings of this chapter keep showing the same picture. The distance between what technology is bought and tried out and what of it actually works in daily operation shows up in the official statistics as well as in the surveys and in the vendors' usage data. Precisely this distance between pilot and production is the quantity that the report will measure year after year in a metric of its own from the 2027 edition on, because it says more about the true state of things than any figure about models or investment sums. How an organization closes this distance, that is, how it builds the knowledge that lifts artificial intelligence out of the trial and into ongoing operation, is the question this report comes down to in the end. At this point the finding suffices that the most consequential of the three forces is so far not failing because of the technology, but because of the organizations that still have to learn how to work with it.

<a id="WAAI-2026-C09.2"></a>
### Looking ahead

The long rebuild has a historical script, and its decisive scene is still to come. With electrification the jump in productivity did not come when the factories replaced their steam engines, but only when a new generation of plants was built around the electric motor, with different floor plans, different processes and a different division of labor. The economic historian Paul David of Stanford University measured this script in 1990 in his essay "The Dynamo and the Computer", which went on to become famous, at a time when computers were producing exactly the same disappointment as artificial intelligence does today. To the objection of the Nobel laureate Robert Solow that one could see the computer age everywhere except in the productivity statistics, David answered with a look back: around 1900 an observer could have said the same about the dynamos. In 1899 electric motors supplied less than 5 percent of the driving power of American factories, only a good two decades later was half reached, and the measurable boost to productivity came only in the twenties, four decades after the first power station. Then, however, it came with force, because about half of the acceleration that productivity growth in American industry experienced in that decade can be traced statistically to the build-out of electric drives alone. David added a warning that strikes harder today than it did then: factory buildings wear out and eventually force a new build, but a company's processes and information structures do not wear out. Whoever does not deliberately resolve on the rebuild is forced into it by nothing.[67] Transferred to the present, that means that the real effect of artificial intelligence will not arise where employees receive one more tool, but where processes, products and responsibilities are designed from the ground up around the capabilities of digital team members.

> "The first boost to productivity from electrification came only four decades after the first power station."

> "Factory buildings wear out and eventually have to be renewed, a company's processes and information structures do not. Whoever does not resolve on the rebuild is forced into it by nothing."

From the distinction between the product and the process channel that the study by Kim and Koning contributes, a strategic decision follows that many companies have not yet recognized as one. If AI capabilities increasingly migrate into bought products, then knowledge work migrates with them out of the company and over to the vendor. Companies therefore have to decide today already, and in future far more urgently, which knowledge work they hand over to bought-in products and where they adapt the technology to their own processes so that their own knowledge stays in the company. This is not a software question but one of the most important strategic decisions of the coming years, and it is not an either-or. The real leadership achievement consists in determining, topic by topic, which work belongs in the one channel and which in the other. That the German economy, with its pronounced inclination toward refining existing models, is already feeling its way in this direction turns the decision from a theory into an observable practice, yet it is far from being taken deliberately and thought through topic by topic.[51]

> "If AI capabilities increasingly migrate into bought products, knowledge work migrates with them out of the company and over to the software vendor."

What this decision looks like in everyday practice is shown by a simple pair of opposites. Payroll is a competitive advantage for hardly any company, and here the bought-in AI product is the sensible choice, because it supplies routine knowledge cheaply and reliably. Product development, by contrast, is the core of what distinguishes a company from its competitors and therefore belongs on its own side of the line. What matters there is capturing the company's expertise in a form that belongs to the company itself and that digital team members can work with directly, regardless of which model or which vendor happens to sit underneath. That way the knowledge simply travels along when the technology changes, while knowledge handed over to someone else's product stays with the vendor.

The delegation findings in this chapter show that this step is a leadership decision in its own right and not an automatic consequence of better models, and that is precisely where the good news that follows from the diagnosis lies. The bottleneck can be shaped. An organization that makes learning itself the object, that systematically examines which task it hands to a digital colleague under which level of supervision, does not shorten the long rebuild to a single quarter, but it does turn it from a fate into a program. The metric by which this report measures the distance between pilot and production year after year will show who has taken this step.

---

<a id="WAAI-2026-C10"></a>
## The technological foundation is being built faster than companies adopt it into their work

The three forces describe what is happening in the labor market. Beneath them runs a technological development that this report tracks all year long at workafterai.org, and this chapter condenses it into a situation report. The sources are the investment decisions of early stage investors and the work of research institutions, the same evidence base as in the annual report, because where this capital flows reveals earlier than any survey which tools will arrive in companies in the coming years. These investments say less about research than about the path of the technology into the market, because they indicate which tools are moving into everyday company work, and they therefore allow an estimate of timing. The investors are always evidence and never the subject, because what can be read from their decisions in the first half of 2026 is not about returns but about work itself, and it falls into three clearly recognizable layers.

The first layer is the transition from tool to employee. The systems into which most capital is currently flowing are no longer assistants that speed up individual steps, but digital workers that take over entire tasks from beginning to end, in customer service, in research and analysis, in insurance claims processing, increasingly specialized by function and industry. This is already visible in the size of the financing rounds, when a vendor of digital employees for companies such as Sierra (sierra.ai) receives 950 million dollars in a single round. [83] How fast the task length is growing that can be handed to such workers is now measured by a research line of its own. METR, an organization specialized in evaluating AI models, determines the so-called time horizon, that is, the time humans need for those tasks that a model completes with a fifty percent success rate, and this horizon has doubled roughly every seven months since 2019, in the most recent measurement period even faster. [84] The metric comes from the field of AI safety research, is limited to software and research tasks and measures under laboratory conditions rather than in everyday operations, but it is so far the cleanest translation of model progress into the language of delegable work, and this report will carry it forward in coming editions as a reference curve. Anyone who uses this curve as a reference must also disclose how fragile its upper end is. The most recent peak value of about 16 hours comes from a measurement on a preliminary version of Claude Mythos in the spring of 2026 and lies within a confidence interval ranging from 8.5 to 55 hours, because only five of the 228 tasks in the test series take that long at all. On top of that, the human comparison times come from externally paid developers, while the maintainers of the respective code base completed the same tasks 5 to 18 times faster, and the time horizon collapses outside programming, by a factor of 40 to 100 when operating graphical interfaces. [29] The curve therefore still shows the right thing, namely an unusually fast shift in what can be delegated at all, but it shows it for a narrow slice of work and with a yardstick that no one would accept for human work, because a success rate of 50 percent would not be a passed task in any company. What is being built here is the object around which the three forces of this report revolve, a digital workforce that companies can hire.

What happens beyond this narrow slice was examined in July 2026 by a research group around Peter Kirgis and Sayash Kapoor in the CRUX evaluation project at Princeton University, with a method developed for the purpose. In what they call shadow evaluation, a lavishly equipped top agent receives the central research question of a high quality, as yet unpublished paper, and it has six days for it, has 3,000 dollars in model credit and its own computing capacity, can research freely on the web and commission subagents of its own; afterwards the original authors review the result the way they would review a conference submission. In both cases examined, the verdict was clear, because on the six point rating scale of a leading academic conference the machine written papers scored a two and a one. What is remarkable is where they failed and where they did not. The technical execution succeeded completely and without human help, from the literature review through setting up and repairing the computing environments to hundreds of experiments and the print ready document. The systems failed at the judgment of when a finding is sufficiently supported, at the creative response to fundamental criticism, to which they replied with further caveats in the text instead of a new experimental design, at withdrawing from dead ends once entered, at awareness of their own resources and deadlines, and at meeting the requirements, because both papers exceeded the permitted page count and would therefore have been screened out on formal grounds alone. The findings rest on two cases and on the researchers' observations of their own experiment, there is no independent replication, and two of those involved are among the best known critics of the thesis of an imminent machine self improvement, which the paper itself discloses. What it shows is nonetheless exactly the dividing line around which this report revolves, because the machine already fully masters the craft of execution, while it does not master the direction of a research project, that is, the judgment of what to do and when a result is good enough, and this finding does not come from a midsize company but from the workshop of the technology itself. [85]

How far this transition has already progressed in mass market business is shown by a data set from the American housing market that Andreessen Horowitz published in July 2026 from the operations of its portfolio company EliseAI (eliseai.com), whose dialogue systems handle tenant communication for roughly one in six rental apartments in the United States. In mid 2023 the median machine handled phone call lasted 46 seconds, barely more than announcing the opening hours, at the end of 2025 about one minute and 40 seconds and thus exactly as long as a conversation with a human employee. The system that used to only take the call now conducts the conversation itself. What the same data show about the handover to the human is revealing, because if a prospect is passed to an employee immediately after the machine led preliminary conversation, a good quarter end up signing the lease, whereas if three days pass before the handover, the rate falls to under 9 percent. The value of digital labor therefore does not arise where it replaces the human, but at the seam where the two work together. The numbers are an investor's own report about its portfolio company and are to be read accordingly, but as a measurement from the ongoing operations of a mass market they show that the transition from tool to employee is already everyday practice in individual industries. [86]

> "The value of digital labor does not arise where it replaces the human, but at the seam where human and machine work together."

That customer service forms the vanguard is not a peculiarity of the American housing market, it also shows up in the payment data of a great many companies. The research team of the financial services provider Ramp, whose analysis of company data this report already draws on in the chapter on the career gap, publishes a monthly ranking of the vendors that the roughly 70,000 American companies on its payment platform are newly buying from, and in July 2026 two vendors of machine customer service systems, PolyAI (poly.ai) and Sierra, took first and fourth place on the list of risers. More remarkable than the placement itself is the reason the vendor's economist gives for it, because it reaches far beyond customer service. The success of artificial intelligence in software development, he says, can so far only be read off the number of submitted code changes, the lines written and the share of developers who work with such tools at all, and these figures show the quantity of the work but not its value. In customer service, by contrast, the success stands directly in the metrics that are tracked anyway, in the share of requests handled without human involvement and in the time to closing a case. The ranking itself is the market letter of a commercial vendor, it names no growth rates, does not disclose its selection method, and its population consists of American companies that are predominantly young and close to technology. What it nonetheless shows is a selection rule that this report encounters in several places, because digital labor prevails first where its contribution is measurable, and not where it reaches furthest technically. [76]

> "The success of AI in software development so far shows up only in code changes, lines written and the share of developers who work with such tools, but not in value."

Where the digital workforce takes hold first, a decline in employment is now also becoming visible first. According to an analysis by Revelio Labs from October 2026, global call-center employment is 5.3 percent below its December 2023 peak, and it is falling because fewer people are being hired, not because more have to leave. Whether the technology is causing this decline cannot be read from these data, which is why the report records the finding as an indicator whose attribution remains open, in the part on the four observation fields that appears with the final version. [173]

> "The occupation that has long been seen as the most obvious candidate for automation is shrinking worldwide because fewer people are being hired, not because more have to leave."

The second layer is more remarkable, because it indicates a maturing. Investors are visibly no longer asking primarily what these systems can do, but whether they can be trusted, and they are now financing a whole class of tools of a kind that so far existed only for human workforces. Separate software categories are emerging for the supervision of digital team members, for testing their reliability and for controlling what they are allowed to do, and in parallel attention is shifting to the running costs of operation, because whether an autonomous digital colleague pays off is decided not by the capabilities of the model but by the inference costs of its continuous use. Translated into the language of this report, this means that the technology industry is currently retrofitting management structure, oversight and cost accounting for the digital workforce, that is, building exactly those organizational preconditions whose absence the chapter on the long rebuild diagnosed as the real bottleneck.

A secondary measurement in the American study of AI financial advice that the chapter on the long rebuild already drew on shows how justified the question of reliability is. If the same query is put five times in a row, the recommended equity allocations vary by an average of 6.5 percentage points, while the researchers' evaluation step alone contributes only 1.6 percentage points of that. The imprecision therefore does not arise in the measurement but in the machine itself, and it is the tangible reason why oversight and reliability testing of digital workers are currently becoming a market of their own.[81]

> "The technology industry is building the digital workforce faster than companies are learning to manage it."

The third layer is the most recent and perhaps the most consequential, the preparation for live deployment. Digital workers are given training environments in which they are tested before they get access to live operations, instead of learning on real tasks, and at the same time the development reaches, with legal work, the first profession with genuine liability, in which an AI system takes over the volume business under the supervision of a licensed attorney and is billed not by the hour but by result. That of all fields a profession long considered barely automatable takes this step first is revealing, because the line no longer runs between simple and demanding activities, but between organizations that can regulate oversight and liability for digital work and those that cannot. [197]

> "With legal work, an AI system takes over the volume business of a profession with genuine liability for the first time."

Two boundary conditions belong to the honesty of this situation report. The first is physical, because as soon as it is no longer individual systems but whole digital workforces that are running, infrastructure becomes the bottleneck. The investments of the large cloud providers for 2026 are estimated, depending on the definition, at 725 to 805 billion dollars, [87] the American Department of Energy expects data centers to claim between just under 7 and 12 percent of American electricity consumption by 2028, [88] and the power supply is thus developing into the hard limit of the buildout. The second boundary condition is financial, because both the Bank for International Settlements and, according to media reports, the American Treasury Department are now warning of parallels to the dotcom bubble, [89] and some of the valuations described here will not survive that test. For the question of this report, however, that is a secondary objection, because the bursting of the dotcom bubble did not undo the internet either, it only replaced its owners.

> "The large cloud providers are investing between 725 and 805 billion dollars in 2026, and the American Department of Energy expects data centers to claim up to 12 percent of American electricity consumption by 2028."

This physical limit has a second, less visible side that has less to do with building than with arithmetic. Whether a digital team member actually replaces a human colleague is decided in the end by the plain question of cost, because the only work replaced is work that is more expensive than running the artificial intelligence meant to take it over. And running it is anything but cheap. Unlike classic software, whose additional costs fall toward zero with every further user, powerful artificial intelligence incurs costs anew with every single request, for data centers, electricity, water and the data it draws on, and it therefore scales more like a streaming service that pays again for every song played than like a program that is written once and then copied a million times. As long as that remains the case, the question of replacement shifts from capability to economics, and its threshold is high at first, because the expensive frontier technology only pays off where it takes over work that is well paid anyway. The financial economist Aswath Damodaran, who initially left this cost point out of his analysis of AI disruption and expressly added it in the summer of 2026, draws the sober conclusion that the pace of the long rebuild depends not only on the learning capacity of organizations but also on the cost structure of the technology itself, and that both brakes will only ease when the operating costs of artificial intelligence fall significantly, which is by no means settled as long as a large part of these costs remains physical in nature and hangs on electricity and computing power. [31] That this threshold does not hang on electricity and computing power alone, but lies partly in the hands of the organization itself, is shown by the case in the chapter on the long rebuild in which the costs per unit fell while usage multiplied.

> "Unlike classic software, whose additional costs per user go toward zero, artificial intelligence incurs costs for computing power anew with every single request."

<a id="WAAI-2026-C10.1"></a>
### Looking ahead

Read together, the three layers show that the technology industry is currently building the supply side of the long rebuild, digital labor, its management instruments and its capacity to bear liability, and it is building faster than most organizations are developing the corresponding demand side, namely their own knowledge of which work they want to hand to this workforce and with what oversight. The bottleneck of the coming years is therefore foreseeably not on the supply side. Once the tools for trust, oversight and cost accounting are standard, the argument that the technology is not ready for live deployment loses its basis, and what remains is the question this report identifies as the real one, whether the organization is ready. Coming editions will record at this point, year after year, how far the technology has run ahead of the companies that are supposed to use it.

---

<a id="WAAI-2026-C11"></a>
## The pioneers already lead teams of agents and reorganize them with every new model

The chapters on the three forces rest on measurements: payroll records, register data, surveys and the usage data of the model providers. For the question of how people work with agents day to day, such measurements so far exist mainly in software development, and even there only for individual aspects. Anyone who wants to know where this work is heading therefore has to look where it is furthest along today: at the people who build the tools and are the first to work with them. This report treats their statements as field reports. A field report names the person, their role and the date, it states their business interests in the same breath, and it does not count as evidence for a key finding. It shows what the pioneers are doing today. Whether it spreads is something this report checks, with some distance, in the other functions, just as it treats software development as a whole as an early indicator. No prediction about how quickly the models will improve is implied. This chapter takes from the reports mainly the observed practice; where it reproduces the expectations and product goals of their authors, it marks them as such.

> "A field report is not evidence. It is the earliest indication there is of what working with agents looks like."

One such report was given by Tibo Sottiaux, who is responsible for ChatGPT and Codex at OpenAI, in a conversation with Lenny Rachitsky that was recorded at the company's developer conference and published on October 4, 2026. Asked how many agents he runs in parallel in his daily work, he described a cycle. When he is pushing the limits of what is possible, he builds larger and larger teams of agents. When the next breakthrough in models arrives, suddenly a bigger agent can do all of it on its own, keep everything in memory and learn, and he shrinks the team again. Then the expanding and contracting starts over. Most recently, thanks to a much faster agent, he has again been running fewer in parallel and is back in a state of flow.[165] Sottiaux speaks for a company that sells exactly these agents, and he is describing his own way of working, not that of his customers.

For companies, what matters in this account is less the number of agents than how often it changes. The following chapter on the organization of the future cites the units McKinsey describes, two to five people who together supervise 50 to 100 specialized AI agents.[92] Sottiaux's account of his own work suggests that such a number need not be a fixed blueprint but may be a snapshot that holds until the next leap in the models. This is an interpretation by this report, not a measurement, but it has a practical consequence: anyone who builds processes, roles and responsibilities around a particular number of digital assistants is building them around the state of one model.

> "Anyone who builds an organization around a particular number of agents is building it around the state of one model."

The second part of the account concerns how agents are directed. Fixed procedures made of loops and branching work plans, set up and adjusted by hand, have excited many people recently. Sottiaux does not consider them the approach that will prevail. He is betting on a highly capable agent that works around the clock, knows its user's goals and preferences and learns from feedback, instead of someone working out how to repeat a procedure exactly so that it produces good results; in doing so he is also describing the product OpenAI presented at the same conference.[165] How teams actually direct their agents today has been studied by a research group from Stanford University and Carnegie Mellon University across 441 open-source projects, to which the chapter on the long rebuild already refers; the work has not been peer reviewed. 73.8 percent of the instruction files in which teams give their coding agents rules are created once and never changed again.[154] Read together, and this is an interpretation by this report, the rules for the agents stand still in most of the projects studied, while a pioneer reorganizes his way of working with every leap in the models. The distance is reminiscent of the gap between pilot and production that this report describes for companies as a whole, only one level deeper, in the daily handling of the tool.

> "73.8 percent of the instruction files for coding agents are never changed after they are first created."

Where the pioneers' agents work was described by David Heinemeier Hansson, the creator of Ruby on Rails, a widely used toolkit for web applications, on October 4, 2026, the day the conversation with Sottiaux was also published, in a post on the platform X that was viewed more than 600,000 times within two days. Every developer, he wrote, needs a dedicated machine for their AI that is always on, reachable on their private network, and on which most of their agents run. The laptop then no longer needs to be particularly powerful, and the agents keep working even when you close the lid.[166] The research for this report found no business stake of Hansson's in the tools and devices he names. The program he names for his agents, which displays many agents side by side and tracks their state, was presented in July 2026; according to the company, it had raised six million dollars in seed funding by early September, including from the founder of Shopify and the chief technology officer of Cloudflare.[167] Sottiaux reports in the same conversation that OpenAI runs some of its specialized agents on their own hardware, with the control system not running on the user's device, and that the company is spending more and more computing power on having the working agents monitored by a second system that intervenes in risky actions.[165] Both are field reports, not measurements. In both accounts the agents' work runs on machines of their own rather than on the human's work computer, and at OpenAI the computing power devoted solely to monitoring them is growing. For the cost question raised by the chapter on the technological foundation, it follows in this report's assessment that the price of an agent's work includes the price of its supervision.

> "The price of an agent's work includes the price of its supervision."

Under Hansson's post, a consultant objected that many in his line of work are not allowed to take their clients' code off the company laptop at all; a machine of their own at home would breach the contract immediately. Hansson saw this as a further advantage for the companies that commit fully to agents, over the laggards and the incumbents.[166] The brief exchange shows that the question of where agents run is also a question of organization. Who owns the machine on which they work around the clock, where the data they work with may be stored, and who is liable if something goes wrong there are things every organization has to decide for itself. If it does not decide, there is in this report's assessment a risk that its employees will settle it on their own, just as they are already crossing the boundaries between occupations today.

How far companies are from answering these questions in an orderly way can be read from BCG's survey of 1,330 executives at mostly large companies, published at the end of September 2026, the first broad survey this chapter draws on, even though it contains only self-reports. 42 percent of companies expect their agents to decide without human approval by 2030, yet only 5 percent have all six safeguards in place today that BCG considers necessary for this. These safeguards include a defined scope and oversight, the ability to check results and roll back actions, and clear ownership of every agent. Companies thus expect far more independence from their agents than they can secure today, and the question of who is responsible for an agent cannot be answered by any technology, only by the organization itself.[176] Where this responsibility ends today is shown by a survey by the agency network Dentsu Creative of 1,950 marketing decision-makers in 14 markets, including Germany, which likewise rests on self-reports. 40 percent would let an agent adapt existing assets without human sign-off, 31 percent would let it brief their agency on strategic priorities, and 27 percent would let it communicate with the board and the CEO, a task one in ten would never hand over. The closer a task sits to the top of the company, the less often it is handed over, and this boundary, too, is drawn not by the technology but by the organization.[199]

The third part of Sottiaux's account concerns how working with agents feels for people. Even with voice control and the combination of text, image and sound, he says, operating it still feels somewhat clunky, and it will keep feeling clunky until it no longer does.[165] As an example of what he is aiming for, he describes not a person in front of a screen formulating one instruction after another, but an agent in the meeting room that listens, takes notes, starts building in the background and keeps working with people in conversation, without the fatigue that constantly formulating instructions produces; that has not yet been achieved today. In his own words, he wants to reduce the fatigue of constantly adjusting settings and the fact that working with an agent has so far been a solo undertaking. Asked about the pressure to run thirty agents at once because it is possible, he answered that perhaps people need fewer meetings and would even be more productive well rested; the promise of the technology is not one more instruction per second.[165]

These sentences from a vendor fit findings that the chapter on the long rebuild has already measured. 69 percent of executives in the G-P survey now spend more time monitoring unchecked AI work and reworking it like editors,[71] among the participants in the Lenny's Newsletter survey the share of at least moderately burned-out respondents rose within a year from 44.7 to 55.7 percent,[28] and in the company studied by Stanford and Carnegie Mellon the load per reviewer roughly doubled.[152] That of all people a pioneer whose company sells these agents considers less parallelism and more rest conceivable is remarkable. It fits the thesis this report derives from the measurements: the bottleneck in working with agents is not their number but the attention of the people who direct them.

What this thesis means in everyday work was measured in October 2026 by the vendor Anthropic on itself, and it is the first measurement of its kind across an entire company, not just across software development. For the Anthropic Institute, Nathan Wilmers, Maxim Massenkoff and Peter McCrory evaluated their own company's agent session data, calendars, Slack channels and personnel records.[182] Since March 2026 the running time of the agents there has exceeded the working hours of the people, and in August the agents directed by individual employees worked six times as many hours as their principals, counted as compute runtime including tool use against 40 hours a week per head. The real number lies behind that. In 2026 the human becomes the bottleneck: the agents spend four of every five minutes waiting for a person. Around 80 percent of the time of a session with an agent is time spent waiting for a human input, and whoever wanted to read everything their own agents produce would, by the authors' calculation, need more than half a working week even in non-technical functions. The organization answers with tooling, just as the Stanford and Carnegie Mellon measurement already showed for code review. The share of sessions in which employees work not on the task itself but on steering their agents, on instructions, review loops and monitoring, rose within half a year from 10 to 22 percent; written standing instructions to agents grew within a year from a few hundred to 8,500; agents review the work of other agents. And the people sit together longer. Meeting time for the median employee has risen by 20 percent, for technical staff without management responsibility by 16 percent, because the questions that remain when working with agents require, in the respondents' words, coordination and decisions between people. A fifth of employees now describe their role as managing Claudes rather than doing the work, and one engineer compares it to babysitting, because every agent presents its concern with the same urgency. The measurement comes from the vendor about itself, its raw data cannot be inspected, the share of steering work rests on a machine-coded sample of 2,000 session summaries, and the reading time is calculated from word counts, not observed. As a measurement of production use it is nevertheless the most complete so far, and it shows the same thing as the field reports in this chapter: the limit is not the number of agents but the attention of the people, and as soon as the organization hits that limit it first builds tools to relieve the people.

> "In 2026 the human becomes the bottleneck: the agents spend four of every five minutes waiting for a person."

How small the circle of people who work this way today is was estimated by Andrej Karpathy on the platform X on October 11, 2026. Karpathy was one of the founders of OpenAI, later headed Tesla's AI department and has worked in model development at Anthropic since May 2026, that is, at one of the companies whose systems he writes about. By his napkin math, around 6 billion people, roughly three quarters of the world's population, have so far barely come into contact with language models. Between one and two billion people use free offerings in the style of ChatGPT occasionally, much like a better search engine or as a writing aid. Around 20 million, 0.2 percent, use the most capable models professionally in mathematics and software development and see first-hand that agents complete large projects that used to take them weeks or months from a single prompt. Finally, around 5,000 people at the companies that develop these models have access to systems of which, in Karpathy's words, the outside world has so far only seen the preview. There, by his description, swarms of thousands of agents work for weeks on software mega projects, and human review and comprehension are starting to fall behind.[180] More important for this report than the numbers is one assignment. Karpathy explicitly places many professionals outside mathematics and software development in the second tier as well, among them executives who spend much of their time talking to other people. In his words, they understand what is happening intellectually, but only second-hand.[180]

The numbers are one individual's estimate, not a measurement, and they come from an employee of a model provider. There is at least one broad measurement that can be set alongside the second tier, even though it too comes from a vendor. According to Microsoft's usage data, 18.8 percent of people aged 15 to 64 worldwide used a generative AI tool in the second quarter of 2026, which by this report's calculation with United Nations population figures comes to around one billion people.[181] That is at the lower end of Karpathy's range, although Microsoft does not count younger and older people. For the tiers above it there is no comparable measurement. What the field report does show is a direction. Karpathy describes the gap between the tiers as widening and names three reasons: the size and ambition of the tasks handed to the systems, their uneven strength, which peaks in domains that are digital, verifiable and economically valuable, and access, which can be free, paid or internal.[180] For companies, in this report's assessment, it follows that precisely the people who decide on the rebuild of their organization often know working with agents only second-hand. Whoever is to lead the long rebuild will therefore have to gain this experience themselves or bring someone into the leadership who has.

> "Those who decide on the rebuild often know working with agents only second-hand."

<a id="WAAI-2026-C11.1"></a>
### Looking ahead

Read together, the field reports in this chapter yield three decisions that every company working with agents has to make, and none of them is technical. The first concerns the blueprint. It has to allow contraction as much as expansion, because the right number of agents can change with the leaps in the models. The second concerns location. Where the agents run, who owns the machines, where the data may be stored and who is liable belongs in the company's rules before employees settle it themselves. The third concerns supervision. It is a task of its own with costs of its own, in computing power as in human attention, and according to Sottiaux, OpenAI is devoting increasing computing power to it. Whether the pioneers' way of working spreads beyond software development is something this report will check in the coming versions against the other observation fields. Until then, what this report holds as its central thesis applies to working with agents: it is decided not by the model alone but by the organization that uses it.

> "Working with agents is decided not by the model alone but by the organization that uses it."

---

<a id="WAAI-2026-C12"></a>
## The organization of the future arranges its work around topics instead of functions, and it gets smaller in the process

The debate about artificial intelligence and work is almost always about which occupations are under threat, and that pushes aside the question that owners and management actually have on their desks: which blueprint a company will use to organize its work from here on. The functional organization, the division into marketing, sales, finance and engineering, is not a law of nature but a historical answer to a cost calculation. Specialist knowledge was scarce and expensive, so companies bundled the specialists into departments and accepted that work would travel through handoffs between them. Ever since the essay "The Nature of the Firm" by the later Nobel laureate Ronald Coase in 1937, we have known that the boundaries and the internal structure of firms follow coordination costs. [90] When that calculation changes, the blueprint changes sooner or later.

> "The division into marketing, sales, finance and engineering is not a law of nature but a historical answer to a cost calculation. When the calculation changes, the blueprint changes."

That calculation is exactly what is now in motion, and measurably so. The chapter on the long rebuild showed that the usage data of the three large model vendors reveal no replacement of entire occupations. What the OpenAI measurement makes visible instead is the handoff that is saved: almost half of the professional tasks the tools are used for belong historically to a different occupation, and the pattern is strongest where no specialist is within reach to delegate to. [10] The first organizational effect of the technology is therefore not the replaced position but the replaced detour across a colleague's desk. Where handing the task to the specialist department used to be the norm, the task is increasingly done by the person where the need arises, with a digital team member alongside. The boundaries between roles are shifting faster than any org chart says.

> "The usage data of the large model vendors reveal no replacement of entire occupations."

> "Almost half of the professional tasks the tools are used for actually belong to other occupations."

Whether work done outside one's own field also delivers what it promises cannot be read from usage data, but it can be read from a controlled experiment, and the research group around Fabrizio Dell'Acqua and Karim Lakhani, whose experiment at Boston Consulting Group the chapter on the long rebuild uses as the benchmark for the individual's gain, repeated it at Procter & Gamble (pg.com), this time with the question of collaboration. Between May and July 2024, 791 professionals from product development and commercial functions worked on real innovation tasks from their own business units, randomly assigned to four groups: alone or in pairs, each with or without a tool based on GPT-4. The peer-reviewed analysis, published in Organization Science in 2026, found that an individual with AI matched the quality of a two-person team without AI, and that the team with AI did not measurably exceed it. Above all, the machine made the functional lens disappear. Without AI, the developers' solutions stayed technical and the marketers' solutions stayed commercial; with AI, the two backgrounds could no longer be told apart in the results, and participants reported more enthusiasm and less frustration at the same time. That is, under controlled conditions, the handoff saved that OpenAI's measurement makes visible in everyday work. Two caveats belong here. The experiment lasted one day and measured idea generation, not execution, and four of the eleven authors work at the company studied, which the study itself discloses. And the machine helped with finding, not with choosing: without AI, teams picked their best idea half the time, with AI only in just over a third of cases. Those who let the boundaries between roles fall get more usable proposals and need more judgment at the same time about which of them counts. [203]

At the level of entire companies, the same movement shows up in new ventures. The study by Kim and Koning, drawn on repeatedly over the course of this report, found that AI-native firms are set up about a quarter smaller and roughly half a hierarchy level flatter than comparable companies in the same industry and the same founding cohort, at comparable or higher valuations. [51] In established companies, a broad reduction of management layers is running in parallel, which Korn Ferry (kornferry.com) documents as a pattern without attributing it to artificial intelligence, [91] and individual cases show how radical that reduction can be, for instance the reported cut in management levels in Microsoft's gaming division from 14 to three to five. [24] That companies themselves expect further cuts precisely in the middle is shown by BCG's survey of 1,330 executives at mostly large companies, published at the end of September 2026. Roughly half of the companies expect artificial intelligence to reduce roles in middle management and among team leads by more than 10 percent, while in upper management only about a quarter do. This, too, is an expectation and not a measurement, and it comes from the survey of a consultancy that supports such restructurings. [176] None of these observations proves on its own that the technology is the cause. Taken together, though, they suggest that organizations are moving toward fewer levels, smaller units and broader roles.

What is striking is that three very different players in the market now sketch the same target picture. From its own consulting practice, McKinsey describes units of two to five people that oversee a complete process from start to finish with 50 to 100 specialized AI agents. [92] Gartner expects that in 2029, 60 percent of organizations will deploy small developer teams across the board, up from 15 percent in 2026, and explicitly speaks of the boundaries between roles dissolving. [53] Tool vendors such as Warp (warp.dev) describe the developer's new core task as understanding and adjusting the system that does the work, no longer as doing the work itself. [93] All three have a business interest in this picture, none of them presents a solid measurement, and their figures are expectations, not measured values. But the agreement between consulting, market research and software vendors with what the usage data already show about employee behavior means the target picture has to be taken seriously: the small unit built around a topic, fully accountable for an outcome, pulling the adjacent expertise from the machine.

> "The target picture is the small unit built around a topic, fully accountable for an outcome, pulling the adjacent expertise from the machine."

What it looks like when a corporation applies this target picture to itself has been on the record since August 2026 at Meta. The restructuring plan that the first chapter treats as the fourth case of the AI argument was, at its core, an organizational plan. According to the guide circulated internally, small units of two to three developers and one designer were to work in four-week cycles instead of half-year plans, the job titles product designer and software developer were to be merged into the single title builder, the middle management layers were to disappear, and every unit was to report to a single area lead who decides on evaluation and promotion for 30 to 50 people, while the head of the small unit no longer had any management authority; an agent-supported analysis was to set the daily priorities. By June, according to the Reuters report, at least eleven areas had adopted this form. That is, almost point for point, the target picture drawn by consulting, market research and tool makers, implemented in a corporation with 75,000 employees. What Reuters quotes from the internal forums is the cost side that this chapter describes further below: an employee who had been given the lead of a unit noted that he received neither management training nor access to the evaluation tools; the responsibility had been distributed, the authority had not. Together with the internally measured incidents, which rose by 40 percent, and a workforce whose approval fell from 74 to 55 percent within half a year, that adds up to the first documented large-scale attempt to build this chapter's target picture, and its provisional outcome: the downsizing could be ordered, the coordination could not be abolished. [26]

Meta was not the first corporation to take this path, only the first whose failure is documented. Half a year earlier, Block (block.xyz), Jack Dorsey's payments group with the brands Square, Cash App and Afterpay, had announced the same blueprint and executed it at once. In the shareholder letter for the fourth quarter of 2025, filed with the securities regulator on February 26, 2026, Dorsey announced that the workforce would shrink from more than 10,000 to just under 6,000 people, by nearly half in the words of the letter, by more than 40 percent in the words of the mandatory filing; more than 4,000 employees were dismissed or, where the law requires it, entered into consultation, and the company put the cost of the restructuring at 450 to 500 million dollars. The reason was not financial distress, and Dorsey said so explicitly, but the technology itself: intelligence tools had changed what it means to build and run a company, a significantly smaller team could do more and do it better with them, and within a year the majority of companies would reach the same conclusion. The share price closed 16.8 percent higher the next day, which makes the case a confirmation of the mechanism described in the first chapter. [201]

Five weeks later, Dorsey supplied the organizational plan to go with it, together with Roelof Botha, head of the venture capital firm Sequoia (sequoiacap.com) and a member of Block's board, in an essay titled "From Hierarchy to Intelligence". Hierarchy, the argument runs, was only ever a means of bridging the limited span of control of human beings, from the Roman legion through the railroads of the nineteenth century to the modern corporation; earlier attempts to abolish it, from Holacracy to Spotify's squads, failed because they lacked the technology for coordination. Block now wants to hand that coordination to a model of its own company that records processes, responsibilities and knowledge in machine-readable form, and to reduce its workforce to three roles: individual contributors who build and operate and decide within their own layer; directly responsible individuals who own a cross-cutting outcome, such as merchant churn in one segment over 90 days, with the authority to pull resources from other teams; and player-coaches who build themselves and develop people at the same time. There is no need, the two write, for a permanent middle management layer; the intelligence lives in the system, the people are on the edge. The text concedes that the company is at the beginning of this transition and that parts of it may fail at first. For this report, Block is thus the second documented large-scale attempt of this kind, and unlike at Meta, the blueprint is not available through leaked documents but as a program signed by the chief executive and a member of his board. The reduction is documented, its attribution to the technology comes from the company. The quarterly letter of November 3, 2026 names neither a headcount nor a reversal, but attributes an 11 percent decline in personnel costs in product development to the restructuring, alongside a 22 percent increase in gross profit; whether the smaller organization delivers the promised speed for good can be read at the earliest from the full-year figures for 2026, and the sixth forecast of this report therefore keeps the case on record. [201][202]

What it looks like when this target picture meets a single function can already be inspected in marketing, the function whose tasks travel furthest into other occupations according to the OpenAI measurement. A McKinsey survey of 521 marketing leaders published in June 2026, produced in cooperation with Google's advertising business and therefore to be read with a double interest at play, traces there the same movement this chapter describes. Marie Gulin-Merle, responsible for global ads marketing at Google, sums it up in the sentence that AI agents do not respect silos, and expects that responsibility for performance marketing, growth and brand will no longer sit in separate roles. The consultancy itself sketches the future marketing organization as a diamond rather than a pyramid, explicitly as an illustration from client examples and not as a measurement, with fewer purely executing people and three role types that repeat this chapter's blueprint almost word for word: some build the digital team members, others orchestrate them across the entire process, and the third group secures standard and judgment as the quality authority. More telling than the target picture is the measured distance from it. Only 28 percent of respondents see a fundamental rebuild of teams and processes in their own company, and while just under 60 percent use the tools several times a week, fewer than 10 percent draw a benefit from end-to-end processes, which means the gap between pilot and production from the chapter on the long rebuild returns here in marketing. One figure, finally, exposes the emotional side of the organizational decision: the tension is greatest at the very top, with 96 percent of marketing chiefs reporting high enthusiasm, 71 percent reporting fear at the same time, and 80 percent seeing a risk to their own role. The reordering of work is therefore decided by the people most exposed to it. [94]

> "Marketing tasks travel furthest into other occupations."

> "Marketing chiefs report high enthusiasm and, at the same time, fear for their own role. The reordering of work is decided by the people most exposed to it."

How broadly the function is already exposed to the technology is shown by the figures from Germany. According to a representative survey by Bitkom Research among 603 companies with at least 20 employees, in the summer of 2026 a majority of 57 percent of German companies use artificial intelligence for the first time, and 54 percent of them use it in marketing and communications; only for customer inquiries is it more frequent, at 72 percent, while in sales it is 14 percent.[168] Nevertheless, marketing shows no reduction that could be attributed to the technology. In a survey by the Content Marketing Institute among more than 600 marketers, only 11 percent of organizations state that they have replaced employees with AI, while 76 percent do the work of more than one position and entry-level hires are made less often than experienced ones; both are self-reported.[169] The effect therefore shows up not in layoffs but in denser work and a narrower entry point, the same pattern this report describes for companies as a whole.

That this movement does not stop at the company boundary becomes clear when you look at the supply side of marketing. Martin Sorrell, who built WPP (wpp.com) into what was for a time the largest advertising group in the world and who today runs the digital agency group S4 Capital (s4capital.com), told his own industry in late July 2026 that it is having the wrong discussion. The biggest disruption does not lie in the question of whether the machine replaces the creatives; the real change runs through media, data, platforms, organizational structures and the economics of the agency business. Clients will demand that the efficiency gains of the technology be passed on to them, agencies that continue to sell time rather than outcomes will come under growing pressure, and the large platforms will become even more powerful, while retail advertising inventory and the influencer business shift where the ad money flows. This is not a measurement but the assessment of a man who earns his living with S4 Capital on the counter model and who presents it as a pointer to his own podcast appearance. It is revealing nonetheless, because it describes from the supply side the same pincer movement that the usage data show from the demand side. From the inside, neighboring functions take over the tasks of the function that, according to the OpenAI measurement, gives away its work furthest, as when sales builds its own sales campaign with digital team members and management pulls brand stewardship to itself. From the outside, the platforms and the retailers take over the execution that used to sit with the agencies, whose business model of selling working time bills for precisely what the technology devalues fastest. [13]

This outer flank can now be quantified. The advertising channel that structurally bypasses the agency, because the retailer markets its own inventory directly, grew to just under 175 billion dollars in 2025 according to the forecast of the market researcher WARC and will, at just under 197 billion in 2026, overtake combined spending on classic and connected television for the first time; WPP's own media holding GroupM arrives independently at almost the same figure, and the fact that of all players the largest agency holding documents the growth of the channel that undercuts its intermediary role speaks for the number. Depending on the count, that puts roughly 15 to 16 percent of the entire advertising market in retail advertising inventory. At the same time, according to the same source, Alphabet (abc.xyz), Amazon (amazon.com) and Meta together account for around 55 percent of the advertising market outside China, and the trend is rising; Amazon's advertising business grew by 26 percent in the second quarter of 2026 to 19.8 billion dollars, Meta's by 27 percent to 59.4 billion. Where the platforms are taking this business was described unmistakably by Mark Zuckerberg in an interview in May 2025: a company tells the platform its goal and provides its bank details, and the system handles creation, audience selection and measurement. Google has already taken the first step in that direction and has since August 2026 been automatically moving its clients' search campaigns into the AI-driven campaign format. How far reality is keeping up is shown by an April 2026 survey of media agencies by the trade service Marketing Brew: automated delivery already accounts for 60 to 70 percent of Meta budgets at individual agencies, yet none of the agencies surveyed knew of a client that already fully trusts machine creation. The gap between pilot and production of the long rebuild returns on the platform side. [14]

How this double pressure affects the supplier industry can be read in its own financial results, and those show no simple decline but a spread. WPP, for decades the industry benchmark, lost 5.4 percent organically in 2025, closed the year with a net loss, cut the dividend from 39.4 to 15.0 pence and, after a profit warning in July 2025, lost 16 percent of its market value in a single day, to its lowest level since 2009; the new chief executive Cindy Rose, brought in from Microsoft, has been rebuilding the group since. Publicis (publicisgroupe.com), by contrast, which bet early on data and its own AI platform, grew 5.6 percent organically in the same year and raised its prediction in the summer of 2026. The merged group of Omnicom (omnicomgroup.com) and Interpublic (interpublic.com), the largest vendor in the industry since November 2025, is growing and cutting staff on a large scale at the same time; revenue and employment are therefore decoupling even where the business is going well. And Sorrell's own group S4 Capital, launched as the digital counter model, is shrinking for the third year in a row and owes its profit jump in the first half of 2026 almost entirely to cost cutting. The crisis is therefore not hitting the agency as a species but the business model of selling working time, and it spares not even the person who diagnoses it most loudly. [15]

On the client side, the pincer closes. The American association of advertisers ANA has been measuring for almost two decades how many of its members run an in-house agency: 42 percent in 2008, 58 percent in 2013, 78 percent in 2018, 82 percent in 2023, with 92 percent still working with external agencies at the same time, so the shift concerns tasks, not the relationship as such. For Germany a first figure has existed since September 2026, and it was collected, of all parties, by the agency association itself. In a survey by the Handelsblatt Research Institute and techconsult for the GWA among 170 executives with budget responsibility in companies with at least 100 employees, 80 percent plan to use AI to bring more marketing work in-house in future, most often in market research, creation and the production of advertising materials; at the same time only 8 percent expect the role of agencies to shrink overall.[171] What is new is that artificial intelligence is accelerating this shift. In the spring 2025 spending survey by the market researcher Gartner, 22 percent of marketing chiefs state that generative AI has already reduced their dependence on external agencies for creation and strategy, and 39 percent plan to cut their agency budgets. One year later, according to the spending survey of May 2026 among 401 marketing chiefs, an average of 15.3 percent of marketing budgets is earmarked for AI, but only 30 percent of respondents consider their organization ready to deploy the technology broadly, and 70 percent admit to immature processes.[170] That expectations of the technology are becoming more sober is shown by the Dentsu Creative survey cited in the previous chapter, which repeats the same statements every year. The share of marketing decision-makers who expect generative AI to make producing assets much faster and cheaper fell within a year from 89 to 82 percent, the share who expect it to let them bring much of their marketing in-house from 90 to 83 percent, and 70 percent say they have not yet seen major cost efficiencies. Instead, respondents pin their hopes for savings on their suppliers: 78 percent expect the technology to cut their agency costs this year. That an agency network collects and publishes this figure itself speaks for it rather than against it.[199] What that looks like in individual cases is demonstrated by two companies whose figures are to be read as self-reported: the payment provider Klarna (klarna.com) reported as early as spring 2024 that it had cut its spending on external agencies by a quarter and shortened the production cycle for campaign images from six weeks to seven days, and Unilever (unilever.com) has been building its own AI design studios since the summer of 2025, remarkably together with the agency group Interpublic, which thereby earns from the move of value creation to its own client. The academic survey of marketing chiefs by Duke University adds the staffing side to the picture: growth in marketing teams has halved within a year, to 2.49 percent, and respondents expect the technology to take over more than half of marketing activities within three years. Here too the counter calculation belongs in the picture: the British industry survey by ISBA measured a considerable gap as early as 2023 between what companies expected from the shift into their own teams and what they experienced. In-house it is often no faster, the paths are merely shorter. [16]

**[Figure 7: Four in five advertisers run the agency in house.]**

Taken together, that is the full pincer: from the inside, neighboring functions take over marketing tasks with their digital team members, from the outside, platforms and retailers pull the execution to themselves, and on the client side, whatever agency work remains moves into in-house teams. What is left for the function in the middle is the role the McKinsey typology describes as the quality authority, and with it the question of whether that still requires a department of its own. Of all the classic functions, marketing is therefore the first in which this chapter's blueprint can be inspected in full, from the inside as well as from the outside, and therefore also the one in which it will be decided earliest whether a function shapes the rebuild or suffers it.

Anyone who turns this into a simple downsizing formula, however, is leaving out the cost side of the rebuild. Coordination does not disappear, it shifts, from alignment between people to oversight of parallel digital contributors, and there are precedents for this in the history of failed experiments with flat hierarchies, from Zappos (zappos.com) to Medium (medium.com), in which it turned out that the abolished coordination had not been saved but merely shifted in silence. [91] The orchestration load of the previous chapter is the human side of this calculation. The business side has been measured quarterly by KPMG: 49 percent of the organizations surveyed have already adjusted the pace of their AI rollouts once expected costs began to exceed expected benefits, and only a good third have full visibility into their own AI operating costs. [72] On top of that comes a quieter danger, which the Boston Consulting Group describes as a distributed loss of expertise: when everyone does a bit of everything, the depth of expertise on which the quality of the whole rests can erode. [56] What this supervision looks like in the practice of the pioneers, and how often the number of digital assistants changes there, is described in the previous chapter.

The OpenAI measurement already contains this second warning itself, because what is measured are attempts, not results. Whether work done outside one's own field delivers what it promises is not shown by usage data, the experiment at Procter & Gamble answers the question only for idea generation, and OpenAI explicitly notes that specialists remain central for judgment and review. [10] An organization that simply lets its employees cross boundaries without reordering review and accountability is not organizing productivity for itself but an unanticipated risk. The Stanford research on human-centered AI supplies the framework for this: the division of labor between human and machine has to be designed according to task, expertise and risk, and the risks accumulate over time instead of becoming visible in individual errors. [95]

> "An organization that simply lets boundary crossings happen is not organizing productivity for itself but an unanticipated risk."

From both follows the real organizational decision of the coming years, and it is a decision no one is spared. The delegation experiment by Colaiacovo and Koning showed that the willingness to hand work to the machine depends only to a good third on what one credits the technology with, and beyond that is an independent leadership decision. [7] The same holds for the structure of the organization. Three sub-questions belong to this one decision: first, which value creation a small team will be accountable for from start to finish, with digital team members for the adjacent competencies; second, where depth of expertise deliberately stays bundled, no longer as an executing department but as an authority for standards, review and judgment; third, from the chapter on the long rebuild, which knowledge work may migrate into the purchased product and which stays tied to the company's own processes. Whoever does not make this decision gets the new division of work anyway, just without steering it, because employees are already crossing the old boundaries today, chat by chat.

<a id="WAAI-2026-C12.1"></a>
### Looking ahead

If you extend the lines of this chapter, what emerges is the picture of an organizational type that differs from the org chart of the industrial age more clearly than anything digitization has produced so far, with small units arranged by topics and outcomes, each leading people and digital team members together, while the classic functions become what quality authorities always were, guardians of standard, review and training. Careers in this organization lead less often through the size of a span of control and more often through the depth of the topics one is accountable for. Whether it turns out this way is open, and the history of management fads counsels caution. This assessment will therefore track the question of the division of work annually from now on, from 2027 in the board pulse and, from this edition onward, in its own predictions as well. The companies that shape this division deliberately will differ from those that let it happen, and that is in the end the same fault line along which this report pins the future of work as a whole: it is not the capability of the machine that decides, but the capability of the organization to reorganize itself around it.

> "The organization of the future will differ from the org chart of the industrial age more clearly than anything digitization has produced so far."

> "Careers in these organizations lead less often through the size of a span of control than through the depth of the topics one is accountable for."

How quickly an organization of this type can be rebuilt will depend on whether it can describe itself. Software development is already further along here. Coding agents read the entire source code they work on and draw the knowledge of how work is done in a given company from so-called skills, instructions in simple text files that record procedures and company knowledge in version-controlled folders; Anthropic released the format as an open standard in December 2025, and the tools of OpenAI, Google, Microsoft and Cursor now read it as well.
