[188] This report expects that such skills will soon exist for a company's existing organization as well, for responsibilities, approvals, procedures and capabilities, and that the organization can thus be extended and changed in a way that was previously unimaginable: no longer in projects that take months, but in changes that are described, reviewed and take effect immediately. A first company is already testing this on itself. In its paper The AI-Empowered Organization, published in early October 2026, the Zurich-based consulting group Eraneos describes the organization as code, with responsibilities, rules and capabilities, which the paper explicitly calls skills, held as version-controlled text files that people and digital team members read and change according to the same rules. The paper presents no measured contribution to results. It admits friction in its own house, sees itself as still at the beginning and comes from a consultancy that sells exactly this transformation. [189] As confirmation of the direction, it suffices: whoever makes their organization readable today will be able to rebuild it faster tomorrow than anyone who knows it only from the org chart.

> "Just as skills know a company's source code today, they will know its organization tomorrow, and then the organization can be changed like software."

Whoever makes their organization readable, however, also changes where decisions are made within it. Erik Brynjolfsson and the economist Zoë Hitzig of the Harvard Society of Fellows describe this reverse side in the National Bureau of Economic Research volume already mentioned, under a title that alludes to an essay by Friedrich August von Hayek from 1945. Hayek had argued why central planning fails: the necessary knowledge lies dispersed among the people on the spot, and much of it cannot be written down. According to Brynjolfsson and Hitzig, artificial intelligence weakens this argument in two ways. It turns knowledge that previously resided only in the experience of the people on the spot into information that can be passed on, and it allows a headquarters to process far more information than before. They derive three consequences from this: larger companies, by the evidence they cite larger in revenue rather than in headcount, greater concentration within industries, and less decision-making latitude for local management. For artificial intelligence this has not yet been measured. The authors draw on examples from the time before, such as Walmart, where in the late 1980s every store was linked to headquarters by satellite and category managers at headquarters rather than those responsible on site decided what each store would carry. They explicitly name countervailing forces, from physical skills and rare special cases through the time advantage of whoever decides on the spot to competition law, and in times of upheaval the decision on the spot can keep the advantage, which is why, by their account, centralization need not increase steadily even during the transition. [192] For the outlook of this chapter, this yields a refinement. Whether the readable organization produces smaller units with more responsibility of their own or a headquarters that reaches into every unit is decided not by the technology but by who maintains the description of the organization and who determines its changes.

<a id="WAAI-2026-C13"></a>
## Anyone who transfers the American debate to Europe is measuring with the wrong yardstick

Now that the three forces have been told apart, a question presses forward that the evidence so far does not answer at all, because that evidence consists almost entirely of American data. Does the same picture hold for Europe, or is an American story being quietly transferred to a continent with different rules? The obvious assumption, backed by the force of the Stanford numbers, would be that the same collapse at the entry level must show up in Europe as well, as soon as one looks closely enough.[4] The European aggregate figures, however, speak against that assumption at first glance.

At the level of the 27 EU member states, the youth unemployment rate stood at around 15 percent in April 2026, the overall rate at 6 percent. Over the course of the years since 2022, overall unemployment has fallen slightly and has held stable at a historic low, while youth unemployment has risen from just under 15 to a good 15 percent, so the relative disadvantage of the younger group has widened slightly, even though the labor market overall remains in good shape. But a different number is the decisive one. The American finding of a collapse among young university graduates does not appear in the Union's aggregate figures; on the contrary, the employment rate of younger graduates rose to around 83 percent in 2025, after a little more than 82 percent the year before, with a small dip among university graduates that does not continue. Anyone who transfers the American headline to Europe unchecked is therefore measuring with a yardstick that the European numbers themselves do not supply.[96]

That does not mean nothing is happening in Europe, but that the average of the whole continent hides what matters. Germany, for instance, has the lowest youth unemployment rate in the entire European Union at around 7 percent, well below the EU average, while Austria stands at around 11.5 and Switzerland at 8 percent.[96] The large European institutions, Eurostat, the OECD and the European vocational training center Cedefop, have so far not confirmed any collapse at the entry level along American lines, and the fact that it has failed to appear is itself a finding and not merely a lack of evidence.[97][36][98]

An honest reading of this non-finding, however, has to include a second possibility that qualifies the all clear. The absence of the American pattern can mean protection, but it can also mean delay, because the technology is arriving in Europe later and flatter than in the United States. The continent's private AI investment stands at a fraction of the American figure[99], the number of first time AI funding rounds for young companies has grown eightfold within four years in the American data[51], while the European founding landscape remains distinctly narrower, and even where European companies have long been using the tools, the actual intensity of use is growing only slowly according to the Bundesbank data.[66] But if the American entry level collapse, whichever of its three possible causes lies behind it, is connected to the diffusion of the technology, then today's European aggregate figures may be measuring not the resilience of the continent but only its lag in diffusion. European regional research supports this reading with a look below the country level. The regional economists Andrés Rodríguez-Pose and Federico Bartalucci, in their paper "A Union under siege, or a Union besieging itself?" published in June 2026, set the AI exposure of Europe's regions against their innovation capacity and come to the conclusion that large parts of southern and eastern Europe appear untouched by the technology only because they are largely excluded from the knowledge economy. Apparent safety from automation, the authors write, is not resilience but irrelevance.[100] What such a delay looks like in a single occupation is now shown by German call centers. In establishments whose business is call center services, the number of employees subject to social insurance contributions fell by 17.9 percent between December 2022 and December 2025 according to the statistics of the Federal Employment Agency, most recently faster than before, by 9.5 percent in the year to December 2025, while total employment rose slightly.[174] In American call center establishments the decline began before the launch of ChatGPT and is now slowing, from 12.0 percent in the year to August 2023 to 4.1 percent in the year to August 2026.[175] Whether the technology is behind this has not been measured in either country, but the time lag is exactly the pattern this reading would expect. The European finding would then be no counterproof but a window of time, and whether it closes is among the questions this report will keep following in the same numbers in the coming editions.

> "Apparent safety from automation is not resilience but irrelevance."

How far this observation reaches beyond Europe was shown by the World Bank in early August 2026, when it devoted its World Development Report, its most important annual publication, entirely to artificial intelligence. The finding the news agencies lifted into their headlines sounds like a reversal of the entire history of automation so far: in high-income countries 14.2 percent of existing jobs are accessible to automation by generative AI, in low- and middle-income countries only 4.5 percent, so the rich economies are more than three times as exposed as the poor ones. Earlier waves of automation hit the factory work of the global South; this one hits cognitive work, which is concentrated in the rich economies, and Europe thus stands expressly on the exposed side of the world map. Anyone who reads the report itself, however, finds a more cautious and at the same time more interesting message. What is measured is whether the technology could in principle take over the tasks of an occupation, not a forecast loss. On the potential for complementing work with the technology, by contrast, the two groups of countries are close together: in the poorer countries it is 16.2 percent of jobs, and according to the report's figure the rich ones lie only slightly above that. The World Bank's actual thesis is about the opportunities of developing countries, to which, in the words of its chief economist Indermit Gill, the technology has thrown a lifeline that they should seize. The low exposure of the poorer countries follows the same logic that Rodríguez-Pose and Bartalucci described for Europe's regions, only on a global scale: it measures not resilience but distance from the knowledge economy, and whether this distance means protection or exclusion depends, in the report's reading, on electricity, connectivity and skills, not on the technology itself.[148]

For the questions of this report, a secondary finding of the World Bank report is almost more revealing than its headline. According to the first evidence it gathers, the introduction of generative AI reduced the number of job postings in both China and India, in both cases with disproportionate force among highly educated entry-level workers, and in India in precisely those office occupations that are most accessible to the technology and least complemented by it. The World Bank itself notes that these findings are consistent with the American pattern among 22- to 25-year-olds in the most exposed occupations. The figure it gives for this, however, it attributes to a different study; it corresponds to the value of a 2025 preliminary version of the Stanford study, whose headline figure the authors have since replaced.[148][4] The career gap, the second force of this report, thus appears independently in two labor markets that share little with the American business cycle, the American interest rate cycle and the American debate on education, and that supports an uncomfortable conjecture: the gap does not depend on geography but on how widely the technology has spread. Where the tools are used densely enough, the entry level is hit first, on whatever continent, and the European window of time discussed above would then indeed be a window and not a license.

What this diffusion looks like in a single European country can be demonstrated with Austria since the summer of 2026, because the AI-Econ Lab at Örebro University, whose exposure index plays a role of its own in the next chapter, now publishes its indicators.[101] Three mutually independent surveys produce a picture that does not fit together on its own. The share of employees whose occupation falls in the most exposed quarter of all occupations is 32 percent in Austria, just above the mean of 30.2 percent across 36 countries, but lower than in Germany at 35 and Switzerland at 36 percent. The share of companies actually using artificial intelligence stands at 30 percent according to the Eurostat survey for 2025, well above the European Union average of 19.9 percent and above the German figure of 26 percent. The share of job postings that ask for AI skills, by contrast, is 0.8 percent, third from last among 22 measured countries against a mean of 1.9 percent. Austrian companies therefore use the technology more often than average and still post almost no jobs that ask for skills in it. The three numbers count different things, namely employees, companies and postings, and cannot be set against one another; taken together, though, they describe exactly the situation this report calls the long rebuild. The tools have been bought, the work has not yet been reorganized, and as long as it has not been, the technology shows up neither in the job profiles nor in the productivity figures.

> "AI skills are asked for in 0.8 percent of Austrian job postings, third from last among 22 measured countries."

The measurement of Austria's specialist AI workforce by the team at the Vienna University of Economics and Business, already introduced in the chapter on the career gap, adds a fourth measurement to this triad that explains why it is so hard to resolve.[50] Counted at the level of individual employees, Austria's specialist AI base is both unusually high in quality and unusually narrow. Measured by the share of specialized roles among all AI roles, the country ranks third out of 38 European countries, behind Germany and Switzerland; measured by density, that is, AI specialists per 1,000 employees, only 19th. At the same time, 97 percent of the companies observed in the panel employ nobody at all in an AI role, and even in the size class of 11 to 100 employees only a good one in eight companies has someone in such a role. That gives the contradiction in the triad an explanation: a country whose companies use the technology more often than average but employ almost no staff of their own to carry it is using it as a bought in tool, not as a capability of its own, and consequently has little reason to post jobs for it either. The numbers come from the same panel of profiles from professional networks and are to be read accordingly as values of that panel; they do not merely confirm the reading of the long rebuild, they add to it the observation that the rebuild cannot even begin without people of one's own.

How far this reading has already worked its way into official prediction arithmetic is shown by a look at the medium term prediction issued by the Austrian Institute for Advanced Studies in July 2026. In its section on America, it names productivity gains from the use of artificial intelligence as the most important future growth driver of the United States, with cited estimates of between half a percentage point and one and a half percentage points of additional annual productivity growth, factored into expected growth of 1.8 percent per year. In the prediction for its own country, by contrast, the same technology does not appear in the baseline scenario at all, but shows up only in the risk section as an opportunity that could improve the outlook, while Austria's trend productivity for the coming five years is set at 0.1 percent per year, after the country's total factor productivity has not grown at all since 2023. Whether the American calculation is too optimistic or the European one too cautious will only become clear later; what is telling is the asymmetry itself, because the same economists credit the same technology with a measurable contribution to growth on one continent and with none as yet on their own doorstep. The lag in diffusion is therefore no longer only in the investment figures, but in the growth arithmetic of the predictions themselves.[102]

> "The same institute names artificial intelligence as the most important future growth driver for the United States; in the prediction for its own country it appears only in the risk section as an opportunity."

The real European controversy therefore has to be sought where it is actually being fought out, namely in the individual DACH countries themselves.

---

<a id="WAAI-2026-C14"></a>
## The real European controversy is being fought out in the individual countries, not in the aggregate figures of the union

On German soil at least part of the American pattern is taking shape, even if the attribution of causes remains as contested as in the United States itself. The German unemployment rate among graduates climbed from just under 3 percent in 2024 to just over 3 percent in 2025, crossing that threshold for the first time since 2007, with around 335,000 unemployed graduates and an increase of 16 percent over the previous year. The Federal Employment Agency itself, however, names not artificial intelligence but economic weakness and structural change as the cause, and a good fifth of the increase is due to refugee migration, while graduates continue to hold the lowest unemployment rate of all qualification groups.[96] The job board StepStone (stepstone.de) reports for the first quarter of 2025 a share of entry level positions that is 45 percent below the five year average, a so-called junior paradox, yet this figure too is overlaid by the weak economy and cannot be cleanly attributed to the technology.[103]

Since early August 2026 this German picture can also be drawn occupation by occupation. The Ingenieurmonitor, compiled by the Verein Deutscher Ingenieure (VDI) together with the Institut der deutschen Wirtschaft, counts a good 58,000 unemployed people in engineering and IT occupations for the fourth quarter of 2025, the highest level since the survey began in 2011 and an increase of 16.7 percent over the previous year, while the number of open positions fell by 18.2 percent to just under 97,000.[104] What is revealing is how unevenly this decline is distributed, because it follows the same pattern as in the United States: in computer science occupations a third of the postings disappeared, in civil engineering occupations not even 4 percent, and supply shrank most sharply in Bavaria and Baden-Württemberg, where the ailing vehicle industry shapes demand.[104] The Federal Employment Agency reports the same picture for IT occupations, the stock of registered vacancies fell by 22 percent in 2025 to around 13,000, and the occupation specific unemployment rate rose from 3.7 to 4.5 percent.[105] Both institutions nevertheless read their own figures explicitly as a consequence of the business cycle and not of the technology. The VDI warns against deriving a falling need for technical specialists from the slump, and the Federal Employment Agency points out that employment continues to grow, to 1.15 million ICT specialists in jobs subject to social insurance contributions in 2025, 2 percent more than the year before. What its executive board chair Andrea Nahles describes alongside this, however, is not a mere cyclical trough but structural change made up of rising skill requirements, more academic qualification and more specialization, in which experts with an unemployment rate of 3.5 percent are barely affected.[105] On this reading demand is not disappearing, it is shifting toward the specialists.

How German companies themselves answer the questions that were also put to American ones has been shown for the first time since the spring of 2026 by a survey involving several central banks. For the working paper "Firm Data on AI" by Yotzov, Barrero, Bloom, Bunn, Davis and others, with which the first chapter already documented the expectation gap between leadership and workforce, the Deutsche Bundesbank put the same questions in the same wording to firms in its online panel in January 2026 as the Federal Reserve Bank of Atlanta, the Bank of England and an Australian university did in their panels. Around 2,500 companies responded. The German picture is subdued throughout. 65 percent of German firms use at least one AI technology, against 78 percent in the United States. 95 percent report no effect on their employment over the past three years, against 89 percent there, and for the coming three years German firms expect a considerably smaller change in their employment than American ones, which on average reckon with minus 1.2 percent, something the authors cautiously connect to the more heavily regulated German labor market. Up to this point the measurement supports the argument of the following chapter, according to which protection against dismissal makes the same shock arrive more quietly in Europe. The same damping, however, has an uncomfortable flip side. On productivity, German firms expect growth of 0.9 percent over the coming three years, American firms 2.3 percent and British firms 1.9 percent, and 91 percent of German companies have so far observed no productivity effect at all. What appears as protection on the employment side appears as a lag on the productivity side, and precisely this lag touches on the converse of the demographics chapter, according to which the technology can offset the departure of the baby boom cohorts if bold investment is made now. The report lets this tension stand instead of smoothing it over. The expectation gap between 0.9 and 2.3 percent is perhaps the single most precise figure for the delay hypothesis of the previous chapter, and it says nothing about whether the lag can be made up, only that the companies themselves are already reckoning with it.[54]

The methodologically most convincing and at the same time most European evidence comes from Switzerland. The KOF study by ETH Zurich explicitly replicates the Stanford method and shows that the number of registered job seekers in occupations highly exposed to AI has risen by up to 27 percent more than in occupations with low exposure, an effect that applies particularly to younger job seekers. For those under 50 in highly exposed occupations the number of unemployed has even more than doubled since the autumn of 2022.[106] At first glance this is contradicted by the European Central Bank, whose analyses of its own SAFE business survey from two survey waves in 2025 show no significant difference in the hiring balance between firms that use AI and those that do not, with a slight tendency even toward hiring rather than dismissing.[107] Both investigations are methodologically sound, and both simply measure at different levels, the Swiss study at the occupations of job seekers, the central bank at the hiring balance of firms across the entire euro area.

> "In occupations highly exposed to AI the number of unemployed people in Switzerland grew up to 27 percent faster than in occupations with little exposure, and among those under 50 it has more than doubled since the autumn of 2022."

How quickly this controversy falls back into the confusion of exposure with replacement, against which the studies on the career gap warn, could likewise be observed in Switzerland in early August 2026. The Tages-Anzeiger laid the Dynamic AI Occupational Exposure Index, an annually updated exposure measure from a research team around the AI-Econ Lab at Örebro University, which also includes the Kiel economist Holger Görg,[108] over 391 occupations in the Swiss labor market and asked in its headline how replaceable one's own occupation is by artificial intelligence and which occupations are most at risk.[109] That is precisely what the index, on the explicit statement of its authors, does not answer, because it captures the potential applicability of the technology to the content of an occupation and leaves open whether displacement, complementarity or nothing at all follows from it, since that depends on how widely the tools spread, on complementary skills and on the decisions of organizations. The case becomes revealing because the same team used register data covering almost all firms in Denmark, Portugal and Sweden over two decades to test what consequences exposure, measured with its own index, has for employment. According to the authors' summary this analysis found no systematic effect on the total employment of more heavily exposed firms, but it did find a consistent upskilling, because demand shifts from simple clerical work to highly qualified white collar work.[108] For the European controversy this is revealing in two respects. Alongside the central bank's hiring balance, there is now a second firm based European measurement that shows no volume effect but a structural effect, and the Swiss case at the same time demonstrates how a measure of possibilities turns into a headline about risk. The article itself does concede that a high index value does not mean a large risk of dismissal, but what remains is the ranking of risk, not the qualification.

Together with the Austrian finding already mentioned, according to which only a very small share of the open positions advertised as data or AI roles goes to people at the entry level[47], what emerges is indeed a distinct version of the American dispute between Stanford and the Federal Reserve, fought out on European soil, and not a mere copy of it. It is most clearly visible in Switzerland, in Germany more hinted at than proven, and it has still not reached the official EU statistics.

---

<a id="WAAI-2026-C15"></a>
## Dismissal protection is the structural reason why the same shock lands more quietly in Europe

Why the same technology lands markedly more quietly in Europe than in the American wave of layoffs described in the first chapter is explained not by the technology itself, but by a structural feature of the continent: dismissal protection. The relevant OECD index does exist in several, in part contradictory versions, yet the basic picture is stable across all of them. In the classic version, the United States, with an index value close to zero, comes last among all 42 countries covered, while Germany stands at about 2.5, Austria at about 2.25 and Switzerland at about 1.5, against an OECD average of a little over two. In almost every American state an employment relationship can be ended at any time and without statutory severance, whereas in Germany the Dismissal Protection Act applies after half a year of service in companies with more than ten employees, with graduated notice periods and a de facto severance standard, and in Austria there are graduated notice periods as well as the continuously accrued new severance system.[110]

The most convincing empirical evidence for the different European adjustment path is short-time work. In Germany it reached its peak in April 2020 with about 6 million employees, in Austria about one million people, just under 30 percent of everyone actively employed in the country.[111] The result was that Europe kept unemployment low in one of the sharpest economic crises of recent history, while in the United States it briefly shot up into double digits. The same logic applies to any technological shock that an AI could set off. Where American firms lay people off quickly and hire again just as quickly, Europe holds on to staff through short-time work and is pushed harder by law toward retraining than toward separation, which is consistent with an older McKinsey survey from early 2018, to be cited with caution, according to which 94 percent of the European executives surveyed, against 62 percent of the American ones, rely on retraining rather than on new hiring, a figure from the time before generative AI that nonetheless shows the same structural pattern.[112] Exactly this difference is the reason why the wave of layoffs described in the first chapter has so far run in a more muted way in the DACH region than in the United States, not because European companies were more restrained, but because quick dismissal is made harder for them legally and financially.

What is remarkable is that this reading is by now being confirmed from outside, and from the direction one would least expect it. The American entrepreneur Dan Shapiro, head of the laser device maker Glowforge, reduced the decision facing companies with more productive employees to three options at the end of July 2026. The first is to do nothing, the second to pursue the same targets with half the workforce and pocket the savings, and the third to keep the workforce and double the output instead. And he expressly records that countries with strict dismissal protection do not have the second option at all, which is why only the third is left to them. What counts in the European debate as a disadvantage for the location thus appears from an American point of view as a forced advantage, and the question Shapiro attaches to it is the genuinely interesting one: who wins when a halved company competes against a doubled one?[114]

The thought should not be confused with its premise, though. Shapiro reckons with a doubling of individual effectiveness, and for that there is no evidence worthy of the name. The two field experiments that the chapter on the long rebuild cites by name arrive at a quarter to two fifths in time savings on individual, clearly defined tasks, and in the same trial they show that the success rate outside that range falls by 19 percentage points instead of rising.[9][61] What remains is not the number but the switch. In Europe it is set differently than in the United States, and what set it there was not insight but the law.

> "What remains is not the number but the switch. In Europe it is set differently than in the United States, and what set it there was not insight but the law."

This switch does not have to be set by law. It can also be set in a collective agreement, and there it happens earlier. On August 21, 2026, about 39,000 members of the Korean Metal Workers' Union KMWU stopped work for a full day at the Hyundai plants in Ulsan, Asan and Jeonju, the group's first full-day strike since September 26, 2016. Alongside the usual items of a pay round, 149,600 won more in monthly base pay and a bonus equal to 30 percent of 2025 net profit, two demands were on the table that took the conflict beyond an ordinary bargaining round. The first was raising the retirement age from 60 to 65. The second required that humanoid robots reach the assembly lines only after an agreement between the company and its workforce. The demand becomes concrete once one sees who owns the machines. In July 2026 Hyundai (hyundai.com) acquired the remaining shares in Boston Dynamics (bostondynamics.com) from SoftBank (group.softbank) for 325 million dollars, at a valuation of about 3.3 billion dollars, and plans to deploy Atlas robots at its American plant in Georgia from 2028. The company countered that the use of robots was not a subject of collective bargaining.[144]

A say in technical change is not new in itself; in Germany the Works Constitution Act has governed it for decades. What is new is the subject and the place. The demand was for a say not in the consequences of an introduction but in the introduction itself, and it was made at the factory gate rather than in the statute book. While the European legislator, with the Digital Omnibus that entered into force on July 27, 2026, postponed the binding obligations for high-risk applications at the workplace, expressly including selection, performance monitoring and termination of employment, by 16 months to December 2, 2027, consent itself was already on the list of demands in Ulsan.[145] The demand for a higher retirement age fits this pattern too, even though it first looks the wrong way round. Whoever fears automation would intuitively ask for an earlier exit. Here it is the reverse, and the obvious explanation is that the existing employment relationship is the safest place available and that every year in it is one year less on a labor market whose condition nobody knows.

The conflict has since been settled, and the outcome is more sober than the escalation in August suggested. A few days after the full-day strike, in the night to August 25, 2026, the company and the union reached agreement, and in the ballot on August 31 the members accepted the deal with 61.55 percent of the votes cast.[146] On the retirement age the union received a conditional commitment: Hyundai raises it from 60 to 65 as soon as a corresponding change in the law is passed for the whole country, and the existing arrangement under which pay declines in the final working years will not be extended for this.[147] On robots, by contrast, the union did not prevail. In place of the agreement it had demanded before humanoid robots are deployed came a joint statement that robotics and AI-controlled machines are necessary for the competitiveness and the survival of the company, combined with a commitment to inform each other openly about progress in new business areas and technologies and to respond jointly to the changes in employment and working conditions.[147] The workforce is therefore informed and involved in the consequences, but its consent to deployment is not required. For IG Metall, the UAW and every other union facing the same negotiation, a first comparable case now exists.

---

<a id="WAAI-2026-C16"></a>
## Demographic pressure inverts the whole starting question

While the American debate assumes a labor market that has too many workers, workers a machine could make redundant, Europe faces the opposite starting position, and this inversion is the real reason why the job killer story stands on weaker legs here than elsewhere. According to the most recent release from the Federal Statistical Office in June 2026, Germany will lose around 13.3 million people in the labor force to the statutory retirement age of 67 by 2040, that is 30 percent of all people in the labor force who were available to the labor market in the year before, and the younger cohorts cannot make up this loss in sheer numbers. The two oldest cohort groups alone, those shortly before retirement, comprise around 10 million people in the labor force, more than the entire group of 25 to 34 year olds, and the share of those over 55 in the total labor force has risen from a little over a fifth in 2015 to more than a quarter today. [17] The old-age dependency ratio has been rising for years across the entire European Union, but the effect is felt most where, as in Germany, a single, exceptionally large generation leaves as a block within a few years. That this decline is not a distant projection but has already begun is recorded by the Institute for Employment Research of the Federal Employment Agency in its labor market prediction, most recently confirmed in spring 2026: in 2026 Germany's labor force potential falls for the first time ever, by around 40,000 people, because the exit of the large birth cohorts is for the first time no longer offset as immigration weakens. [115]

> "By 2040 Germany will lose 30 percent of its labor force to retirement, and the younger cohorts cannot make up this loss in numbers."

Austria shows the same picture on a smaller scale. According to a December 2025 study by the consultancy Prognos (prognos.com), the number of people of working age falls from around 5.5 million in 2023 to around 5.3 million in 2035, and if the economy keeps growing over this period as expected, around 246,000 workers will be missing by then, an order of magnitude also supported by a study by the Vienna Institute for International Economic Studies commissioned by the Ministry of Economic Affairs, according to which around 540,000 workers will leave working life through the retirement of the large birth cohorts between 2022 and 2027 alone. [116][117] How directly this mechanism already shapes the current decade is shown by the medium-term prediction of the Institute for Advanced Studies from July 2026. It expects the Austrian unemployment rate to fall from 7.5 percent this year to 6.4 percent by 2030, even though the economy grows by only one percent a year on average over the same period, because the supply of labor barely rises any more for demographic reasons and the working-age population, according to the underlying projection by Statistics Austria, grows at all only through immigration; without immigration it would shrink by 2030. The shortages of skilled workers, the institute states explicitly, will intensify further. In this prediction the Austrian labor market therefore does not ease because enough new jobs are being created, but because the people who could fill them are becoming scarce. [102] Switzerland too is aging in every population scenario of the Federal Statistical Office, and in the more cautious of these scenarios the domestic working population could no longer be sufficient from the early 2050s onward to cover the economy's demand. An analysis by the umbrella business association economiesuisse, to be classified accordingly as the voice of an association, puts the gap at around 431,000 missing workers by 2040, that is around 8 percent of those employed today, with the peak of the retirement wave in 2029, when for the first time more than 30,000 more people reach retirement age than young ones move up. [118] Since spring 2026 the federal government itself has stood behind this diagnosis, because a report based on a study by the State Secretariat for Economic Affairs and adopted by the Federal Council in April names demographic aging as the main cause of the Swiss labor shortage. [119] For comparison, in the United States the population keeps growing well into the 2030s according to the projections of its own statistical agency, because immigration there is increasingly becoming the load-bearing engine of growth and keeps the share of people of working age stable, while Germany, Austria and Switzerland are structurally heading toward a shrinking working population without comparable immigration reliably offsetting it. [120] In the speech already mentioned, Bundesbank President Joachim Nagel named exactly this possibility of cushioning demographic pressure, because AI-driven productivity gains could offset or at least soften the losses from the decline in the working-age population. [66] The starting question of the job killer story therefore poses itself exactly the other way around, because the real problem of an economy that loses almost a third of today's labor force over the coming decade and a half is too few workers, not too many.

**[Figure 8: The departure of the large birth cohorts hits all three countries.]**

> "While Germany, Austria and Switzerland are heading toward a shrinking working population, the American one keeps growing well into the 2030s."

These country figures still say nothing about who the departure hits first, and that is exactly where the picture becomes interesting, because it contradicts the common assumption. The official age structures of the three countries line up into a remarkably stable ranking. At the top everywhere is public administration, in Germany with 29 percent of employees over 55 against 23 percent in the economy as a whole, in Austria with 24 percent and, with nearly 150,000 people affected, at the same time the largest absolute stock of any sector, in Switzerland with 22 against 19 percent. Behind it come transport and storage as well as utilities and waste management. At the lower end stands the same group in all three countries, namely information and communication, hospitality and business services, and the distance is considerable, because Austrian information technology services come to 10 percent and thus to less than half the top value. Contrary to expectation, health and social services is not at the top but, in Germany, exactly at the average, because its gap arises not from departures but from the simultaneous additional demand, which in the predictions of both countries accounts for by far the largest increase in employment. [121][122][123]

> "At the top everywhere is public administration, it loses staff to age-related departures first and most."

Most revealing is construction, because it shows that the sector level is too coarse. The construction industry is not above average in age in any of the three countries, in Germany it is below the average at 21 percent, in Switzerland likewise at 18 percent, and in Austria civil engineering stands almost at the end of the entire list among those 60 and older. Go one level deeper, to occupations, and the picture flips completely. In building construction supervision, 41 percent of employees are 55 or older, in civil engineering 37, and there, according to the same analysis, nine out of ten open positions can no longer be filled. Logistics shows the same pattern, where the driving occupations are at 40 percent and above, while the sector as a whole is only moderately above the average. It is not the sectors that are aging, but their management and supervisory levels. From this follows a feature of the European picture that has so far received the least attention. The demographic departure hits first and hardest the work that depends on experience, supervision and local knowledge, that is administration, construction site management, driving, maintenance as well as utilities and waste management, and it hits last the work where artificial intelligence is put to use first. Where the people disappear, the technology reaches least far, and where it reaches furthest, the fewest people disappear. [121][122][123]

> "It is not the sectors that are aging, but above all their management and supervisory levels."

That this ranking also shapes companies' wage expectations is shown from the other side by a survey by the ifo Institute from June 2026, because construction companies are the least likely to expect that the use of artificial intelligence will push wages down, at 39.0 percent for short work experience and 31.3 percent for long work experience, while among service providers the figures are 53.3 and 44.2 percent, and conversely it is precisely the construction firms that most often expect rising wages for their qualified staff. Where experience becomes scarce, companies trust the technology least to make it cheaper. [49]

<a id="WAAI-2026-C16.1"></a>
### Looking ahead

It is precisely here that artificial intelligence could turn from the supposed problem into the answer. If the DACH region loses millions of workers over the coming decade and a half, then a technology that raises productivity per head and takes over routine work is not a threat scenario but the most likely instrument for maintaining the prosperity of a shrinking working population, exactly the cushioning effect the Bundesbank describes. [66]

> "If the DACH region loses millions of workers, the technology is not a threat scenario but the most likely instrument for raising productivity per head and maintaining prosperity despite a shrinking working population."

This hope is of course tied to a condition, and it is not met as long as the productivity effect stays as small as the most cautious calculations show it to be. In his macro analysis, the economist Daron Acemoglu arrives at an increase in total factor productivity of 0.53 to 0.66 percent over ten years, that is around 0.06 percent a year, and the budget model of the University of Pennsylvania puts the contribution of artificial intelligence to American productivity growth for 2025 at 0.01 percentage points. [29] Magnitudes like these do not offset the loss of a substantial part of the labor force. The European calculation therefore only works out if the technology reaches the upper end of the published estimates, and it is thus less a prediction than a task.

The window for this, however, is not a comfortable one. The large birth cohorts that leave by 2040 carry a large part of the experiential knowledge of these economies, and only while they are still in the business can this knowledge pass into processes, systems and digital team members instead of leaving with them. Whoever invests boldly while the experienced are still there turns demographic pressure into the strongest reason to invest in this technology that exists in Europe. Whoever waits until the gap is there will no longer fill it with any machine, because by then no one will be left who can teach it what to do.

> "The large birth cohorts that leave by 2040 carry a large part of the experiential knowledge of these economies, and only while they are still in the business can this knowledge pass on."

---

<a id="WAAI-2026-C17"></a>
## European sovereignty is a strategic lever, not a compliance argument

From the same speech by Nagel, the keynote "Already Here, Not Yet Everywhere" of April 2026, follows the last and least discussed building block of the European picture, namely that the much invoked European sovereignty in artificial intelligence is not a compliance argument but a genuine strategic lever. For the year 2024, the Stanford AI Index counts 40 significant American AI models against 15 Chinese ones and merely three European ones, and the capital gap behind that has widened further since. According to the 2026 edition of the same index, private AI investment in the United States reached around 286 billion dollars in 2025, against around 12 billion in China and around 21 billion in all of Europe. These figures capture only private equity capital in AI firms, that is, venture capital and stakes, and not yet the infrastructure spending of the large cloud groups, which at an estimated 725 to 805 billion dollars for 2026 alone is in a category of its own and is borne overwhelmingly by American corporations, as the tech chapter of this report shows. The Chinese state, for its part, directs capital through its state funds on a scale that this private statistic does not capture at all, an estimated 184 billion dollars cumulatively since the turn of the millennium. [99] What this gap feels like inside a single company is demonstrated by the SAP case described in the first chapter, because Europe's largest software group is financing its AI offensive not out of the strength of a capital market but out of the hiring, travel and supplier budgets of ongoing operations. At first glance these figures are sobering for Europe, yet Nagel himself expressly does not conclude from them that Europe must capitulate, and instead arrives at the assessment that the industrial AI race is open, because European and especially German industry can build on a wealth of data for training highly specialized models, with vendors such as Mistral AI (mistral.ai), Black Forest Labs (bfl.ai) or Aleph Alpha (aleph-alpha.com) and a whole series of barely known but internationally leading hidden champions in the background. [66]

> "SAP, Europe's largest software group, is financing its AI offensive not out of the strength of the capital market but through savings in ongoing operations."

The difference from the usual European discussion is telling. Where sovereignty is mostly negotiated in public as a question of data protection and regulation, Nagel argues economically, namely that Europe's real raw material is not sheer computing power, in which it is structurally behind, but the depth and quality of industrial data, in which the continent has a genuine lead precisely because of its old, broad industrial base, if it knows how to use it. Even the productivity estimate Nagel cites, according to which a rapid diffusion of AI could bring the G7 states between eight tenths and one point three percentage points of additional annual productivity growth over the coming decade, comes from the most favorable of three scenarios in the underlying OECD study, whose full range across all three scenarios runs from 0.4 to 1.3 percentage points, a nuance that Nagel selectively omits. The condition under which the estimate holds at all matches the diagnosis of this report, namely that organizations actually have to learn to work with the technology instead of merely buying it. [66]

How hard European policy finds it to raise this raw material where it actually lies is shown, of all things, by the answer Brussels has found to its own competitive weakness. The Union's financial framework for the years 2028 to 2034, proposed in July 2025 and at just under 2 trillion euros the largest in its history, shifts the weights clearly toward competitiveness, defense and strategic autonomy; the new competitiveness fund alone, together with the doubled research program Horizon Europe, bundles around 409 billion euros, of which a good 51 billion is for digital leadership. The already mentioned paper "A Union under siege, or a Union besieging itself?" by the regional economists Andrés Rodríguez-Pose and Federico Bartalucci, who write as committed advocates of European cohesion policy and whose thrust must be read accordingly, nevertheless demonstrates that this architecture, conceived along sectoral and national lines, will with high probability direct its capital to where innovation systems are already dense, to Île-de-France, Upper Bavaria, Stockholm or Hamburg, and that the same few metropolitan regions will at the same time gather the defense and the autonomy gains, while cohesion policy, the only instrument that has so far reached the remaining regions, is dissolved into national plans. Should the proposal be adopted in this form, the European budget of the coming years will therefore reinforce precisely that split between capital corridors and the provinces which regional research has described for two decades as the real European pattern. [100]

For the question of this report there is a double point in that. The industrial depth of data on which Nagel's confidence rests sits precisely not in the metropolitan corridors favored by the proposed financial framework, but in the mid-sized industrial regions in between, where the hidden champions have their plants and their process data and where at the same time hardly any of the new funding windows reaches. But if the European capital of the coming years flows above all to where strong ecosystems already stand, then the combination of artificial intelligence and a company's own domain expertise will not arrive in these regions as a program, neither from Brussels nor from the respective capital. Building this capability therefore remains, once again more sharply, a task for the individual company, and the sovereignty the European discussion talks about is decided less in the funding architectures than in the question of whether the companies of the industrial middle can turn their lead in data into productive practice themselves.

How the sovereignty question lands on the side of the state itself is shown by the same Capgemini Research Institute survey of 600 public sector executives that the chapter on the long rebuild draws on for the gap between trial and operation. 70 percent of respondents say political leadership drives the agenda, and 65 percent rank sovereignty as equal in importance to cost, performance and innovation. The barriers they name, however, are not technical ones: 84 percent cite a shortage of domestic technical skills, 74 percent interoperability problems and unclear or fragmented rules and responsibilities, 72 percent high costs and the difficulty of quantifying the return on such investments. Here too the caveats of self-assessment and the publisher's own interest apply. The ranking of the barriers, though, matches the line of this chapter: it is not the availability of models or computing power that first limits European sovereignty, but the skilled people and the organizations that would have to put them into operation.[172]

Alongside capital and education, a third axis of the sovereignty question is currently opening up, governance, that is, the question of who writes the rules by which the coming models are measured. How far from Europe these rules are taking shape is shown by a proposal that Demis Hassabis, Nobel laureate and the most prominent European born mind in top level AI research, made public in July 2026, at the time still as head of Google DeepMind, since August 2026 as chief scientist of the parent company Alphabet and chairman of the board of Google DeepMind, to be read as the positioning of a leading market player on its own behalf, not as neutral analysis. Modeled on the American financial regulator FINRA, he sketches a largely industry financed testing body that would examine the most capable AI models for dangerous capabilities before release, whose review would later become a condition for access to the American market, expressly independent of the model's country of origin, and which in an emergency could initiate a coordinated slowdown of development. Should this path prevail, the binding standards of AI development would arise in an American led, industry borne institution, and on this third question of its sovereignty Europe would again stand only at the margin, as it already does on capital and on education. [124]

On the question of how sound such self regulation by the industry would be, there has been an independent answer since the beginning of July 2026, though from an organization that is itself a party to the matter. The Future of Life Institute, an American organization founded in 2014 that advocates strict regulation and a slowdown of AI development, organized the 2023 call for a development pause and owes its assets largely to a donation from Ethereum founder Vitalik Buterin, had a seven member expert panel grade nine leading model vendors for the summer edition of its AI Safety Index, and the verdict is sobering. Not a single vendor gets beyond a C+, which in the American grading system corresponds to a bare satisfactory, roughly a three on the scheme customary in Germany; the leading vendors have weakened or in effect abandoned their earlier commitments to pause on reaching red lines; and at Google DeepMind of all places the panel points to the gap between the reassuring public communication of the leadership and the political conduct of the group. The panel's conclusion is that the credibility of safety precautions for the most capable models can no longer rest on self regulation but needs external verification, and this in the same month in which Hassabis proposes an industry financed form of self regulation. For Europe the index holds a point of its own, which the panel itself calls the European dissonance. While the Union leads the world in AI safety regulation, Mistral, the most important European AI company, lands in last place among all nine vendors examined, though Mistral has publicly disputed the evaluation logic. The grades are averaged expert judgments on the basis of public information, not audits, yet the finding fits the diagnosis of this chapter, because here too the ability to build safe models proves to be an achievement of the organization, not a result of the regulation that surrounds it. [125]

How deep the European gap actually runs is shown by a look at the universities, and it also shifts the familiar picture of the triangle of AI powers. In university research China is by now at least on a par with the United States; according to the Stanford AI Index it leads in AI publications with a good 23 percent of the world share, in citations with just under 23 percent and in granted patents with almost 70 percent, and according to a Fortune analysis from November 2025 Tsinghua University in Beijing has produced more of the 100 most cited AI papers than any other university in the world. The American lead in frontier models, by contrast, rests not on its own young talent but on immigration. According to MacroPolo's Global AI Talent Tracker, 59 percent of the world's top researchers work at American institutions, yet most of them were trained elsewhere, above all in China, where 38 percent of the top researchers earned their first degree and only 11 percent have stayed in the country. Europe's position in this triangle is contradictory. Its universities are not the problem; their share of the world's cited AI papers, at just under 21 percent, is even ahead of the American 13 percent, yet the continent subsequently loses those it has trained to American industry, which according to industry reports pays 30 to 70 percent higher salaries for comparable roles, and it hardly converts its research into models and firms of its own. [126] The European deficit therefore does not begin in the lecture hall but behind it, in holding on to people and in making use of the knowledge, and it is thus the same capability question this report diagnoses at the level of companies, only one level higher. How far Europe has already been pushed to the edge in the American imagination is shown, incidentally, by the leading future scenarios of the US discussion themselves, because both the scenario AI 2027, read millions of times, in which a group around former OpenAI researcher Daniel Kokotajlo played out the development month by month up to a superhuman AI in April 2025, and the policy draft AI 2040 added by the same group of authors in July 2026 conceive the coming order of artificial intelligence as a purely bilateral arrangement between Washington and Beijing, in which Europe no longer appears even as an actor. [127][128]

> "59 percent of the world's top researchers work at American institutions, but most of them were trained elsewhere, above all in China with 38 percent."

> "Europe's universities provide just under 21 percent of the world's cited AI research, more than the American ones, yet the continent subsequently loses those it has trained to American industry."

> "The leading American future scenarios conceive the coming order of artificial intelligence as purely bilateral between Washington and Beijing; Europe does not appear in them even as an actor."

> "The European deficit does not begin in the lecture hall but behind it, in holding on to people and in making use of the knowledge."

This closes the circle back to the demographic argument of the previous section. Europe has fewer workers but more industrial data than its competitors would have one believe, and which of the two forces prevails in the end will be decided not by the number of models but by the speed at which European companies actually turn the knowledge they have into productive practice, the question this report as a whole comes down to.

Alongside capital, education and governance, the sovereignty question is decided on a fourth axis as well, the political economy of acceptance, that is, the question of who in an economy has a tangible interest in the technology succeeding and who in its failing. The sharpest formulation of this question so far comes from Julia Willemyns, cofounder of the British think tank Centre for British Progress and thus herself a voice of the technology friendly camp, in an essay from July 2026 on the new Luddites. Her observation starts where the comparison with the Luddites of the early nineteenth century usually ends. The English weavers who destroyed the new looms in 1812 were put down by the military, because they had no political voice and the English elites profited from industrialization as a bloc. This time, by contrast, the upheaval hits the occupations of the well organized middle, from law firms through newsrooms into public administration, that is, groups that have professional chambers, associations and voting power behind them, and by Mancur Olson's classic logic the well organized groups on which the costs are concentrated regularly prevail in a democracy against the many across whom the benefits are spread thin. For the United States, Willemyns nevertheless expects that the resistance can grow loud and still lose, because capital markets, the federal budget and geopolitics there are by now so tightly coupled to the success of the technology, down to direct federal stakes in key firms, that the country's governing interests hang on its succeeding. Europe lacks exactly this counterpart: there is no European equivalent to the large technology groups and no treasury that would share appreciably in the AI surplus and could compensate the losers of the rebuild out of it, but there is a decision making architecture full of veto points. On this reading, the capital gap with which this chapter began would be not only an innovation problem but also an acceptance problem, because where no gain of one's own arises there is nothing to distribute, and the political space belongs to those who have something to lose. [129]

> "The weavers were put down because the English elites profited from industrialization as a bloc. This time the upheaval hits occupations that are well organized and have associations and chambers behind them."

> "Europe lacks the counterpart: no large technology group, no treasury that would share appreciably in the AI surplus in order to compensate the losers of the rebuild."

What does not appear in this calculation, however, is demography, and with it the account lacks precisely the factor that actually distinguishes Europe's political economy from the American one. A labor market that, by the figures in this part, is running out of people simply produces fewer losers to be compensated than the blockade thesis assumes, and an employment system that legally pushes toward retraining rather than separation turns potential objector groups into participants in the rebuild. Whether Europe has the political economy most prone to blockade or the one most capable of consensus for this technology is therefore not a settled matter, but depends on whether the introduction of digital team members in the company is experienced as an answer to the shortage of workers or whether the shortage merely serves as a pretext for it. In the end this too is not a question of regulation but of leadership in the companies themselves.

What Willemyns describes as a political observation, Susan Athey of Stanford University and Fiona Scott Morton of Yale University, both former chief economists of the Antitrust Division of the U.S. Department of Justice, have worked through as an economic model in the National Bureau of Economic Research volume. They treat artificial intelligence as an input that a country buys from abroad at a price, and ask what happens when the supplier has market power. Domestic companies cut their costs with the technology and replace part of their workforce, but the fees flow abroad, and domestic prices do not reflect the fall in labor costs. Under certain conditions this hits displaced workers twice, first through falling real wages while the technology is cheap, and a second time when the supplier raises its prices; in the unfavorable case the welfare of the entire country falls. For a country, the authors argue, what matters decisively is where the value added of artificial intelligence accrues, and its bargaining power vis-à-vis suppliers depends on its own alternatives, which dwindle when the purchased technology displaces capabilities of its own that can be rebuilt only at great expense. [193] Europe does not appear in the paper, and it is a model, not a measurement. For a continent that obtains the most capable models predominantly from the United States and that, according to Willemyns, lacks a treasury sharing in the technology's surplus, the model nevertheless describes the situation more precisely than the usual discussion about regulation. In this calculation, sovereignty helps decide where the gain from the technology remains, and thus also whether something arises in one's own country with which the losers of the rebuild can be compensated.

<a id="WAAI-2026-C17.1"></a>
### Looking ahead

If one puts together the building blocks of this part, the missing collapse in the Union's aggregate figures, the separate controversy in the individual countries, dismissal protection and demography, then a possibility emerges for Europe that does not appear at all in the imported American discussion. Europe could become the first continent to introduce artificial intelligence not as an instrument of downsizing but as an answer to a labor market that is running out of people. The ingredients for that are in place: an employment system that legally pushes toward retraining rather than separation, an industrial base of data whose depth the competition cannot copy, and a demographic pressure under which any productivity raising technology no longer appears as a threat scenario but as a promise of relief.

> "Europe could become the first continent to introduce artificial intelligence not as an instrument of downsizing but as an answer to a labor market that is running out of people."

What is missing is not further regulation and not a European frontier model either, but the organizational ability to bring these ingredients into ongoing operations, that is, the same ability which, by everything this report shows, even the most capital rich are only now working on. In this one respect the race does not begin for Europe from behind, but with an open outcome.

---

<a id="WAAI-2026-C18"></a>
## Nine predictions for 2027 that this report will grade publicly next year

This chapter is the part of the report that turns it from a snapshot into a series. It contains nine predictions about the coming year, each worded so that in the fall of 2027 it can be said without ambiguity whether it came true, each with the measure against which it will be tested, and each as a personal bet by the author, not as the output of a model. The 2027 edition grades them publicly, counts the hits and the misses, and explains what was wrongly reasoned in the misses. Alongside them, the chapter keeps a block of external benchmarks, meaning already dated predictions by vendors, market researchers and researchers on the same subject, with the source, the target year and the stated self-interest. They do not replace a prediction of one's own, but they will be graded along with the rest in the following year, because nobody learns anything from a debate in which new forecasts are made every year and the old ones are forgotten. Where an external prediction names no measurement criterion, that is noted, and the grading is tied to observable indicators that the report itself defines.

The predictions follow the order of the report, from the loud first force to the organization. They are the personal view of the author, formed from the findings of this report, and they state what he himself, given the evidence, considers the most likely course of the coming year. They are deliberately cautious where the evidence is thin, and deliberately sharp where the report commits itself. Anyone who thinks they are wrong will find in each one the place where that will show in 2027.

<a id="WAAI-2026-P01"></a>
**First prediction: the AI argument remains a front rank reason for layoffs in the United States in 2027, while the officially measured effect stays small.** In the layoff statistics of Challenger, Gray & Christmas, the technology will again be among the three most frequently cited reasons in at least six months of 2027, and at the same time fewer than 10 percent of AI using companies in the business survey of the American Census Bureau will continue to report a reduced headcount. The prediction is a bet that the story and the measurement keep drifting apart, because the AI argument is an instrument of capital market communication and not a description of the operating business. It will be tested against the monthly Challenger reports and against the next AI supplement to the Business Trends and Outlook Survey.

<a id="WAAI-2026-P02"></a>
**Second prediction: at least three well known companies publicly reverse an AI justified hiring freeze at the entry level by the fall of 2027.** The thinned out pipeline of their own junior talent and the rising cost of experienced specialists force the change of course, and it will not be carried out quietly but announced as a program, as Cognizant and EY already demonstrated in 2026. It counts as a hit when a company with more than 10,000 employees publicly announces that it is explicitly withdrawing or reversing an earlier reduction of entry level positions that was justified by the technology; programs that were never preceded by a freeze do not count.

<a id="WAAI-2026-P03"></a>
**Third prediction: the American entry level slump will again not appear in the official series of Germany and Austria in 2027, and the delay persists.** Unemployment among people under 25 with a university degree will not rise more steeply than overall unemployment in either country, and the continuation of the Swiss KOF measurement remains the only European finding in which exposed occupations perform measurably worse. The prediction is the delay hypothesis from the chapter on the European yardstick in testable form; it is refuted if the Bundesagentur für Arbeit or the Arbeitsmarktservice reports a disproportionate increase among young graduates in exposed occupations for 2027.

<a id="WAAI-2026-P04"></a>
**Fourth prediction: the gap between pilot and production does not close in 2027.** In McKinsey's annual survey series, the share of companies that attribute an effect on the operating result to the technology will stay below 45 percent, after 37 percent in 2026, a value that McKinsey itself reports as unchanged from the prior year. The group with a measurable contribution to operating profit of at least 5 percent will stay below 10 percent, after around 6 percent. Adoption will keep growing, the effect will not grow to the same degree, because organizations learn more slowly than the vendors deliver. It will be tested against the 2027 edition of the same series, with the caveat that these are self reported answers from a survey run by a consultancy that sells rebuild programs.

> "Organizations learn more slowly than the vendors deliver, and the group with a noteworthy contribution to operating profit stays below 10 percent in 2027 as well."

<a id="WAAI-2026-P05"></a>
**Fifth prediction: at least five well known companies in the German speaking countries announce a reorganization by the fall of 2027 that structures units around topics and outcomes instead of functions.** The organization of the future, described this year as a target picture by consultancies, market researchers and tool vendors, reaches practice first in companies with a high share of knowledge work and light regulation, and it will be announced publicly there, because it can be marketed as a competitive advantage. Announced reorganizations count as a hit when small units take over responsibility for the entire process, as do the corresponding answers in the 2027 board pulse, into which the question about the division of work will be taken up as a core question.

<a id="WAAI-2026-P06"></a>
**Sixth prediction: at least one publicly visible large scale attempt to flatten hierarchies and align the organization around AI agents will be partially withdrawn by the fall of 2027.** Block and Meta are the first two documented cases in 2026, one still being executed, the other already partly reversed, and they will not be the last, because in most cases the coordination work that was cut turns out to have been quietly shifted rather than saved, following the pattern of Zappos and Medium. It counts as a hit when a company with more than 10,000 employees publicly withdraws or suspends an announced reduction of hierarchy; a mere slowdown does not count.

<a id="WAAI-2026-P07"></a>
**Seventh prediction: the gap between productivity and wages widens further in the early years of the technology.** In the American series of the Economic Policy Institute and the Bureau of Labor Statistics, productivity will grow more strongly in 2026 than the hourly wage of typical employees. In the German speaking countries, the spread between entrants and experienced staff in companies' wage expectations will not narrow if the survey is repeated; the ifo Institute measured this spread for the first time in 2026. The prediction puts the distribution question of the closing chapter into testable form, and it is refuted if wages keep pace with productivity in 2026.

<a id="WAAI-2026-P08"></a>
**Eighth prediction: the AI argument does not arrive in the German speaking countries in 2027. No company in Germany, Austria or Switzerland with more than 10,000 employees justifies job cuts of more than 1,000 positions primarily with artificial intelligence by the end of 2027.** What has been part of capital market communication in the United States since 2025 fails to appear in the German speaking countries, and not because the technology is used less here, but because the argument yields nothing here. Where job cuts have to be negotiated with a works council, quantified in a social plan and justified before labor courts, invoking the technology is a poor justification, because it invites the question of which task exactly the machine has taken over. The job cuts that come in 2027 will be justified with the business cycle, costs, location and restructuring, the way SAP ran its restructuring of around 10,000 positions as a realignment and, in July 2026, saved on hiring, travel and suppliers in order to finance its own AI push, without attributing a single job to the technology, and the way Ingka retrained 8,500 call center employees instead of laying them off. It will be tested against the mandatory disclosures of listed companies, against press releases on restructurings, and against the social plans and mass layoff notifications of 2027 that become public. A counterexample counts only if the company itself names the technology as the main reason in its own announcement, not if the press attributes it to the company; a single counterexample refutes the prediction.

<a id="WAAI-2026-P09"></a>
**Ninth prediction: the gender gap in the professional use of generative AI does not close in 2026.** In Eurostat's statistics on ICT use in households, men in Germany use generative AI at work 29 percent more often than women, 18 against 14 percent. In the annual figure for 2026, which Eurostat publishes in December 2026, this lead remains above 20 percent, even though women aged 16 to 24 were already ahead of men of the same age in 2025. The gap arises in the workplace, and in 2026 companies are not reorganizing their training fast enough for a single age cohort to close it. It is measured by the ratio of the professional usage rates of men and women between 16 and 74; the prediction is refuted if the men's lead falls to 20 percent or below. Austria runs alongside as a control series; there, the lead stood at 30 percent in 2025.[6]

The author considered two further predictions and did not include them, because their measure will not yet be cleanly available in 2027. According to the first, the midterm elections in the United States in November 2026 become the first election in which concern about AI driven job loss is a load bearing campaign issue. According to the other, at least two further large commerce platforms follow Shopify's example and report AI mediated orders as a separate metric. They stand here so that the 2027 edition can also check whether leaving them out was right.

<a id="WAAI-2026-C18.1"></a>
