The table grows with every edition, and no row is deleted. Anyone who puts a number into the world in 2026 will find it here again in 2027, next to what became of it.

---

<a id="WAAI-2026-C19"></a>
## The future of work is decided not by the machine but by the company's ability to work with it

This report began with the promise of breaking a headline down into its components, and at the end of that path the great misconception can be pinpointed exactly. The job killer story makes the machine the actor, and that is precisely what does not hold up against any of the three forces examined here. Behind the AI argument stand capital market logic and restructuring, for which companies borrow a technological justification. The career gap is real and measurable, yet even where the technology demonstrably plays a part, it hits a stage whose value companies determine through their own hiring practice. And the long rebuild, the actual effect of the technology, is stalling precisely not on the capabilities of the models but on the ability of organizations to put them to work in ongoing operations. For all three forces the investigation arrives at the same result: employment is decided by people and organizations, not by the autonomy of a technology.

How far from self-evident it is that the gains of this rebuild reach the people who do the work is shown by the longest series that labor statistics offer. Since the 1970s, productivity in the United States has grown considerably faster than real hourly wages, and the same pattern runs through the United Kingdom, Japan and large parts of Europe; the Economic Policy Institute puts the gap for the period since 1979 at a productivity gain of a good 92 percent against a gain of just under 34 percent in the hourly wage of typical workers. Should artificial intelligence further strengthen capital's share of value creation, there is much to suggest that this gap will widen rather than close; the Financial Times recently distilled this concern into the formula that the productivity gains of the technology could flow above all to the owners of the machines and data centers. Erik Brynjolfsson, whose Stanford lab measured the career gap, answers in the same piece with a distinction that confirms this study's central thesis from the economic side: whether the technology replaces people or complements them is a question of design and not a property of the machine. The trap he warns about he already called the Turing trap in 2022, the building of systems that imitate people instead of complementing them. For this study's question that means: the distribution of the gains, too, is not a natural constant of the technology but a consequence of decisions, in companies as in politics, and the rebuild will be measured by whether it not only raises productivity but also shows up in the wages of those who carry it. [131] That this concern is no preserve of voices critical of capitalism is shown by the fact that the economics department of a German state bank explicitly assumed in August 2026, in all of its own scenarios, that without state intervention the lion's share of the higher labor productivity does not flow to employees in the form of rising wages but increases the profits of capital owners. [33]

That this distribution question is not a distant one is suggested by an analysis that came out of the financial industry in July 2026 and has so far remained without a European counterpart. Torsten Slok, chief economist of Apollo Global Management (apollo.com), and the Princeton researcher Sania Edlich compared wage developments from 2015 to 2026 for 321 matched American occupations, and they determined exposure not theoretically but by the observed occupational use of Anthropic's models. In the highly exposed occupations, real wage growth since 2023 has been 6.7 percent lower than in the weakly exposed ones, while employment in the same occupations has remained unchanged, and the gap widens toward the bottom: in the lowest wage quartile it is around 11 percent, in the two middle quartiles 5.4 and 4.0 percent, and among top earners it is no longer detectable. Should this finding hold, the effect of the technology on the existing workforce shows up not in the job but in the price of labor, and it shows up first among those who earn the least. That is the same movement this chapter describes along the long series, only at the current edge and with the technology as the named cause, and it also supplies the series against which this report's prediction on wage developments can be checked next year. An honest reading includes the fact that the calculation here comes from the research department of an asset manager and that no peer reviewed work exists, that exposure is drawn from the usage data of a single vendor and the finding therefore must not be listed as an independent piece of evidence alongside that vendor's other analyses, and that the authors themselves leave open whether their values denote percent or percentage points, which is why the report adopts only the direction and the ranking by wage quartile. [137] It is notable that Benjamin Verschuere, one of the authors of the task decomposition cited earlier, arrives at this very question of his own accord in the same front page story when he notes that wage growth has become detached from productivity.[138] How far, the official statistics say: the labor share in the American nonfarm business sector, that is the share of value added that goes to employees as compensation, stood at 52.8 percent in the second quarter of 2026, lower than at any time since this series began in 1947, after a peak of 66.2 percent at the end of 1960.[139] That is no work of artificial intelligence, because the decline has been running since the early 2000s, but it is the starting position into which this technology acts, and it explains why the question of how the gains are distributed cannot be postponed to the day the rebuild is complete.

In September 2026 an AI vendor itself ran the numbers on how large the upheavals in the labor market could turn out to be. In a working paper from the Anthropic Institute, the company's research arm, the economists Anton Korinek and Charles Jones, together with further authors, sketch three scenarios for the American economy through 2030, explicitly without stating probabilities. The finding is simple. In the two scenarios with moderate and with substantial change, the unemployment rate in 2030 lies between 3.9 and 4.6 percent, that is, close to today's level, and the model arrives at the same values if, instead of the authors' assumptions, one enters the expectations from a representative survey of American adults. The image of the job killer emerges only in the extreme scenario, in which the economy grows by 15 percent a year, artificial intelligence handles nine out of ten work steps on its own and no new tasks arise for people; unemployment in office and knowledge occupations then rises to 17.9 percent. What does shift as early as the middle scenario is the distribution: the share of labor income in all income falls from 60 to 56 percent by 2030, and in the extreme case the entire increase flows to capital income. The model applies only to the United States, leaves political countermeasures out and rests in part on the vendor's own usage data, by the standard already presented in the chapter on the career gap. [40] The image of the job killer requires the most extreme assumptions of this calculation; for an unequal distribution of the gains the middle ones already suffice. [130] How far the expectations within the industry itself depart from this frame was supplied by the same research institution in October 2026 for its own workforce: 1,334 employees of the vendor answered the same questions in August as the public did, and if their expectations are entered into the same model, the public lands in the middle scenario and the workforce in the extreme one, at around 17 percent growth a year. The people who build the models thus expect the very scenario from which the job killer story draws its numbers; this report does not adopt that expectation as a forecast, because the confidence of a company's own workforce is at the same time its valuation story, but it does record that both groups derive the same unemployment from the same exposure and differ only in how much of their own work they trust the machine to handle by 2030: 75 versus 28 percent. [183]

What the distinction between employment and distribution looks like at the outermost end is shown by the most radical chapter of the National Bureau of Economic Research volume already drawn on several times. Pascual Restrepo of Yale University, a member of Anthropic's Economic Advisory Council since 2025, assumes an artificial intelligence that can perform all economically valuable work with computing power, and asks what then becomes of human work. His answer is not the end of employment. In his model people go on working, partly alongside the machines, partly in activities left to them alone because growth does not depend on them. What disappears is the economic weight of this work. Wages approach the cost of the computing power that would be needed to perform the same work by machine, and the share of labor income in economic output falls toward zero. Restrepo means the title of the chapter, We Won't Be Missed, literally: today a general work stoppage would bring the economy to a standstill, while in his world human work would hardly be missed. [194] This is a thought experiment about a technology that does not exist, and it contains no time frame. But it shows that even at the outermost end of the scenarios what stands is not mass unemployment but the question of who shares in the value added.

How far off the job killer story is shows most clearly where its proponents do not look at all, at new company formations. The study by Kim and Koning, which has already supplied evidence at two points in this study, contains a third finding as well. AI-native new companies are around a quarter smaller and about half a hierarchy level flatter than comparable firms in the same industry and the same founding cohort, and in doing so they reach comparable or higher valuations. One can read this as confirming the fear of shrinking workforces, but that misjudges what is actually happening at these companies. No work is disappearing here; new value creation is arising in a different organizational form, smaller in setup, staffed differently and geared from the start to the capabilities of digital team members. The work does not disappear, it changes place and shape, it migrates into products, into new firms and into roles that do not appear in the personnel statistics of existing companies. Anyone who only counts which positions are cut at established companies is taking stock of an economy's rebuild with the categories of its old state. [51]

That leaves the question this report put at the start and carried through every chapter: not whether artificial intelligence destroys jobs, but how fast an organization builds the knowledge with which it makes the technology productive. The evidence that the fault line runs exactly here has grown denser from chapter to chapter. The gap between pilot and production has been measured by three mutually independent findings. The delegation experiment by Colaiacovo and Koning demonstrates that the willingness to hand work over to an AI actor depends only to a good third on what one credits the technology with, and beyond that is a management decision in its own right. [7] The models themselves can by now be rented by any company, at falling prices and in increasingly interchangeable quality. The ability to work with them cannot be rented.

> "Any company can rent models. The ability to work with them every company has to build for itself."

This diagnosis no longer stands alone. In June 2026 the Stanford Institute for Human-Centered Artificial Intelligence presented an industry report that was produced within the institute's corporate-funded membership program and that condenses a research overview by more than 50 authors; its central message coincides almost entirely with this study's central thesis. Because the large foundation models are converging in performance, the difference between vendors shifts from the question of what the model can do to the question of how well it serves the people who work with it. Top scores in the usual performance tests accordingly guarantee no business benefit, productivity gains arise only when employees actually change the way they work, and the design of the collaboration between a human and an AI colleague often brings greater progress than switching to the next model. That of all places the academic home of human-centered AI research locates the competitive advantage where this report diagnoses the learning gap shows that the central thesis is no European peculiarity but reflects the state of the international discussion. [95]

International management scholarship has by now arrived at this point as well. Gary Hamel and Michele Zanini, whose work on organizational research has shaped the discussion for decades, measured the industry's three loudest promises against the evidence in June 2026, mass displacement, imminent general machine intelligence and the productivity revolution, and arrive at the same finding as this study: the evidence does not support the headlines, the gains are real where tasks are clearly delineated. Their most robust figure comes from the same calculation by the Penn Wharton Budget Model with which it was already shown above what condition the European hope for higher productivity is tied to. What is remarkable is less the agreement than the open flank, because they conclude from it that expectations must be recalibrated, and they attribute part of the gap, unlike this study, to the limits of today's models themselves. This study's findings go one step further: the gap between promise and effect is no fate of the technology but the distance between its capabilities and the ability of organizations to use them. That the most prominent critics of the hype arrive at the diagnosis and stop short of this explanation underlines why this report puts the capability question at the center. [29]

A sector-specific confirmation comes from the very industry that traditionally proceeds most cautiously with new technology. The study by Wavestone and the University of St. Gallen already cited in the chapter on the long rebuild, a survey of 30 business unit and IT managers from banks, insurers and technology vendors in the German-speaking region, draws a conclusion that points beyond the financial sector: what decides how far a company can actually scale agentic AI is not the capability of the models but the maturity of its operating model. That such a regulated and risk-aware industry arrives at the same conclusion as management research and academic AI research before it shows that this report's central thesis describes no isolated case of the long rebuild but a pattern that repeats across industries and continents. [68]

How far this fault line reaches beyond the individual company is shown by an essay that the economist E. Glen Weyl, the sociologist James Evans and the Microsoft research director Chris White published in July 2026 in the magazine Noema together with a 23-member coalition from research, business and civil society; two of the three authors do research for Microsoft, and the text nevertheless criticizes the investment logic of their own industry. For every dollar that flows into more capable models and their alignment with people, only a fraction goes into the ability of institutions, organizations and education systems to absorb these systems, and the authors call this neglected counter-direction reverse alignment. If it fails to come, three wrong turns loom, which the authors call productivity without prosperity, execution without review and capacity without limits, and the first of them is the same distributional flank that became visible above in the wage-productivity gap. Their diagnosis for the world of work repeats almost word for word the lesson this study drew from the introduction of the electric motor, since the real bottleneck for large productivity gains is not the model but the rebuild with which organizations align themselves to the possibilities of the technology. What this report describes as the capability of the individual company returns there as a task for entire societies, and both levels condition each other, because no company builds its capability more easily than in a country whose education, labor market and legal institutions support the rebuild. [132]

What the mechanism behind the second of these failures, execution without verification, might look like was worked through in February 2026 by the economists Christian Catalini of the Massachusetts Institute of Technology, Xiang Hui of Washington University in St. Louis and Jane Wu of the University of California, Los Angeles, in a theoretical model on which the Noema essay also draws. Two cost curves diverge in it: the cost of automating a task falls with every model generation, the cost of verifying a result remains tied to human attention, and the bottleneck of growth therefore moves from intelligence to verification capacity. Today's equilibrium, in which a human signs off on every action of the machine, erodes in this model from two sides at once: from below, because the entry-level tasks disappear on which tomorrow's verifiers would have learned their judgment, and from within, because experts turn their judgment into a template with every review, which the machine applies itself the next time; for the individual it then becomes rational to deploy unverified. The authors call the first the Missing Junior Loop and the second the Codifier's Curse. The career gap of the second chapter and the petition of Meta's workforce against the recording of their work in the first are, on this reading, two sides of the same mechanism. It is a model without data of its own, and the paper names cryptographic provenance, the research field of one of its authors, as a future source of value; as an explanation of why a company's capability lies in verification rather than execution, it is nevertheless the sharpest currently available. [204]

What such a national counter-direction could look like has been on view in the United Kingdom since the start of the year. There, the University of York, the higher education data provider QS and the consultancy Public First presented in January 2026 the attempt to quantify the education side of the capability question for an entire national economy, along the eight priority sectors of the British industrial strategy. Of the 1,890 occupations into which the underlying market model divides the British labor market, the analysis classifies 1,436 as critical for exactly one of these sectors and a further 635 as critical for several at once, whose scarcity would therefore slow several growth strategies at the same time, and more than 80 percent of the most important of these occupations require a university degree. The same model estimates the complementing potential of the technology to be considerably larger than its displacement potential, since three quarters of employees work in occupations whose work the technology can at least noticeably complement, while only half are exposed to automation to a comparable degree. The report's conclusion transfers this study's central thesis to the entire national economy, since employment growth will in future be less a question of demand in individual industries than the question of whether the workforce masters those tasks that the technology complements instead of replacing, which makes higher education and lifelong learning systemically critical infrastructure. An honest reading includes the fact that parties with interests of their own are doing the calculating here, a university in the funding crisis of its sector and a commercial higher education data provider, and that the most spectacular figure of the report, an AI growth contribution of 490 billion pounds by 2030, rests on the heroic assumption of up to two AI-supported working days per week and thus lies many times above the cautious productivity estimates with which it was shown above what condition the European hope is tied to. The basic statement holds even without this set of figures, however, since the government agency Skills England expects independently of it that two thirds of the additional employment in the priority occupations will require a qualification above school education, and precisely there lies the gain for this study's capability question: a country has begun to look for the bottleneck of its growth no longer in the technology but in the education of the people who are to work with it. [133]

By now it can also be read off how employees themselves experience this fault line. The second annual sentiment survey by Lenny's Newsletter among 5,332 working people in the technology industry, to be read as a self-selected mood picture strongly shaped by product management and by American respondents and not as a labor market measurement, describes a workforce that splits into two camps. 49 percent experience themselves as strengthened by the technology in their professional role, just under 19 percent as unsettled or devalued, and this self-placement predicts career optimism and the willingness to recommend one's own field more strongly than role, hierarchy level and company size together; for optimism, the distance between the strengthened and the devalued corresponds to about three times the effect that a founder role brings with it. At the same time, the share of respondents with pronounced burnout rose within one year from just under 45 to a good 55 percent. So the same question that divides organizations into learners and onlookers divides working lives as well, and what tips the scale is the experience of whether the technology carries you or runs you over. An organization that builds its capability thereby also decides on which side of this line its people stand. [28]

What this capability consists of at its core was already named in the chapter on the long rebuild; here the findings are brought together. Between the model and one's own process lies what matters: the company's knowledge, its rules, its experience and its decisions, held in a form that belongs to the company itself and that digital team members can work with directly, no matter which model happens to underlie it. Whoever owns this foundation can switch vendors like tools and takes their knowledge along every time. Whoever does not own it hands a piece of knowledge work to their supplier with every AI function they buy and calls that progress. The decision as to which work may migrate into the purchased product and which remains tied to one's own processes is made task area by task area and is therefore not a software question but the form in which a company's capability becomes concrete. It is remarkable that an entirely different investigation arrives at the same order of magnitude. When a research group from the Massachusetts Institute of Technology and Stanford University broke down the differences in the advice that language models give private individuals about their finances, there too only a third was attributable to the model itself and two thirds to how the people had asked.[81]

How rare this foundation still is today is shown by two surveys by Oliver Wyman from the summer of 2026, and they show at the same time that the executive floors already share the direction. Among the chief executives surveyed, only 4 percent expect a lasting advantage from privileged access to a leading model, whereas 74 percent expect it from redesigned processes and forms of organization and 64 percent from their own data and knowledge of their own customers. Preparation lags behind this insight. Only 10 percent of the technology leaders surveyed have set down in writing which data and assets remain in their hands if the main model provider doubles its prices or withdraws a model, and half of them estimate 21 to 60 person-days of development work for switching the base model in their main use case alone. The consultancy locates the dependency not in the model but in what grows around it, in the agents' memory, in project knowledge, in permissions and in the orchestration of workflows, that is, precisely in the layer that this report describes as the company's foundation. These are two parallel surveys of 200 executives in total by a firm that itself sells consulting on sovereignty in AI, and they measure assessments, not actual switches. [190]

How seriously this shift is now being taken is shown by the supply side of the model market itself. Thinking Machines Lab (thinkingmachines.ai), the company of former OpenAI technology chief Mira Murati, presented a manifesto in July 2026, a few days before the release of its first model, that locates its own business foundation precisely here. Organizations should train the models further with their own knowledge instead of buying an artificial intelligence that skims this knowledge off once and replaces it with a standard offering. That is a vendor text in its own cause and is to be read as such, yet when a vendor of top models looks for the future of its business where companies keep their knowledge in their own operations, the market is betting at this point on the same capability this study is about. [134]

For Europe this calculation turns out sharper, for better and for worse. The second part of this study has shown that the starting position is more favorable than the imported discussion suggests: a demography in which any technology that raises productivity does not appear as a threat but promises relief, an employment system that pushes toward adaptation instead of separation, and an industrial depth of data that sits in the mid-sized regions and cannot be copied. But all three advantages are conditional. They pay off only for organizations that build the capability to use them, and this work, as the look at the European financial framework has shown, is taken off their hands by no funding scheme. The location supplies the ingredients, but every company has to process them itself.

<a id="WAAI-2026-C19.1"></a>
### Looking ahead

When this assessment appears for the second time next year, it will ask the same question again, with the same core questions to the same group of decision makers, with the same metric for the distance between pilot and production and with the public grading of its own predictions. Only this recurrence turns a diagnosis into a series of measurements, and only the series of measurements will show whether the learning gap is closing or whether it is opening further month by month, like the American figures on the career gap.

The great misconception about the AI job killer will settle itself in the process, as technological figures of fear have always settled themselves, not through refutation but through precision. What remains is the question on which the coming years will be decided: which organizations have made productive digital colleagues out of a capable tool, which have rebuilt the entry level, because tomorrow's capability too needs entrants who are allowed to learn today, and which no longer need the AI argument because their numbers speak for themselves. To the question of whether artificial intelligence destroys work there is, at the end of this study, a precise answer: it depends on who leads it. That is not a reassurance but the most exact description of the situation that this year's evidence permits, and it places responsibility where it belongs, in the organizations themselves.

---

Data from Revelio Labs (public professional profiles, mostly LinkedIn, and job postings), 395,638 country-level affiliates in 41 countries, January 2021 to March 2026; estimation sample of 100,813 foreign affiliates, of which 26,102 affiliates of adopting companies and 27,697 control affiliates; adoption defined as at least one AI-related job posting after November 1, 2022, instrumented by the adoption rate of other multinational parents headquartered in the same metropolitan area. Entry-level covers the Revelio seniority levels entry and junior, experienced all levels above. Unrefereed working paper; core figures checked against the primary PDF; the country estimates for Germany, Austria and Switzerland are not significant.

The Digital Omnibus on artificial intelligence postpones the application date of the obligations for high-risk AI systems under Annex III of the AI Act, including selection and recruitment, promotion, performance monitoring and termination of employment, from August 2, 2026 to December 2, 2027. The AI Act itself has applied since August 2024; only the application date of these obligations is postponed, they are not abolished.

The figures (4.5 against 14.2 percent accessibility to automation, 16.2 percent potential for complementarity in low- and middle-income countries, p. 48; the value for high-income countries is shown only graphically in Figure O.6) measure the accessibility of task profiles to generative AI, not a forecast loss of jobs; the methodological basis includes Gabriel Demombynes, Jörg Langbein and Michael Weber: "The Exposure of Workers to Artificial Intelligence in Low- and Middle-Income Countries", Policy Research Working Paper 11057, February 2025. The report cites the findings on China and India from Li et al. (2026) and World Bank (2025c) respectively. It attributes the American comparison figure (endnote 52) to "Brynjolfsson, Li, et al. 2025", which its bibliography lists as "Generative AI at Work" (Quarterly Journal of Economics 140, 2025), a study of customer service; by our own attribution the figure corresponds to a 2025 preliminary version of the Stanford study (source 4). The quotation from Indermit Gill appears in substance in the report's foreword (p. xvii) and verbatim in the World Bank press release of August 4, 2026 ("AI has thrown developing economies a lifeline, and they should seize it"), https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2026/08/04/ai-offers-lifeline-to-developing-economies-in-an-era-of-weak-growth.

Two separate panel surveys from January 2026 in ten countries, 1,303 HR professionals and 5,501 employees in organizations with 100 or more employees; self-reports without disclosed weighting or margin of error. Survey by a consulting firm that itself sells the redesign of the HR function. Year-on-year comparisons only as a direction, because the set of countries was expanded and the previous wave was fielded in December 2024. Observational analysis of 441 open-source projects; largely the same research group as source 152, so the two are not to be read as independent evidence.

<a id="WAAI-2026-Q155"></a>
155. Judy Hanwen Shen and Alex Tamkin: "How AI Impacts Skill Formation". arXiv:2601.20245, version of February 1, 2026, not peer reviewed, https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.20245. Preregistered randomized experiment with 52 developers, conducted in the Anthropic Fellows Program; the authors put the difference on the test at 17 percent (Cohen's d = 0.738, p = 0.010), the time difference is not significant. Produced in a vendor's orbit; the assistant tested was based on GPT-4o, not an Anthropic model. The breakdown by task type, in which debugging shows the largest gap, was not preregistered.

Communications of the ACM, Vol. 69, No. 4, Opinion, April 8, 2026, DOI 10.1145/3779312, https://cacm.acm.org/opinion/redefining-the-software-engineering-profession-for-ai/. Opinion piece, no survey and no measurement. Russinovich is Chief Technology Officer of Microsoft Azure, Hanselman Vice President of Developer Community at Microsoft; both work for a maker of the tools described. Used: "AI boost" for experienced engineers versus "AI drag" for newcomers, the formula "hire seniors, automate juniors" and the recommendation to keep hiring newcomers.

Analysis of more than one billion online job postings (Lightcast) from 27 countries, Austria not included; unreviewed in-house analysis by a consultancy that earns money from the transformation it measures. The calculation on entry-level positions with senior requirements (plus 35 versus minus 10 percent, 2019 to 2025) rests only on American postings in the top exposure quartile, and PwC set the thresholds itself. Job postings, not hires.

No survey of its own, but a synthesis of existing PwC and WEF data and anonymized interviews without stating their number or selection; Peter Brown is a member of the project team. The expectation of structural change comes from Finding 5 and Figure 8, based there on PwC's 29th Global CEO Survey (2026); it refers to change in both directions, growth or decline. Peter Brown's statement comes from a public LinkedIn comment thread of August 7, 2026, in reply to a question from the author, and is a personal assessment, not a finding of the report.

Aaron Chatterji, David Holtz, Neel Rakholia, Prasanna Tambe and Gawesha Weeratunga: "How Organizations Use AI: Evidence from ChatGPT". arXiv:2608.12236, August 12, 2026, not peer reviewed, https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12236. Figures 5 and 6, measured 26 weeks after rollout: weekly messages per active user compared with the average of the same company. 30 to 45 percent of users cannot be assigned to a seniority level. Company statement without external verification; program start January 2028, so an announcement and not yet practice.

<a id="WAAI-2026-Q163"></a>
163. Geoffrey T. Sanzenbacher: "Are the Careers of Older Workers Being Cut Short by AI?". Issue in Brief 26-13, Center for Retirement Research at Boston College, June 30, 2026, https://crr.bc.edu/are-the-careers-of-older-workers-being-cut-short-by-ai/. Short report by a university research center, peer review not stated. Data: Current Population Survey (IPUMS), employees aged 55 and over, employment status one year apart, first observations January 2014 to April 2025, excluding the pandemic period and the one-year intervals spanning the launch of ChatGPT. Exposure according to the Digital Planet AI Exposure Index (Tufts University, scale 0 to 100), which measures how well AI can perform the tasks of an occupation, not how easily it can be replaced. 11.7 versus 14.1 percent: Table 2, employees above and below the median of exposure, without controlling for characteristics. Change after the launch of ChatGPT: Figure 3 and Table A1, significant for transitions into unemployment, not for exits from the labor force or into retirement. The predictions for individual occupations in Figure 4 are model values and are not used here.

<a id="WAAI-2026-Q164"></a>
164. Seyed Mahdi Hosseini Maasoum and Guy Lichtinger (Harvard University): "Generative AI as Seniority-Biased Technological Change: Evidence from U.S. Résumé and Job Posting Data". SSRN Working Paper 5425555, first version August 31, 2025, authoritative revised version of June 6, 2026 (109 pages), not peer reviewed, https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=5425555. Résumé and job-posting data (Revelio Labs, from LinkedIn profiles, overrepresenting professional and managerial occupations) on around 65 million employees at 281,111 companies, 2015 to 2025; seniority according to the data provider's classification, not by age; shares refer to employment in the dataset. Adopters: 3.71 percent of companies with around 16 percent of employment (pp. 4 and 21). Decline in entry-level employment relative to non-adopters of around 9 percent after six quarters (pp. 29 and 94, Figure A.21; the triple difference entry-level versus experienced in Figure 5, p. 31, also yields around 9 percent); concentration in exposed occupations (Figure 6); hiring versus separations according to Table 4 (4.0 fewer newcomers hired, 1.1 fewer separations per company and quarter); task analysis according to Table 1 (difference significant only at the 10 percent level, around 1,000 companies). The reduction in hiring of around 80 percent stated by the authors (p. 40) relates the 4.0 missing hires to the pre-period mean of all companies in the sample (5.06 hires per company and quarter), which are mostly considerably smaller than the adopters (Table A.5); for the adopters, the reference value is likely higher and the percentage reduction therefore smaller, and the authors give no figure of their own for it. The number is therefore not used, nor is the reading spread in the media of "minus 80 percent per quarter since 2023" (e.g. Forbes, May 29, 2026). The figure of 13 percent often attributed to this paper comes from the Stanford series by Brynjolfsson, Chandar and Chen (source 4: 22- to 25-year-olds in the most exposed occupations, relative to trend), which the paper merely cites (p. 10).

The 18 largest call-center countries in three income groups; comparison with other office work, entry and exit rates and job transitions only for twelve middle-income countries; transitions only for workers who record a new job in their profile within twelve months. Official full count of employees subject to social insurance contributions on the reference date; classification by the economic branch of the establishment or by the activity performed; rates of change are our own calculation. 176. Jessica Apotheker, Vinciane Beauchene, Nicolas de Bellefonds, Dylan Bolden, Sylvain Duranton, Marc Roman Franke, Michael Grebe, Nina Kataeva, Santeri Kirvelä, Djon Kleine, Romain de Laubier, Vladimir Lukic, Amanda Luther, David Martin, Natasha Taylor, Jeff Walters, Rich Lesser and Christoph Schweizer: "The Formula for Agentic AI Value". Maturity scale built from self-assessment across 41 capabilities, statements on benefit self-reported, no regional breakdown, not peer-reviewed. Cross-sectional survey with self-reported use; the values describe associations, not effects. Following the OECD's own note (Box I.2.2), differences between user groups are not to be converted into school years.

<a id="WAAI-2026-Q179"></a>
179. Chris Adams, Arjun Singh Banga, Parveen Bansal and 28 further authors, among them Audris Mockus, Peter Rigby and Nachiappan Nagappan (all Meta): "Automating Low-Risk Code Review at Meta: RADAR, Risk Calibration, and Review Efficiency". arXiv:2605.30208, first version May 28, 2026, authoritative is the revised version v2 of June 12, 2026, not peer reviewed, https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30208. Company publication about its own system, measurements from production, no raw data and no dates; changes were not randomly assigned to RADAR, so the comparisons of reverts, incidents and cycle times are observational and shaped by the preselection of low-risk changes. The attribution of more than 80 percent of the growth to AI agents is a company figure without a disclosed method. The reduction in time to close "by over 330 percent" stated in the abstract is not adopted, because a reduction by more than 100 percent is not possible; what is evidently meant is that human-reviewed changes take several times as long. No independent second source, only reproductions of the paper.

<a id="WAAI-2026-Q180"></a>
180. Andrej Karpathy: post on the platform X, October 11, 2026, https://x.com/karpathy/status/2109361546505966046. Update of his own post from April 2026. Field report: napkin math by an individual without a disclosed data basis; all tiers and figures (around 6 billion, 1 to 2 billion, around 20 million, around 5,000) are estimates, and the percentages add up to around 95 percent. Karpathy has worked in model development at Anthropic since May 2026 (TechCrunch, "OpenAI co-founder Andrej Karpathy joins Anthropic's pre-training team", May 19, 2026, https://techcrunch.com/2026/05/19/openai-co-founder-andrej-karpathy-joins-anthropics-pre-training-team/), that is, at one of the companies whose internal systems he describes. Wording from a transcript of the post dated October 15, 2026, checked against the reproductions in Benzinga and The New Stack of October 12, 2026; the platform X could not be accessed directly for the research.

<a id="WAAI-2026-Q181"></a>
181. Microsoft: "The continued state of global AI diffusion in 2026". Microsoft On the Issues, September 21, 2026, https://blogs.microsoft.com/on-the-issues/2026/09/21/the-continued-state-of-global-ai-diffusion-in-2026/. The metric is the share of people aged 15 to 64 who used a generative AI product during the reporting period, estimated from aggregated and anonymized Microsoft telemetry and adjusted for operating system and device market shares, internet penetration and population; frequency and intensity of use are not distinguished (method: Misra et al., arXiv:2511.02781). Microsoft itself sells generative AI tools and holds a stake in OpenAI. The report for the second quarter could not be accessed in the original for the research; the figure of 18.8 percent is supported by two independent reproductions, and the previous quarter's figure of 17.8 percent was checked against the original (Microsoft, "AI Diffusion Report 2026 Q1", May 2026, https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/wp-content/uploads/2026/05/Microsoft-AI-Diffusion-Report-2026-Q1.pdf). The conversion to around one billion people is this report's own calculation: 65.3 percent of the 8.30 billion people in 2026 are between 15 and 64 years old (United Nations, World Population Prospects 2024, medium variant).

<a id="WAAI-2026-Q182"></a>
182. Nathan Wilmers, Regina Catipon, Maxim Massenkoff and Peter McCrory: "How employment is changing at Anthropic". The Anthropic Institute, Technical Report, October 16, 2026, https://www.anthropic.com/research/employment-at-anthropic, PDF at https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/72628f1863c53432195db83279252dfd2cb53158/Employment%20at%20Anthropic.pdf (20 pages). Company publication about its own organization: internal personnel, hiring, calendar, Slack and agent session data plus 17 employee interviews; no raw data, no peer review, not verifiable by outsiders; the publisher sells the agents described. Values used: share of entry-level employees in technical departments 21 to 14 percent since early 2025, share of new hires 27 to 9 percent; agent runtime six to one (compute runtime including tool use, excluding waits over 30 minutes, humans at 40 hours a week per head); around 80 percent waiting time in interactive sessions; steering work 10 to 22 percent (machine coding of a sample of 2,000 session summaries); reading time calculated from word counts (0.75 words per token, 250 words per minute); meeting time plus 20 and plus 16 percent from calendar data. Productivity comparisons before and after adoption (192 versus 74 engineers) are not randomly assigned and are used only as an indication; the fivefold code output per engineer is not adopted as evidence of productivity. No independent second source, only re-reports of the paper.

<a id="WAAI-2026-Q183"></a>
183. Szymon Sacher, Tess Cotter, Charles I. Jones, Anton Korinek and Peter McCrory: "The AI Expectations Gap: Public and Frontier Lab Views on AI's Impact on Work". The Anthropic Institute, Technical Report, October 16, 2026, https://www.anthropic.com/research/ai-expectations-gap, PDF at https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/a28f24814c56e2834a3eae7b13946f19d3e97ec4/The%20AI%20Expectations%20Gap.pdf (16 pages, appendix 6 pages). Survey by Morning Consult, 10,980 American adults, August 11 to 23, 2026, weighted; internal survey of 1,334 Anthropic employees, August 9 to 12, 2026, unweighted; model calculation with the scenario model from entry 130 for 3,259 adults and 1,093 employees. Values used: the same slope in both groups (0.4 points of unemployment per 10 points of own exposure); own work that AI could handle by 2030, 75 versus 28 percent; model values of 17.1 versus 5.4 percent growth, carried as model output, not as a forecast or as the respondents' expectation. A vendor surveying its own workforce about its own technology; the workforce's headcount expectations are not adopted.

<a id="WAAI-2026-Q184"></a>
184. Chris Schmitz (Centre for Digital Governance, Hertie School), Lewis Hammond (Cooperative AI Foundation) and Alan Chan (Centre for the Governance of AI): "Characterizing Agentic Flooding of Government Services". Proceedings of the AAAI/ACM Conference on AI, Ethics, and Society (AIES), vol. 9, no. 4, pp. 2266 to 2278, 11 October 2026, DOI 10.1609/aies.v9i4.43305, https://ojs.aaai.org/index.php/AIES/article/view/43305; preprint arXiv:2608.16603v2, 19 August 2026, https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16603; dataset https://github.com/CLSchmitz/flooding-dataset. Case collection from twelve countries (excluding Austria and Switzerland), 2,288 candidates, 84 cases in eleven countries, gathered with language models and checked by humans, only 25 cases with a time series. Values used: 69 percent attribution by the authority itself; 87 percent text volume from language models; 90 percent more complex, 60 percent more numerous submissions; response in 56 percent of cases, own AI tools in 25 percent. The authors rule out causal and quantitative claims and assess the operational risk as not severe; the expectation that, without a redesign, public administration will grind to a halt wherever it must examine freely worded submissions individually is this report's assessment, not the authors'. The attribution of a 55 percent rise at German social courts to AI goes beyond the cited source (see 185).

Values used: urgent proceedings at the eight social courts in North Rhine-Westphalia up more than 55 percent to 7,615 in 2025, part of this attributed by president Martin Blüggel to AI briefs by laypeople, with the decline in social law attorneys as background; average case duration in 2025 of 15.6 months, 1.3 months in urgent proceedings; a statement of grounds of 4,500 pages in an extreme case according to Federal Social Court president Christine Fuchsloch. Federal Administrative Court (Austria), decision of 1 September 2026, W298 2341849-1/19Z, Federal Legal Information System, https://www.ris.bka.gv.at/Dokument.wxe?Abfrage=Bvwg&Dokumentnummer=BVWGT_20260901_W298_2341849_1_00: fine of 200 euros for abuse of process under section 35 AVG in conjunction with section 17 VwGVG (maximum 726 euros), no appeal on points of law admitted; twelve statements from 20 April to 31 August 2026 totalling about 67 pages plus enclosures, according to the court evidently produced entirely with language models and, contrary to Article 50 of the AI Act, not labelled; reported by beck-aktuell, 5 October 2026, which overstates the volume as "several hundred pages". Swiss Federal Supreme Court, judgment 9C_235/2026 of 2 June 2026, according to Beobachter: "'Konturlos, austauschbar und ohne Substanz': Bundesgericht zerpflückt KI-Beschwerde", 14 July 2026, https://www.beobachter.ch/gesetze-recht/konturlos-austauschbar-und-ohne-substanz-bundesgericht-zerpfluckt-ki-beschwerde-951964; full text of the judgment not checked. Collection of 51 AI deployments in 41 organizations (23 from North America, seven from Europe, five from Asia, four from Latin America, two global), selected for operational maturity and quantified benefit, with at least one structured interview and internal documents per company; according to the authors a positive selection based largely on self-reported information and not representative. Headcount outcomes according to Figure 8 (p. 53): reduction 45, hiring avoided 19, no change 19, redeployment 17 percent. Hardest challenges according to Figure 5 (p. 14): change and adoption 33, data 17, processes 10 percent, technical and visible costs together 23 percent. The survey period is stated inconsistently in the document (p. 8 versus p. 57 and p. 76), and the case pages are marked as drafts. Brynjolfsson is a co-founder of the measurement company Workhelix. Discussed among others in Stanford GSB Insights ("Almost Every Job Has Tasks That AI Can Change", Lisa Bonos, 17 September 2026).

<a id="WAAI-2026-Q188"></a>
188. Agent Skills: open specification for capabilities of AI agents, originally developed by Anthropic, introduced in October 2025 and released as an open standard on 18 December 2025. Overview and specification at https://agentskills.io and https://agentskills.io/specification, accessed 19 October 2026; there, skills are described as packaging "procedural knowledge and company-, team-, and user-specific context into portable, version-controlled folders". Release as an open standard according to Frederic Lardinois: "Agent Skills: Anthropic's Next Bid to Define AI Standards". The New Stack, 18 December 2025, https://thenewstack.io/agent-skills-anthropics-next-bid-to-define-ai-standards/. Supported, according to the specification's overview and the vendors' own documentation, by ChatGPT and Codex (OpenAI: "Skills build on the open agent skills standard", https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/build-skills), Gemini CLI (Google), GitHub Copilot and Visual Studio Code (Microsoft) and Cursor, among others, accessed 19 October 2026. Statements by the standard and the vendors, not a measurement of adoption.

Eve by Eraneos, Eraneos Group, Zurich, first edition 2026, 76 pages, PDF dated 29 September 2026, published in early October 2026, download via a form at https://campaign.eraneos.com/ai-empowered-organization. Concept and experience paper by a consultancy with a direct business interest in the transformation it describes, not an empirical study; the organization as code with eight building blocks (actors, triggers, intents, skills, sources, artifacts, rules, audit) in version-controlled text files. Statements about its use in the firm's own operations are self-reports without independent verification; figures from the paper are not used in this report.

<a id="WAAI-2026-Q190"></a>
190. Oliver Wyman Forum: "The Reality Check". The AI Agenda 2026, 15 October 2026, 91 pages, foreword by John Romeo and Ana Kreacic. https://www.oliverwymanforum.com/artificial-intelligence/agentic-ai-strategy-governance-workforce.html, PDF at https://www.oliverwymanforum.com/content/dam/oliver-wyman/ow-forum/template-scripts/templates/ai-agenda/2026/pdf/The_reality_check.pdf. Data basis: Oliver Wyman Forum Global Voices Survey, two online waves in April (12,883 respondents) and August 2026 (12,837 respondents) in 15 countries, including Germany, excluding Austria and Switzerland; Oliver Wyman Forum x NYSE CEO Agenda 2026 (415 chief executives, January to March 2026, 56 percent North America, 32 percent Europe); two parallel surveys of 200 chief executives and technology leaders in total (July to August 2026). Self-reported information; according to the authors, the group comparisons do not establish a causal relationship. Time spent checking (22 minutes, mean, 7,845 respondents) and the allocation of time gained (54, 31 and 8 percent, 9,833 respondents) from the August wave; expectations by job level (p. 29) partly from April, partly from August. Oliver Wyman is a management consultancy of the Marsh group and sells AI consulting through its unit Quotient.

<a id="WAAI-2026-Q191"></a>
191. Sam Manning and Tomás Aguirre: "How Adaptable Are American Workers to AI-Induced Job Displacement?". In: Ajay K. Agrawal, Erik Brynjolfsson and Anton Korinek (eds.): The Economics of Transformative AI. National Bureau of Economic Research Conference Report, University of Chicago Press, Chicago, September 2026, chapter 8, pp. 197 to 218, with a comment by Dimitris Papanikolaou, pp. 218 to 223, ISBN 978-0-226-85408-3, https://www.nber.org/books-and-chapters/economics-transformative-ai/how-adaptable-are-american-workers-ai-induced-job-displacement. Working paper version: NBER Working Paper 34705, January 2026, https://www.nber.org/papers/w34705; accompanying analysis by Sam Manning, Tomás Aguirre, Mark Muro and Shriya Methkupally, Brookings Institution, January 21, 2026, https://www.brookings.edu/articles/measuring-us-workers-capacity-to-adapt-to-ai-driven-job-displacement. Exposure measure following Eloundou and colleagues (2024), employment from the official occupational statistics OEWS 2024. Not a measurement of an effect of the technology, but exposure combined with the preconditions for a change of occupation. The size of the group with high exposure and low adaptive capacity is given in the chapter itself (p. 197, checked in the digital edition on October 26, 2026) as in the working paper: 6.1 million employees or 4.2 percent across 356 occupations covering 95.9 percent of the US workforce; the chapter summary on the NBER website gives 3.3 million or 2.5 percent across 300 occupations, the editors' introduction 7.2 million. The report follows the chapter. The share of around seven in ten in the most exposed quarter with adaptive capacity in the upper half is stated as such in the Brookings analysis and follows from the chapter (26.5 of 37.1 million).

<a id="WAAI-2026-Q192"></a>
192. Erik Brynjolfsson and Zoë Hitzig: "AI's Use of Knowledge in Society". In: Ajay K. Agrawal, Erik Brynjolfsson and Anton Korinek (eds.): The Economics of Transformative AI. National Bureau of Economic Research Conference Report, University of Chicago Press, Chicago, September 2026, chapter 12, pp. 301 to 330, with a comment by Avi Goldfarb, pp. 330 to 335, https://www.nber.org/books-and-chapters/economics-transformative-ai/ais-use-knowledge-society; draft version of September 2025 at https://www.nber.org/system/files/chapters/c15303/c15303.pdf. The title alludes to Friedrich A. Hayek: "The Use of Knowledge in Society", American Economic Review 35 (4), 1945. Theoretical model of decision rights with examples from the time before generative AI (among them Walmart and concentration in American retail), not a measurement of an effect of AI. Brynjolfsson directs the Stanford Digital Economy Lab, which funded the work, and is a cofounder of the measurement provider Workhelix.

<a id="WAAI-2026-Q193"></a>
193. Susan Athey and Fiona Scott Morton: "Artificial Intelligence, Competition, and Welfare". In: Ajay K. Agrawal, Erik Brynjolfsson and Anton Korinek (eds.): The Economics of Transformative AI. National Bureau of Economic Research Conference Report, University of Chicago Press, Chicago, September 2026, chapter 4, pp. 95 to 114, with a comment by Catherine Tucker, pp. 114 to 117, https://www.nber.org/books-and-chapters/economics-transformative-ai/artificial-intelligence-competition-and-welfare. Working paper version: NBER Working Paper 34444, November 2025, https://www.nber.org/papers/w34444. General equilibrium model of an open economy that introduces AI as an input; not a measurement. Europe and the European Union do not appear in the paper; applying it to Europe is a reading of this report. Athey was chief economist of the Antitrust Division of the U.S. Department of Justice (in office according to the agency's page in 2023) and, by her own account, consulting chief economist of several technology companies; Scott Morton held the same position at the Department of Justice in 2011 and 2012.

National Bureau of Economic Research Conference Report, University of Chicago Press, Chicago, September 2026, chapter 9, pp. 225 to 242, with a comment by Neil Thompson, Danial Lashkari and Omeed Maghzian, pp. 243 to 250, https://www.nber.org/books-and-chapters/economics-transformative-ai/we-wont-be-missed-work-and-growth-agi-world. Working paper version: NBER Working Paper 34423, October 2025, https://www.nber.org/papers/w34423. Theoretical growth model without time frames and without wage levels. Restrepo has been a member of Anthropic's Economic Advisory Council since 2025, which he discloses himself in the working paper.

<a id="WAAI-2026-Q195"></a>
195. Melanie Arntz, Michele Dinelli and Oliver Schlenker: "KI als Jobkiller für Berufseinsteiger? Entwarnung mit Vorbehalt". IAB-Forum, Institut für Arbeitsmarkt- und Berufsforschung (Institute for Employment Research) of the Bundesagentur für Arbeit, October 13, 2026, DOI 10.48720/IAB.FOO.20261013.01, https://iab-forum.de/ki-als-jobkiller-fuer-berufseinsteiger-entwarnung-mit-vorbehalt/. Data basis: monthly administrative data of the Bundesagentur für Arbeit, January 2021 to December 2025, an average of around 22.3 million full-time employees subject to social insurance contributions aged 20 to 64, excluding civil servants, the self-employed, those in marginal employment only, and apprentices. Exposure measure following Tyna Eloundou, Sam Manning, Pamela Mishkin and Daniel Rock, "GPTs are GPTs", Science 384(6702), 2024, transferred to the German Classification of Occupations 2010 at the three-digit level. Poisson regression at quarterly level, reference period fourth quarter of 2022, 95 percent confidence intervals. Article in the institute's own journal with its own DOI, not peer reviewed. Accompanying press release "KI bisher kein Jobkiller, aber Berufseinsteiger zunehmend betroffen", October 13, 2026, https://iab.de/presseinfo/ki-bisher-kein-jobkiller-aber-berufseinsteiger-zunehmend-betroffen/. Second source for the base figure via Reuters, Holger Hansen, October 13, 2026.

<a id="WAAI-2026-Q196"></a>

OpenAI Economic Research, September 2026, 12 pages, second edition of the series, published September 16, 2026, https://openai.com/index/unlocking-new-ways-of-working/ , report at https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/work-at-the-frontier-report-202609.pdf. Data basis: more than 1.5 million work-related messages from individual ChatGPT accounts of users registered in the United States, April to July 2026, panel of around 6,200 continuously observed workers, occupational assignment via the O*NET activity catalogs and via the role or department information from business onboarding, nine occupational groups. The vendor's own measurement of its own product use, not peer reviewed; OpenAI expressly states that the sample is not representative of the American workforce. In this edition the series measures recurring out-of-field use and does not continue the cross-sectional share of 43.5 percent from the first edition (source 10); the two quantities cannot be set off against each other.

Series C, led by Khosla Ventures, with participation from Blackstone, Bain Capital Ventures, Coatue and Craft Ventures, among others. The affiliated law firm Norm Law, LLP states that it runs on the company's platform, with senior attorneys supervising and calibrating the agents, and that it prices by outcome rather than by the hour; clients with more than 30 trillion dollars in assets under management combined. Guideline from the senior engineering leadership (Distinguished and Senior Principal Engineers) of Amazon Stores on the role of software engineers: daily AI use is expected, non-use is to be documented and discussed; metrics such as token consumption, tool usage, code changes and reviews are described as error-prone when used as targets, but may be used with care in career and performance evaluations. Company source without figures; the claimed link to outcomes is not substantiated.

In addition, the first report by the Financial Times of October 7, 2026 on the planned cuts in the British wealth business, based on unnamed people and not read in the original, as reported by Reuters (Khaleej Times, October 7, 2026, https://www.khaleejtimes.com/business/hsbc-plans-job-cuts-across-uk-wealth-business-in-ai-push-ft-reports) and City AM (October 7, 2026, https://www.cityam.com/hsbc-set-to-axe-uk-wealth-jobs-as-ai-takes-hold/); figures on the scale vary between "up to" and "closer to" 70 percent. Confirmation of an ongoing staff consultation by HSBC to AFP, as reported by Malay Mail, October 27, 2026, https://www.malaymail.com/news/money/2026/10/27/hsbc-third-quarter-profit-jumps-455pc-but-misses-forecasts-amid-job-cuts-and-ai-push/240816. <a id="WAAI-2026-VERSION-HISTORY"></a>
## Version history

**Version 1.0, September 16, 2026.** First publication of the 2026 edition. English edition of the German original published on the same day.

**Version 1.1, September 18, 2026.** Gender chapter and key finding 5 switched to the Eurostat 2025 statistics on the use of generative AI (previously IAB/D21, survey of July 2024); ninth prediction on the gender gap at work added, the previously deferred prediction on the D21 series dropped; source 6 extended. Newly added as source 135, the Swiss SME labor market study 2026 by AXA and Sotomo: the expectation of job cuts among AI-using SMEs at 12 percent for three years (chapter on the AI argument), trial versus operation and the decline of text tasks after the 2025 peak (chapter on the long rebuild). The source numbers of version 1.0 remain unchanged; new sources are appended.

**Version 1.2, September 24, 2026.** The assessments from the author's working conversations have been dropped; since this version, the report relies exclusively on verifiable sources. The foreword and the method section have been adjusted accordingly, as have the affected passages in the chapters, in the predictions and in the final chapter. Newly added as sources 136 and 137: the task decomposition by Liminal Capital, according to which career entries of 22 to 25 year olds in substitutable occupations have fallen by about a third since 2021 while layoffs remained unchanged (chapter on the career gap), and the wage finding by Apollo Global Management, wage growth 6.7 percentage points slower in exposed occupations with employment unchanged (final chapter); as sources 138 and 139 the New York Times article of September 16, 2026 and the official labor share from the Bureau of Labor Statistics (52.8 percent in the second quarter of 2026, the lowest since the series began in 1947), which give the Amodei benchmark a first interim status and extend the distribution paragraph in the final chapter. Newly added as sources 140 and 141: the Indeed Hiring Lab analysis of the German entry-level job market since 2019 (entry-level postings 46 percent below 2019, apprenticeships 50 percent above, divergence by AI exposure since spring 2022) together with the Die Zeit article of September 18, 2026 with Melanie Arntz's assessment; author attribution corrected for source 58 (first author at the Indeed Hiring Lab). Method section: the rhythm of the report is now fixed. Three levels, the annual edition, the quarterly version and the analyses on workafterai.org, with the rules that predictions remain unchanged within an edition and a version appears only when a finding has changed or an error needs correcting; version 1.3 is planned for early November and the final version 1.4 for December 31, 2026. As of this version, the publisher is Kuerner Group GmbH, Wels; the author remains Gerhard Kürner. Work DOI, ORCID and the new citation recommendation are in the method section. With this version, the report also appears for the first time as a machine edition, that is as a text edition with a fixed identifier for every chapter, key finding, prediction, external benchmark and source, as data files and as a skill package; the files sit next to the PDF under the work DOI. The source numbers of version 1.1 remain unchanged; new sources are appended.

**Version 1.3, November 6, 2026.** Q4 Edition. New chapter “The pioneers already lead teams of agents and reorganize them with every new model” between the technology chapter and the organization chapter, written as a field report of the pioneers, not as a measurement; the field report as a text form and the naming of the versions (1.3 Q4 Edition, 1.4 Year-End Edition, quarter names from 2027) are set out in the method section. The key findings number eleven since this version: new is key finding 7, according to which in 2026 the human becomes the bottleneck wherever agents run in production; key finding 3 additionally cites Anthropic's hiring figures and the first German test of the AI attribution by the Institute for Employment Research; key finding 10 on marketing has been reworded and rests on sources 168 to 171. Newly added as sources 142 to 205: the Economic Letter of the Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco on transitions into employment (142), the analysis by Chandar and Klein Teeselink of AI adoption and labor demand in 41 countries (143), the Hyundai strike over the use of robots including the settlement and the Digital Omnibus (144 to 147), the World Bank's World Development Report 2026 (148), the McKinsey HR Monitor on mobility and the study by Künn-Nelen, Montizaan and Wittich (149, 150), the software strand as the cornerstone of the observation field software development (151 to 155), company voices on entry and experience (156 to 162), Sanzenbacher on older workers in exposed occupations (163), Hosseini Maasoum and Lichtinger on seniority-biased technological change (164), the field reports on working with agents (165 to 167), the marketing figures from Bitkom, Content Marketing Institute, Gartner and GWA (168 to 171), Capgemini on data and AI in government (172), the call center series from Revelio Labs, the Federal Employment Agency and the Bureau of Labor Statistics (173 to 175), the Applied AI Index of the Boston Consulting Group (176), PISA 2025 and the analysis by Lepe and Contreras (177, 178), Meta RADAR (179), Karpathy's observation on the funnel and Microsoft's AI Diffusion Report (180, 181), the two reports of the Anthropic Institute on its own employment and on the expectations gap (182, 183), the cases of AI-assisted floods of filings before courts and authorities (184 to 186), the Enterprise AI Playbook of the Stanford Digital Economy Lab (187), Agent Skills and the Eraneos playbook on the organization (188, 189), the Reality Check of the Oliver Wyman Forum (190), four chapters of the NBER volume The Economics of Transformative AI (191 to 194), the IAB test of the AI attribution at career entry (195), the second edition of OpenAI's Work at the Frontier (196), the company announcement by Norm Ai on legal work (197), Amazon's role description for software engineers (198), the CMO Report by Dentsu Creative (199), HSBC's quarterly statement (200), Block's shareholder letter and organizational program (201, 202), the field experiment at Procter & Gamble on collaboration with AI (203), the model by Catalini, Hui and Wu on the verification bottleneck (204) and Gartner's prediction of October 2024 as a further external benchmark (205). The chapter on dismissal protection carries a key sentence for the first time. Two figures are new, on the gender gap in the chapter on the gender axis and on advertisers' own agencies in the organization chapter; the figures are now numbered in order of appearance. The reasoning sentence of the sixth prediction names Block and Meta as the first two documented cases; the predictions themselves and their measurement criteria are unchanged. An incomplete sentence in the chapter on dismissal protection, left behind by a cut, has been removed; source 113 is no longer cited. The publisher's address in the method section has been corrected. The source numbers of version 1.2 remain unchanged; new sources are appended.
